<meta>This is the title page of Marin Mersenne's seminal work on music theory and acoustics. Continuity begins here as the opening of the volume.</meta>

# UNIVERSAL HARMONY,

## CONTAINING THE THEORY AND THE PRACTICE OF MUSIC,

->Wherein is treated the Nature of Sounds, and of Movements, of Consonances, of Dissonances, of Genres, of Modes, of Composition, of the Voice, of Songs, and of all kinds of Harmonic Instruments.<-

->By *Friar MARIN MERSENNE* of the Order of Minims. <note>The Minims are a Roman Catholic religious order of friars founded by St. Francis of Paola; Mersenne was a key figure in the 17th-century scientific revolution.</note><-

<image-desc>The printer's mark of Sebastien Cramoisy. The central circular emblem depicts a stork in a nest feeding its young, set against a landscape with mountains and a city in the distance. This circle is bordered by the motto: "HONOR THY FATHER AND THY MOTHER, THAT THY DAYS MAY BE LONG UPON THE LAND. Exodus 20." <note>original Latin: "HONORA PATREM TVVM, ET MATREM TVAM, VT SIS LONGEVVS SVPER TERRAM. Ex. xx."</note> The emblem is supported by two cherubs and flanked by four smaller circular vignettes showing biblical or classical scenes of filial piety. At the bottom, a small shield contains the initials "S. C." topped by a cross.</image-desc>

## IN PARIS,

->At the shop of **SEBASTIEN CRAMOISY**, Ordinary Printer to the King, St. James Street, at the Sign of the Storks.<-

## 1636.

->*With the Privilege of the King, and the Approval of the Doctors.* <note>A "Privilege" was a legal monopoly granted by the King to a printer to protect the work from piracy for a set number of years.</note><-

<summary>The title page of Marin Mersenne's 1636 masterpiece, "Harmonie Universelle," which serves as a comprehensive encyclopedia of 17th-century musical knowledge and acoustics. It identifies the author as a member of the Minim order and the publisher as the prestigious royal printer Sebastien Cramoisy.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as the Table of Contents and introductory guide for the monumental work "Universal Harmony." It establishes the structure of the nineteen books and provides instructions for the reader on how to navigate the prefaces and corrections.</meta>

# TABLE OF PROPOSITIONS
## of the nineteen Books of Universal Harmony.

AFTER having read the first general Preface, in which there are twelve or thirteen very significant matters: that of the six books of Consonances <note>Consonances: the study of intervals and chords that sound pleasing together</note>, where one sees seven things to note; that of the book of the Voice, which contains four or five excellent things for the establishment of the most perfect Idiom <note>referring to the search for a perfect or universal language</note>: that of the Instruments, where one has all the different characters and their names, which are used in Printing houses: and that of the Organ in which many things belonging to the book of the Organ are supplemented: and after having corrected all the faults <note>errata</note> which are marked at the end of the said Prefaces, or at the end of the third book of Movements, of the seventh book of Percussion Instruments, by which I desire that one begins, for the reason that those of the first four books of Consonances are marked there, which are very notable because of the musical notes and the practice, and those which are at the end of the book on the Utility of Harmony, which one can have bound first: after, I say, having done all this, one may read the following Propositions, in order to see all at once what is contained in all the books of this work; although the explanation or the proof of several Propositions often contains much more than they promise upon reading: so that they can compensate for those which give less than what one expects. Be that as it may, the charity and benevolence of the Readers will excuse the defects which will be encountered in whatever place it may be in this work: it is only necessary to note that I sometimes change some words in these Propositions, in order to make them more conformable to my meaning; added to which the numbering which is sometimes missing from the Propositions in the books is here restored in its entirety. Now this table of Propositions will supplement what one might desire in the Table of Contents, and will show the relationship that some Propositions maintain with one another, when one sees the citation afterward; as appears in the 21st Proposition of the first book which follows, after which there is, *See the 9th Proposition of Utility*, etc. because they both speak of the same thing.

### *34 Propositions of the first book of the Nature of Sounds.*

See first the general Preface, and the particular one.
*Besides the Propositions there are several Corollaries <note>Corollary: a proposition that follows naturally from one already proved</note> which contain many remarks.*
I. To determine if Sound is made before it is received by the hearing, and if it is different from the movement of the air. Page one.
II. To determine how movement and Sound are made; and why several movements, although very fast, make no Sound that we can hear, as do those of several wheels both in water and in air: and never-

...unless several very slow movements produce great sounds. 3.
III. To determine whether Sound is the movement of the external air, or the internal air which is within the bodies that produce the sound: and if sound can be made without the movement of one or the other. 6.
IV. To determine if sound can be made in a universal or particular vacuum. 8. <note>A "particular vacuum" refers to a localized space devoid of air, a major topic of debate in 17th-century physics.</note>
V. To explain how the air moves when its movement produces sound, and which movements produce no sound at all. 9.
VI. Sounds have the same ratio among themselves as the movements of the air by which they are produced. 11.
VII. To explain how low and high-pitched sounds are made, and what makes them loud or weak. 12.
*See the 16th Proposition of the book on the Voice, where I speak more amply of this.*
VIII. Sound is not communicated in an instant, as light does, throughout its entire extent, but in a space of time. 14. *Note that it is necessary to correct everything said about the speed of sound in this Proposition, following what is in the 9th Proposition of the Usefulness of Harmony.* <note>The author frequently updated his data on the speed of sound based on new experiments.</note>
IX. Sound does not depend as much on the bodies by which it is produced as light depends on the illuminating body. 16.
X. To explain in what way sound is more subtle than light, and if it reflects. 18.
XI. Sound represents the size and other qualities of the bodies by which it is produced. 19.
XII. To determine in what proportion sounds diminish from the place where they are produced until they cease entirely. 20.
XIII. To determine if sound is faster than the movement of the bodies by which it is produced. 22.
XIV. To determine if sound passes through diaphanous <term>diaphanous: transparent or translucent</term> and opaque bodies, and how it is aided or hindered by all kinds of bodies. 24.
XV. The sphere of the extent of sound is greater the louder it is, although two or more sounds are not heard twice or several times as far as one of them. 25.
XVI. To determine if sounds have all kinds of dimensions, namely length, width, and depth, and what the other properties or circumstances of sound are. 28.
XVII. To determine why one hears better by night than by day; and if one can know how much more rare <note>meaning less dense</note> hot air is than cold, and by how much it is rarer than water. 30.
XVIII. To determine why one hears sounds from outside a room better when one is inside, than those from inside when one is outside. 33.
XIX. To determine if sound is heard better from bottom to top than from top to bottom. 33.
XX. Sounds hinder one another when they meet. 34.
XXI. Sounds can serve to measure the earth, and to make known news of what is happening throughout the world in a short time. 36. *See the ninth Proposition of the Usefulness of Harmony.*
XXII. One can use the sounds of each musical instrument, and the different movements given to them, to discourse on all kinds of subjects and to teach the sciences. 39.
XXIII. The force of sounds is multiplied by Rhythmic movements, and by the quality of the bodies and the strikes by which they are produced. 41.

XXIV. That one can represent the squaring of the circle, the doubling of the cube, and all things in the world subject to quantity, by the same means of sounds. 42.
XXV. In what way sound is different from light, and in what way it is similar to it. 44.

XXVII. What are the distances and lengths of the vocal line of the Echo: whether one can know the place from which it responds, and of what length the said line must be, to make an Echo of as many syllables as one wishes. 56. *See the 22nd Proposition of the third book.*
XXVIII. To explain all the figures proper for making artificial Echoes, Conic sections, and their principal properties. 59. *This is done in the following Propositions, from the 23rd to the 32nd Proposition of the book on the Voice, and in the fifth Proposition of the book on the utility of harmony; which must be joined to this one.*
XXIX. To determine if sounds break, that is to say if they undergo refraction, like light, when they pass through different mediums. 63.
XXX. By how much the sound of the same instrument is deeper <note>original: "graue"</note> in water than in air: and if one can infer from this how much rarer air is than water. 67. *See also the first Proposition of the book on utility.*
XXXI. Whether a high-pitched <note>original: "aigu"</note> sound is more pleasing and more excellent than a deep one. 71. *See also the third Proposition of the 4th book on Composition.*
XXXII. To determine if there is any motion in nature, and what is necessary to establish it. 74.
XXXIII. To consider the motions of bodies in general, and the space in which they occur. 76.
XXXIV. To demonstrate whether a string stretched by a peg, or by a weight, is equally stretched in all its parts; and if the force that tensions it communicates its impression sooner and more strongly to the parts that are near it than to those that are further away.

I. To explain the proportion of the speed <note>original: "viteſſe"</note> with which stones and other heavy bodies descend toward the center of the earth; and to show that it is in a squared ratio of the times. 85. *On which see the 29th Proposition of the third book, and particularly its second Corollary.*
II. If a weight falling from a given space no longer increased the speed acquired at the last point of that space, it would cover a space double the first in an equal time, if it continued its fall at the same speed acquired at the said last point: from which one infers that a falling stone passes through all possible degrees of slowness. 89.

IV. Bodies descending to the center of the earth cannot describe a semicircle: where one sees the line they would describe, if one assumes the daily motion of the earth. 96.

V. To explain the utilities and the practices that can be deduced from the preceding Propositions, as much for <term>Mechanics</term><note>The study of machines and the laws of motion</note>, as for several other things; and how one can measure all sorts of heights by the fall of weights, and find the fall in a given time, or the time required, when the fall is given. 99.

VI. To determine if the stars have fallen from the same place by a straight motion, which changed into the circular motion they now have, as <term>Galileo</term><note>Galileo Galilei (1564–1642), the Italian astronomer who championed the heliocentric model</note> imagines, and to give the manner of computing their falls, their distances, and their circular motions. 105.

VII. To explain the movements of weights on planes inclined to the horizon, and the proportion of their speed: and to examine if falling bodies pass through all possible degrees of slowness. 108.

VIII. To demonstrate if a body can descend by an inclined plane to the center of the earth; and the manner of describing a line so inclined, that the weight always presses upon it equally at every point. 113.

IX. To explain another easier geometrical way to describe a plane of equal inclination: and to examine the figure of the motion of a globe rolling on a horizontal plane, and if rolling is faster than sliding. 119.

X. The plane being inclined on the horizon by a given angle, to determine the force which can support the given weight on the said plane. 121. *But the entire Treatise on* ***Mechanics*** *added at the end of the following third book, determines much more exactly and amply everything belonging to this subject, and to several mechanical difficulties.*

XI. To determine if the speed of falling bodies increases according to the ratio of the line cut in <term>mean and extreme ratio</term><note>Now commonly known as the Golden Ratio</note>; where one sees several properties of this section, and the manner of cutting this line to infinity. 125. Add here the 18th Proposition of the fourth book of Instruments. 125. *On which see the Notice placed at the end of the fifth book of Composition.*

XII. To examine if falling bodies always increase their speed, or if they decrease it; and if there is some point of equality at which they begin to descend at an equal speed. 128.

XIII. To explain several experiments on the fall of bodies toward the center of the earth by the circular line. 131.

XIV. To explain how much faster a ball, which descends or rises through a quarter-circle, goes, and how much heavier it is in one place than in another, and of what length it must be to make each of its turns, or returns, in a given time. 133.

XV. To give the manner of making clocks and watches within the time of one minute of an hour, which divide the day, the hour, and the minutes into as many equal parts as one wishes, and the utility of these clocks. 135.

...not the ratio of the strings. 177.
XII. To provide the difference between sounds made by metals, woods, and stones. 181.
XIII. To provide the different weights of all the various species of wood used in our observations. 182.
XIV. One can determine the length of strings and the difference of their sounds by the difference of the weights stretching said strings; and the difference of said weights by the difference of the sounds and by the length of the strings. 184.
XV. To determine why a greater weight, or a greater power, is required to bring a string of double length to **Unison** <note>The sounding of the same pitch by two different sources</note> than to bring one of double thickness to it; and whether **Unison** indicates an equal tension in all kinds of strings. 189.
XVI. What the strength of strings and other cylinders parallel to the horizon is; what the ratio of their lengths is to their strengths, and what the difference is in their strengths considered according to the different arrangements that cylinders or **parallelepipeds** <note>A six-sided solid figure where each face is a parallelogram, like a box</note> can receive. 193.
XVII. The **low pitch** <note>original: "graue"</note> of sounds is greater as the bodies from which they come are less brittle, and as their parts are better bound together, provided that no hindrance occurs. *Wherein many things regarding the principles of Chemistry are seen.* 198.
XVIII. The density and rarity of bodies is, it seems, the cause of their sounds being different regarding **low** and **high pitch** <note>original: "graue & à l'aigu"</note>. *Wherein the principles of Chemistry are again discussed, as well as those of the hardness and weight of bodies.* 201.
XIX. To explain the different qualities of bodies that make sound lower or higher, clearer or duller, and weaker or stronger, etc. 204.
XX. To explain several particulars of falling bodies and the speed of their fall. 205.
XXI. To explain the movements of a weight attached to a string <note>likely referring to a pendulum</note>, and their circumstances and uses. 208.
XXII. To determine the exact measurements of the vocal lines of the **Echo**, and the uses that can be deduced from them for Philosophy and Mechanics. 213.
XXIII. To explain several circumstances and properties of movements, both natural and violent, whether oblique or perpendicular; wherein one sees the examination of the thoughts and experiments of **Galileo** <note>The famous Italian physicist and astronomer, whose work on motion was contemporary to this text</note> on this subject. 221.
XXIV. To explain several conclusions drawn from this entire third book. 226.

## *Three Propositions of the Mechanical Treatise.*

I. Given a plane inclined to the horizon, and the angle of inclination being known, to find a power which, pulling or pushing along a line of direction parallel to the inclined plane, supports a given weight upon the same plane. 7. *Note that before this Proposition, one finds five Axioms and a Scholium <note>A marginal note or explanatory commentary</note> which must be understood.*
II. When the line of direction by which a power supports a weight on an inclined plane is not parallel to the same plane, the inclination of the plane and the weight being given, to find the power. 13. *Wherein the four following Scholia must be seen.*

<meta>This page continues the index of propositions from Marin Mersenne’s "Harmonie Universelle," transitioning from the physics of mechanical weights and strings to the biological and philosophical study of the human voice.</meta>

->of Universal Harmony.<-

III. Given a weight supported by two strings, or by two supports, whose position is given, find what power is needed for each string, or for each support. 21. *Where one must also see the nine Scholia <note>original: "Scholies"; explanatory notes or comments on a mathematical or scientific text</note> that follow.*

### The Fifty-Three Propositions of the Book on the Voice.

*The printers have incorrectly placed "of universal harmony" in the titles of the pages until the eleventh page. See the Preface.*

I. The motor power of the soul is the principal and first cause of the Voice of animals, and has its seat in the tendons. 1.

II. Of all the muscles in the body, those of the chest and the larynx contribute most immediately to the Voice. 3.

III. The glottis <note>The opening between the vocal folds in the larynx</note> is the most direct cause of the Voice. 4.

IV. The muscles and the nerves of the larynx serve to form the low and high voice. 6.

V. The voice is the sound made by the animal by means of the vocal artery <note>original: "artere vocale"; in the 17th century, this often referred to the trachea or windpipe</note>, the larynx, the glottis, and other parts that contribute to forming it, with the intention of signifying something. 7.

VI. Men’s voices are as different as their faces, so that one can distinguish them from one another by the voice; and establish **Phthongonomy** <note>original: "Phtongonomie"; a term coined to describe the systematic classification of voices</note>, or **Phoniscopy** <note>original: "Phoniſcopie"; the scientific observation or measurement of vocal sounds</note> for voices, just as **Physiognomy** <note>The practice of assessing a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face</note> for faces. 8.

VII. The voice of animals serves to signify the passions of the soul, but it does not always signify the temperament of the body. 8.

VIII. The voice of animals is necessary, and that of humans is free <note>implying humans have the agency to choose when and how to speak, unlike animals acting on instinct</note>. 10.

IX. The voice is the matter of speech, and only man alone speaks. 10.

X. To determine if man could speak or sing if he had never heard words or sounds. 11.

XI. Suppose that children were raised in a place where they heard no speech: to know what language <note>original: "idiome"</note> they would use to speak among themselves. 11.

XII. To determine if one can find the best language of all those that can express the thoughts of the mind. 12. *See the 47th Proposition of this book.*

XIII. How many kinds of sounds man can make with the mouth and the other organs of the voice and speech. 13.

XIV. If nature had not given the voices that express the passions: to know if one could invent the same ones she uses, or more suitable ones. 14.

XV. One can sing **Chromatic** and **Enharmonic** music <note>Ancient Greek musical genera involving small intervals like semitones and quarter-tones</note>, and produce the major and minor tone, and even the **Comma** <note>a very small musical interval, specifically the difference between two slightly different versions of the same note in different tuning systems</note> in all the places one wishes. 16.

XVI. To explain how the low and high pitch of the voice are made. *Where the questions of Aristotle on this subject are explained.* 17.

XVII. Whether it is easier to lead the voice from a low sound to a high one, than from a high sound to a low one. 22.

XVIII. To know whether it is easier to sing by conjunct degrees <note>stepwise motion between adjacent notes</note> than by separate or disjunct degrees <note>leaps between non-adjacent notes</note>. 27.

XIX. To determine if one can know with certainty which is the low or high pitch of the sound one hears. 27.

Catchword: recher- (likely for "recherchés," meaning researched or investigated).</meta>

# Table of Propositions

XX. One can learn to speak and pronounce well by means of Music. 28.
XXI. Explaining how the voice increases or weakens. 29.
XXII. Determining if a single man can sing two or three different parts at the same time, and if he can go higher or lower by some sort of artifice than he ordinarily does. 31.

XXVII. How Architects must build structures to aid sounds: where one sees that craftsmen do not trace the Ellipse when they describe their Oval. 35.

### *The 27 Propositions of the book on Songs.*

I. The Song, or the Air, is a deduction of sounds by certain natural or artificial degrees and intervals agreeable to the ear; which signifies joy, sadness, or some other passion through its melody and movements. 89.

II. The Song is a sequence of sounds arranged according to the rules prescribed by Musicians, by which one expresses the passions of the soul, or those of the subject. 92.

III. At what moment sound begins to be Song. 93.

IV. Explaining the types of Airs, or Songs used by Musicians; and giving examples of Church Songs. 94.

V. To know if one can prescribe infallible rules according to which one makes good Songs on all sorts of subjects; and if Musicians have them when they compose Airs. 97. *See the sixth book on Composition which serves this purpose.*

VI. What rules must be used to make good Songs: and in what way sounds and Songs are similar to colors. 98.

VII. If it is possible to compose the best Song of all those that can be...

### *40 Propositions of the Book of Consonances.*

The Preface contains seven or eight very significant things that must be read: and *most of the Corollaries that follow the Propositions contain several excellent moral lessons.*

I. To determine if there are Consonances and Dissonances <note>Consonances are intervals that sound harmonious or stable; dissonances sound clashing or "unresolved."</note> in Music, and what they are. 1.

II. To determine the difference between Sound and Unison; and what the origin of Unison is. 5.

III. To explain in what manner Sound takes its origin from Unison. 7.

IV. To determine if Unison is a Consonance; and if it is sweeter and more agreeable than the Octave. 10. *Where one sees several beautiful moral lessons for Preachers and devout persons.*

V. Unison is the conjunction or union of two or several sounds, which resemble each other so perfectly that the ear receives them as a single sound; and it is the most powerful of all Consonances. 23. *See the moral lessons for raising the spirit to God.*

VI. To explain the reason and cause of the vibration <note>original: "tremblement"; referring here to sympathetic vibration, where one string moves in response to another.</note> of strings that are in Unison. 26. *See excellent elevations of the spirit to devotion.*

VII. To know if the ratio of inequality comes from that of equality, and the Consonances...

Why strings that are an Octave apart cause each other to tremble and sound; how much more strongly those of the Unison tremble than those of the Octave; how much more strongly those that are touched <note>Plucked or played.</note> tremble than those that are not; and how much sweeter the Unison is than the Octave. 52.

XVI. The first and easiest division of the Octave produces the Fifth, the Fourth, the Twelfth, and the Fifteenth. 60.

XVII. The Fifth, whose ratio is three to two, is the third of the Consonances; but when doubled or multiplied, it becomes a Dissonance. 60.

XVIII. All the repeats or repetitions of the Fifth are agreeable, of which the first is three to one, and the second is six to one, and so on with the others, in which the smallest term always remains. It is also determined by how much the Fifth is less sweet than the Octave. 61.

XXII. The *Diatessaron* <note>original: "Diatessaron"; the ancient Greek term for the interval of a Fourth.</note> is the fourth Consonance, whose sounds have a ratio of four to three. 67.

XXIII. The Fourth comes from the Octave or from the second bisection <note>A division into two equal parts.</note> of a string, and its ratio can just as well be called sub-sesquitertial as sesquitertial <note>original: "souz-sesquitierce" and "sesquitierce"; technical Latin-derived terms for the 4:3 ratio.</note>. 68.

XXIV. One finds the *Diatessaron* <note>The Fourth.</note> on the same string divided into seven equal parts, by placing the bridge at the fourth part. 69.

XXV. To determine if the Fourth should be placed among the number of Consonances. 70.

XXVII. The Fourth is so sterile that it can produce nothing good, neither by its multiplication nor by its division. 74.

How many commas the minor and major tone contain, and in what sense one can say that the minor is larger than nine commas. 123. <note>A "comma" is a very small musical interval, used here to measure the precise difference between notes that might otherwise sound the same to the untrained ear.</note>

## *20 Propositions from the book of Harmonic Genera, Systems, and Modes.*

I. To explain what the Diatonic genus consists of, its types, and the one used now: what the scale of Guy Aretin consists of, and what the Tetrachords of the Greeks are. 141. <note>Guy Aretin is the French name for Guido of Arezzo, the 11th-century monk who developed the system of solmization (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la).</note>
II. To know whether the Diatonic degrees are more natural and easier to sing than those of the Chromatic and Enharmonic. 147.
III. The ratios of the Diatonic degrees can be explained by the length of the strings, and by the number of their beats. One sees where the minor tone and the major tone must be placed. 150. <note>term: Beats (Here, Mersenne refers to the frequency of vibrations or "beats of air").</note>
    IV. To explain the Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic Genera so clearly that all Musicians can easily understand them and use them in their Compositions. 153.
V. To explain the use of the Octave, which contains the three aforementioned Genera. 155.
VI. To explain the same System or Diapason by beginning it with *C-sol-ut*. 157. <note>term: Diapaſon (The Greek term for an Octave). C-sol-ut is the note middle C.</note>
VII. One can begin each note of Music on each Diatonic degree of the two preceding Systems, in order to transpose all sorts of tones on the keyboard of the Organ arranged according to the Diapason. 161.

Explaining the force and properties of each tone, and of the modes, and the manner of knowing from which mode or tone a <term>given melody</term> <note>original: "Chant-donné" — a pre-existing melody, like a Gregorian chant, used as a basis for a musical composition.</note> is; and showing that there are only 7 different modes or tones. 187.
XIX. Determining if one can reduce the tones and modes to <term>B-natural</term> <note>original: "b quarre"</note> and <term>B-flat</term> <note>original: "b mol"</note>; and showing how to sing without any <term>mutation</term> <note>original: "muance" — the complex system of shifting between hexachords (six-note patterns) used by Renaissance singers.</note> other than those of these two keys. 190. *See the first two propositions of the 6th Book of Composition, where one is taught to sing without mutations.*
XX. Determining if the 7 species of Octaves and the 12 modes are found in the Chromatic and Enharmonic genera. 194.

### 28 Propositions of the 4th Book of Composition.

I. Determining if simple solo recitals, performed by a single voice, are more agreeable than when the same thing is sung in 2 or more parts. 197.
II. Determining if a song in three parts is more agreeable than in two. 201.
III. Determining if the Bass is the foundation and the principal part of Music, and for what reasons. 207.
IV. Corollary. *On the Music of the Platonists.*
V. All the ways of passing from one <term>consonance</term> <note>A combination of notes that sounds pleasant or stable.</note> to another can be related to the principal movements used in composition, namely to conjunct movement, made by conjunct, disjunct, similar, and contrary degrees. 216.

<meta>This page continues the "Table of Propositions" from Mersenne's *Universal Harmony*. Having previously discussed the mathematical division of scales and microtonal systems, the text now transitions into the practical rules of counterpoint (how parts move from one interval to another) and the notation systems of different cultures.</meta>

...a part can move by such degrees as one wishes, even though they are dissonant, provided that one does not stop on these dissonant degrees, and that one only makes them serve as a bridge to pass to Consonances. But if one of the parts ceases its sound—even if it remains at the Unison—upon resuming the same sound, the other part cannot move through all sorts of degrees. 218.

# Table of Propositions

VII. To determine in general why all the <term>passages</term> <note>Transitions or movements from one musical interval to the next.</note> that can be made from one Consonance to another are not good; and why some are more agreeable than others. 219.
VIII. How one must find all the relations, both external and internal, that occur in the passages from one consonance to another, in order to seek the reason why one is good and the other bad. 219.
IX. To explain two other ways that serve to find the internal relations of passages from one Consonance to another. 231.
X. To explain in how many ways one can pass from one consonance to another of a different kind by contrary, <term>conjunct</term> <note>Stepwise movement between adjacent notes in a scale.</note>, or <term>disjunct</term> <note>Movement that skips over one or more scale degrees.</note> movements: where one sees the used and unused passages, the good and the bad. 232.
XI. To determine why the last two passages of the first table, and the first of the second and third tables, are good or bad: where one sees why the passage from the Major Third to the Unison is not as good as that from the Unison to the Major Third. 238.
XII. To determine if the third passage of the first table is good; which is used to pass from the Major Third to the Unison by the <term>Chromatic</term> <note>A movement using semitones that fall outside the standard diatonic scale.</note> degree, and by the Minor Third: and why one can pass to whatever Consonance one wishes upon leaving the Unison. 240.
XIII. To determine if the 4th, 5th, and 6th passages of the first table, by which one goes from the Fourth to the Unison, are permitted. 241.
XIV. To determine if it is permitted to pass from the Fifth to the Unison by the 7th and 8th ways of the first table. 241.
XV. Of two ways for the Minor Third to move to the Unison by similar disjunct movements—one where the Bass descends by a Fifth and the upper part moves a Major Third, and the other where the Bass ascends by a Major Third and the upper part moves a Fifth—to determine which is better. 243.
XVI. Why several passages from one Consonance to another are not good, even though they have no bad internal relations: and why it is not permitted to pass from the Major Third to the Unison, as it is permitted to pass from the Unison to the Major Third. 244.
XVII. To explain the universal <term>tablature</term> <note>A form of musical notation indicating fingering or instrument positions rather than just pitch.</note> of Harmonic ratios, from which one can compose all sorts of musical pieces for 2, 3, 4, and as many parts as one wishes. 245.
XVIII. To explain two other kinds of tablature that can serve to understand Theory while singing. 250.
XIX. To explain all the types of characters suitable for singing Music, and to show how the Jews, Arabs, Armenians, Samaritans, and other nations can conform to our way of writing and singing Music. 251.
XX. To explain the figures and the value of notes and other harmonic characters of Europe. 255.

## 34 Propositions from the 6th book of the Art of Singing Well.

I. To explain an easy method for learning and teaching how to read and write Music. 332. This is the invention of Mr. <term>des Argues</term> <note>Girard Desargues (1591–1661), a French mathematician and engineer who collaborated with Mersenne and is famous for his work in projective geometry.</note>.

II. To explain another method for learning to sing and to compose without ordinary notes, by means of the letters of the Alphabet alone, without <term>nuances</term> <note>Likely referring to the variations in tone, staff-based notation, or perhaps the "solmization" syllables like ut, re, mi.</note>. 342.

III. To explain all the characters necessary to easily write and compose all kinds of Music, whether for voices or for all types of instruments. 347. *Wherein one sees two compositions by <term>du Caurroy</term> <note>Eustache du Caurroy (1549–1609), an influential French composer of the late Renaissance.</note> for 7 and 8 parts, note-against-note; and the Perfect Harmonic Hand of the Gamut <note>The "Guidonian Hand," a medieval and Renaissance mnemonic device used to help singers find the pitches of the scale (the gamut) on the joints of their hand.</note>.*

IV. To learn to compose music correctly in a short amount of time. 351.

V. To explain the way to know if a voice is good, and the qualities it ought to have. 353.

VI. To explain the method used to train voices for the <term>cadence</term> <note>In the context of singing, this often refers to ornaments like trills or turns used to signal the end of a musical phrase.</note>, and to make them capable of singing all kinds of Airs. 354. *Wherein one sees a notice for Masters who teach singing.*

VII. To explain the characters necessary to signify all the particulars of the Airs that one wishes to perform with all kinds of perfection, and the manner of properly performing cadences and <term>tremblemens</term> <note>French for "shakings"; a general term for trills or vocal vibrato used as ornamentation.</note>. 358.

VIII. To explain the method of creating good songs on all kinds of subjects and <term>letters</term> <note>This likely refers to Mersenne’s interest in "musical language," where melodies could be derived from the letters of a text or name.</note>. 360.

IX. To reveal the <term>industries</term> <note>In the 17th century, "industry" meant skill, ingenuity, or specialized techniques.</note> used to compose good songs. 362. *Wherein one sees a particular notice on this subject.*

X. Accents are so numerous that it is nearly impossible to explain them all. 365.

XI. Accents reveal the country one is from, and sometimes one's temperament and mood. 366.

XII. The accent is a modification of the voice, by which one expresses the passions of the soul naturally or through <term>artifice</term> <note>Refers to the skilled or artistic application of technique rather than "deception."</note>. 366.

XIII. Each affection of the soul has its own accents, by which it expresses its different degrees. 367.

XIV. One cannot express the Accents of the passions without new characters <note>Mersenne is arguing for a specialized notation system to capture the subtle emotional inflection of a voice.</note>. 369.

XV. All the Accents of the 3 passions need nine different characters to be marked, namely 3 for the 3 degrees of anger, and two other sets of three for love and sadness. 370.

XVI. To determine if these Accents can be expressed and performed while singing Music. 371.

XVII. To show the utility that Preachers and other Orators can draw from the Accents of each passion. 373.

XVIII. <term>Rhythmics</term> <note>The study of musical and poetic rhythm.</note> establishes and regulates movements, their sequence, and their mixture to excite, increase, maintain, diminish, and appease the passions. 374. *Wherein one sees 27 examples of movements or <term>metrical feet</term> <note>Rhythmic units borrowed from classical poetry, such as the iamb, trochee, or dactyl, which Mersenne applies to musical composition.</note>.*

## The 20 Propositions of the 1st Book on Instruments.

->*It should be noted that the printers incorrectly placed the title of "Universal Harmony" on the second through fifth pages.*<-

**I.** Determine how many species of sounds and musical instruments there are. 1.
**II.** Explain the material and the manner in which the strings of instruments are made. 3.
**III.** Determine if harmonic instruments were made in imitation of voices, or if the intervals of voices were regulated by those of instruments; and whether Art can perfect nature, or the contrary; and if one must judge artificial things by natural ones. 7.
**IV.** Which is the most pleasant sound of all the instruments, and which instrument one should use to regulate <term>Harmonic intervals</term> <note>The mathematical distances between musical pitches that create harmony.</note>. 9. *Wherein one sees the Monochord of Ptolemy.* <note>The monochord is an ancient scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used to measure musical intervals. Claudius Ptolemy was a famous 2nd-century scholar who refined its use.</note>
**V.** Demonstrate all the divisions of the <term>Monochord</term>, and consequently the entire science of music. 16.
**VI.** Demonstrate that the Monochord divided into 8 equal parts contains all the <term>Consonances</term> <note>Musical intervals that sound stable and pleasant to the ear, such as the octave or fifth.</note>. 19.
**VII.** Explain the simplest division of a string to make it produce Consonances and the <term>Diatonic degrees</term> <note>The standard steps of a musical scale.</note>. 20.
**VIII.** Explain the intervals, both Consonances and Dissonances, which are found in the residues of the Monochord string after the Diatonic degrees have been marked upon it. 21.
**IX.** Explain all the Consonances and Dissonances of the Monochord and the <term>Perfect System</term> <note>The "Systema Perfectum" was the full range of notes used in ancient Greek music theory.</note>, whether one compares the whole string to the parts that make the Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic degrees, or compares each degree or sound with the entire string or with its remainder. In such a way that the Monochord and the Harmonic System are here considered in every way that can serve Harmony. 22.
**X.** Divide all sorts of strings, or straight lines, into as many equal parts as one wishes, without changing the opening of the compass <unclear>by chance</unclear>. 25. *See also the 17th proposition of the 4th book which follows.*
**XI.** Determine the number of <term>Aspects</term> <note>In astrology, "aspects" are the angular distances between planets; Mersenne compares these celestial relationships to musical intervals.</note> by which the stars look upon the earth, and the Consonances to which they correspond. 27.
**XII.** Explain the figure of a particular Monochord and all its divisions. 32.
**XIII.** Explain the difference and the distance from one Consonance or Dissonance to another by means of the Monochord; and the manner of dividing the same string half by half to make all sorts of Consonances and Dissonances. 35.
**XIV.** Explain another Monochord of equality, to divide the neck of the Lute, the Viol, the <term>Cittern</term> <note>A wire-strung instrument popular in the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>, and all other instruments played in 12 equal <term>semitones</term> <note>This is an early description of "Equal Temperament," the tuning system used on modern pianos.</note>, and to create the <term>Diapason</term> <note>The standard pitch or range of an instrument.</note> and the tuning of Spinnets and organs. 37. *See the 6th and 7th propositions of the 2nd book, and the 9th of the 4th book following.*
**XV.** Determine how much the intervals of this Monochord of equality are smaller or larger than those of the Monochord which follows just proportions: and if the ear can perceive the differences. 39.
**XVI.** What is the force of all sorts of strings, of whatever length...

->The 17 Propositions of the 2nd book on Instruments.<-
<note>While the heading mentions 17 propositions, the following list covers the first ten; the remainder likely follow on the next page.</note>

**I.** Explain the shape, the parts, the tuning, and the <term>temperament</term> <note>The system of adjusting the intervals of an instrument to allow playing in different keys.</note> of the <term>Lute</term> <note>A plucked string instrument with a rounded body, extremely popular during the Renaissance and Baroque periods.</note>. 45. *Where one sees two ancient instruments.*

**II.** Explain the construction of the Lute and the <term>Pandore</term> <note>A wire-strung instrument similar to a lute but with a scalloped body and a flatter back.</note>: how they must be strung perfectly, and how one can know if the strings are good. 49. *Where one sees the different kinds of knots.*

**III.** Explain how the neck of the Lute must be divided, and the <term>frets</term> <note>original: "touches"; these are the gut or metal strips tied around the neck to mark where the fingers should press.</note> placed to play it perfectly: where one sees several remarks on strings, and the difference in their sounds. 53.

**IV.** Explain the Genres and the Species of Music, and all the principal things that the Greeks established in their music. 56.

**V.** That the system of <term>Aristoxenus</term> <note>An ancient Greek philosopher who argued that musical intervals should be judged by the ear rather than just mathematical ratios.</note> is used on the Lute and other instruments, and what is defective and advantageous about it. 58.

**VI.** Explain the temperament of the Lute, the <term>Viol</term> <note>A bowed string instrument, ancestor of the modern cello family, held between the legs.</note>, etc., and show by how much each <term>Consonance or Dissonance</term> <note>Consonances are intervals that sound stable; dissonances are those that sound tense or clashing.</note> is altered. Where one will see the three genres of music in their perfection. 62.

**VII.** That the major and minor tone, the Octave, etc., can be divided into two or more equal parts; and consequently that the <term>Diapason</term> <note>original: "Diapason"; a Greek term used here to mean the interval of an octave.</note> can be divided into 12 equal semitones. Where one finds the two mean proportionals, the doubling of the cube, and the frets of each instrument in their proper place. 65. *See page 226.*

**VIII.** Determine if the Diatonic scale in use is the "Synton" of <term>Ptolemy</term>, or that of <term>Pythagoras</term>, <term>Archytas</term>, or Aristoxenus, etc. Where one sees the different species of the three genres. 70. <note>These men represent the great competing schools of ancient Greek music theory, differing on how to mathematically define the notes of the scale.</note>

**IX.** Explain the manner of playing the Lute perfectly, and how to place each hand or finger as is necessary to play well. 76. *Where one sees the conditions required to learn how to play, the position of the right hand, that of the left hand; the ornaments (vibratos and trills), plaintive accents, sighs both simple and compound, and the fingerings of the left hand.*

**X.** Explain the characters of <term>tablature</term> <note>A system of notation that uses letters or numbers to tell the player which fret to press, rather than using a staff with musical notes.</note>, and several specific observations. 82. *Where one sees 16 remarks for playing the Lute and using its neck perfectly.*

## 25. Propositions of the 6th Book on Organs.

**I.** Explain the shape and the parts of **Organ Cabinets** <note>Small, portable organs or the decorative wooden cases that house the organ mechanism.</note>. 309.
**II.** Explain the construction of the Organ and all its parts. 312. *See the 44th proposition.*
**III.** Determine the number of **Stops** <note>Original: *Jeux*. The different sets of pipes in an organ that produce distinct sounds.</note> of the Organ, both simple and compound. 316. *See the 31st proposition.*
**IV.** Explain the proportion of the length and width of Organ pipes, and the practice of **Builders** <note>Original: *Facteurs*. Professional organ or instrument makers.</note>. 318.
**V.** What the length and the height of the mouth of the pipes must be: and the width and thickness of the **Languids** <note>Original: *Languettes*. In flue pipes, the flat metal plate that directs air through the mouth.</note>. 319.
**VI.** Explain the manner of casting, forging, and flattening lead and tin to make the pipes, and of soldering them, and of composing the solder. 321. *See the 17th proposition.*
**VII.** Explain what is unique to **Stopped Pipes** and **Chimney Pipes** <note>Stopped pipes are closed at the top, sounding an octave lower; chimney pipes have a small narrow tube at the top to modify the tone.</note>. 322.
**VIII.** Explain the material, the proportion, and the manufacture of **Reed Pipes** <note>Pipes that produce sound via a vibrating metal tongue rather than an air column hitting a lip.</note>. 323.
**IX.** How one must cut and construct the **Shallots** <note>Original: *Echalottes*. The metal tube in a reed pipe against which the reed vibrates.</note> of the reeds. 326.
**X.** Explain the **Pitch** <note>Original: *Diapason*. Here referring to the standard of measurement and pitch for the pipes.</note> and the construction of the **Vox Humana** <note>An organ stop designed to mimic the sound of the human voice.</note>. 327.
**XI.** In how many ways one can raise or lower the pitch of the pipes and the reeds without changing their lengths and their widths; and which **Tuning Tools** <note>Original: *Accordoirs*. Specifically tuning cones or knives used to adjust pipe mouths or reed lengths.</note> the builders use. 329.
**XII.** Determine if one can make an Organ where all the pipes are of the same height, and in what ratio their widths must be to produce whatever sounds one might wish. 331.

**XIII.** In what ratio pipes of the same thickness <note>Original: *groſſeur*. In organ building, this refers to the diameter or scale of the pipe relative to its length.</note> must be to produce the required intervals: and if one can make an Organ where all the pipes are of the same thickness. 333.

**XIV.** What the ratio of the width of the pipes to their length must be, to produce all the steps of one or several Octaves; and to provide a very accurate **Tuning Scale** <term>Diapason: In this context, the word refers to the "scale" or the ratio of dimensions used by organ builders to determine the size of pipes across the keyboard.</term>. 334.

**XV.** To explain all the types of Tuning Scales, and the Canons <note>Original: *Canons*. In this sense, a set of mathematical rules or standards.</note> or Harmonic rules, which can be used to perfect Organs. 338.

**XVI.** To explain the easiest and most perfect Tuning Scale for Organs that one can imagine, when using temperament <note>The practice of slightly adjusting the pure mathematical intervals of a scale so that music can be played in multiple keys without sounding dissonant.</note>, and when one only wants 13 or 20 **keys** <note>Original: *marches*. Literally "steps," a term used for the physical keys of the keyboard or pedalboard.</note> in the Octave; and the manner <note>A small ink stain on the original page partially obscures this word.</note> of perfectly tuning Organs. 341.

**XVII.** To explain the different solders which can be used to make pipes from all types of metals. 344.

**XVIII.** To explain if pipes of different metals are in Unison when they are equal in size, and if their different shapes cause them to change sound. 346.

**XIX.** To explain the different intervals that the pipes make by means of the different wind [pressure] given to them. 346.

**XX.** To explain the particular properties of each **stop** <note>Original: *jeu*. A set of pipes of a specific timbre, such as a Flute or Trumpet stop.</note> of the Organ; and why one does not perceive the dissonances of the Organ. 347.

**XXI.** Whether new stops can be added to the Organ. 348.

**XXII.** To explain the science of the Organ Keyboard, and how many keys it must have to encompass the three Genres of Music <note>A reference to the Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic systems of ancient Greek music theory.</note>. 349.

**XXIII.** Whether it is expedient to change the ordinary Keyboards, and in what the use of the perfect Keyboard consists: where one sees the explanation of the Keyboard of 27 and 32 keys. 353.

**XXIV.** To explain the way in which sound is produced in organ pipes. 358.

**XXV.** Why the organ stops go out of tune, and which stops are most subject to going out of tune. 359.

**XXVI.** Whether more wind is needed to make the large pipes speak than the smaller ones, and in what manner the **builders** <note>Original: *facteurs*. Specifically "organ builders."</note> measure it. 360.

**XXVII.** Why large pipes produce lower <note>Original: *graues* (graves).</note> sounds than the smaller ones. 361.

**XXVIII.** Why two or more pipes "tremble" [beat] when speaking together when they are out of tune, and how the **drum stop** <note>Original: *jeu du tambour*. A special effect where two large pipes are tuned slightly apart to create a low-frequency pulsing sound like a drum roll.</note> is made. 362.

**XXIX.** To explain the manner of tuning Organs, both in pure intonation and in temperament. 363. *See page 383.*

**XXX.** Whether one can compensate for the accuracy and goodness of the ear for tuning the Organ, without using the sense of hearing. 366.

**XXXI.** To explain 22 simple stops and 24 compound stops of the Organ, with the 12 simple and 12 compound stops of its **Positive** <note>Original: *Poſitif*. A smaller, secondary organ manual or a separate small instrument.</note>. 371.

**XXXII.** That a Cabinet Organ, having only 8 simple stops, can have 247 compound and entirely different ones. 376. *See the 4 Corollaries which contain many noteworthy things for the properties of the stops and **bird-calls**.* <note>Original: *apeaux*. Novelty stops designed to imitate the singing of birds.</note>

**XXXIII.** To explain the different force of the weights pressing the bellows, according to the different inclinations of their covers. 376. *See the first 2 propositions of the Mechanical treatise.*

**XXXIV.** To explain the construction, the size, the parts, the weights, and all the other properties of the bellows. 377.

**XXXV.** To explain how organ stops must be constructed to pronounce vowels, consonants, and speech <note>Original: *dictions*. This refers to Mersenne’s interest in "speaking machines" or organ pipes that could mimic human phonemes.</note>. 380.

**XXXVII.** To explain the manner of inspecting organs, and how to recognize and repair the faults of the builders <note>Original: *facteurs*. The term specifically refers to organ or instrument builders.</note>. 382.

**XXXVIII.** To explain a universal method for the **Tuning Scale** <term>Diapason: Here referring to the proportional measurements used to determine the size and pitch of various instruments.</term> of instruments, for the division of the **monochord** <note>A one-stringed scientific instrument used to demonstrate mathematical relationships between pitches.</note>, and the necks of instruments; wherein one sees a new Theory of Music. 384.

**XXXIX.** To know if the ancients had organs, and to note what is missing from this treatise. 387.

**XL.** To explain organ **tablature** <term>Tablature: A system of musical notation using letters, numbers, or symbols rather than notes on a staff.</term>, with music composed by the King, and the qualities of an excellent organist. 390.

**XLI.** To explain the greatest **diminutions** <term>Diminutions: An ornamental musical technique where a long note is broken down into many faster, shorter notes.</term> that can be performed on the harpsichord and on the organ. 393.

**XLII.** Why a stopped pipe <note>An organ pipe that is closed at the top, which changes its harmonic profile.</note> produces two sounds at the same time, which together form the interval of a twelfth. 395.

**XLIII.** To explain the thickness and width of pipes, and their mouths, following the practice of those who build great organs. 398.

**XLIV.** To explain the construction and the parts of a great organ and of a small cabinet organ; wherein one will see distinctly and clearly what was more confusedly and obscurely described in the 2nd proposition. 399.

**XLV.** To find two mean proportionals between two given unequal straight lines, in order to divide the **Tuning Scale** of organs into 12 equal semitones. 408. *See the two Advertisements.* <note>This proposition deals with the geometric mathematics required to achieve equal temperament, allowing an organ to be played in any key.</note>

->*21 Propositions of the 7th book on percussion instruments.*<-

**I.** To determine the number of percussion instruments, and which is the most excellent. 1.

**IX.** To determine if founders should use the minor or major tone for the tuning of two bells. 15.

### 18 Propositions of the 8th book on the utility of Harmony.

I. That there is almost no science or profession for which the preceding books on Harmony cannot be useful. 1.

II. To show the utility of Harmony for Preachers & other Orators. 4. *Wherein one sees six Advertisements <note>In the 17th century, "advertisements" referred to instructions, warnings, or notices intended to guide the reader.</note> for Preachers.*

III. To show the use of Mathematics in favor of Preachers, & the means of drawing motives for humility from them.

IV. In what way Harmony & other parts of Mathematics can serve the spiritual life. 20. *Wherein one sees 4 notable Advertisements.*

To explain the shapes & properties of <term>Conic Sections</term> <note>The curves—circles, ellipses, parabolas, and hyperbolas—obtained by intersecting a cone with a plane.</note>, both for mirrors and for telescopes <note>Original: *lunettes de longue veuë*. While literally "long-view glasses," this was the standard term for the refracting telescope.</note>, & echoes. 28. *Wherein one sees the manner of measuring the roundness & the radius of the earth by a single observation: & 5 very remarkable Corollaries.*

X. To explain the types of ratios <note>Original: *raisons*. In 17th-century mathematical French, this term specifically refers to the relationship between two quantities (a ratio).</note>, & the terms in which they must be expressed. 51.

XI. To explain incommensurable or irrational quantities & ratios. 53.

XII. A given ratio is continued by making the consequent have the same ratio to another term as the antecedent has to said consequent. 55.

XIII. The addition of ratios is done by multiplying the antecedent of one by that of the other, & the consequent by the consequent, since the products contain a ratio composed of the two added together. 56.

XIV. One subtracts a lesser ratio from a greater one by multiplying the antecedent of one by the consequent of the other, & the consequent by the antecedent. 56.

XV. One multiplies a given ratio by taking the powers of the antecedent & of the consequent to the degree determined by the multiplier. 57.

XVI. One divides a given ratio by taking the roots <note>Original: *costez*. Literally "sides," a term used in early modern mathematics to refer to the root of a square (side of a square) or cube.</note> of the antecedent & of the consequent to the degree determined by the divisor. 58.

XVII. To explain the preceding operations on ratios in another manner by means of lines. 59.

XVIII. Whether heavy bodies become lighter as they are closer to the center of the earth, & to investigate the reason for it. 61. *Wherein one finally sees the printing errors, & remarks on the different ranges of cannons.*

## Compendium of Speculative Music.

**Article I.** Sound is nothing other than a beating of air, which the sense of hearing apprehends when it is touched by it. Now, the two principal properties of sound consist in its strength <note>Volume or intensity.</note> and in the qualities we call *grave and acute* <note>Low and high pitch.</note>. Its strength is greater when it is made by a more violent beating of air: and this beating is more violent when a larger quantity of air is struck at the same time.
As for its gravity <note>Low pitch.</note>, it is greater when it is made by slower beatings; and consequently, it is more acute <note>High pitch.</note> when it is made by faster beatings; for example, if one sound is made in a given time by 50 beatings, and another sound in an equal time by 100 beatings, this latter sound will be twice as acute as the first.

<summary>This page contains a list of printing corrections for various books in Mersenne's work and introduces a "Compendium of Speculative Music" that defines the physical nature of sound and pitch.</summary>
<keywords>errata, speculative music, acoustics, pitch, vibrations, sound frequency, grave and acute, Marin Mersenne</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Compendium of Speculative Music," shifting from the introductory "Advertisements" to the core principles of harmonic theory, specifically the mathematical ratios of intervals and the physical properties of sound waves and speed.</meta>

II. When two or more sounds are produced together and at the same time, they are called **Consonances** <note>Original: *Consonans*. In early music theory, this refers to intervals that sound stable and pleasant.</note>, when they agree well and please the ear and the mind. Now, the reason for these harmonies is taken from the union of the said sounds, such that they create harmonies that are all the sweeter as their union is closer and greater, as one experiences with the **Unison**, the **Octave**, the **Fifth** <note>Original: *Diapente* (Greek)</note>, etc.

The **Unison** is the union or the mixture of two sounds made by an equal number of air beats <note>Mersenne uses the term *batemens* (beats) to describe what we now call frequency or vibrations per second.</note>; the **Octave** is the mixture of two sounds, of which the lower <note>Original: *grave*</note> is made by one beat, and the higher <note>Original: *aigu*</note> by two; and the **Fifth** is the mixture of two sounds, of which the lower is made by two beats, and the higher by three.

All simple Consonances are comprised and explained by the first six numbers: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6. For the Octave is from 1 to 2; the Fifth from 2 to 3; the Fourth or the **Diatessaron** <note>Original: *Diatessaron* (Greek)</note> from 3 to 4; the **Diton** or Major Third from 4 to 5; and the Minor Third from 5 to 6. Now, these represent the number and the comparison of their beats.

III. The Octave is the sweetest of all after the Unison, because its beats unite most often together: for the first beat of the high sound unites with the first part of the beat of the low sound, and the second beat with the last part; or rather, its beats unite every 2 strokes; those of the Fifth every 3 strokes, etc.

And when the union is equal on the part of the high sound, and unequal on the part of the low, the Consonance which unites its sounds equally on the part of both is sweeter: for example, the beats of the Fifth unite every 3 strokes in regard to the high sound, and every 2 in regard to the low. But the **Twelfth** <note>Original: *Douziesme*</note> unites its sounds at every stroke in regard to the low sound: that is why it is sweeter.

IV. Since weight cannot make a string rise to the Octave unless it is fourfold <note>This refers to the law of strings: to double the frequency (an octave), you must quadruple the tension/weight.</note>, one can say that the high sound of the Octave is 4 times heavier than the low sound. But when the strings are different in length, and of equal thickness and material, the weight which must make the string that is 2 times longer rise to the Octave must be <gloss>six-fold</gloss> eight-fold <note>The gloss "sextuple" appears to be a correction or alternative note for "octuple."</note>, because the fourfold weight only brings the double-length string to the Unison of the half-length one; and then the fourfold weight makes it rise to the Octave.

V. One can say that 230 **toises** <note>A *toise* is an old French unit of length, approximately 1.949 meters (6.39 feet).</note> are the proper measure of direct sounds, since they travel this path in the time of one second, whether the wind is favorable or contrary, and whether the sounds are loud or soft; and that 162 toises are the measure of reflected sounds, since a syllable pronounced as fast as possible goes to strike a wall 81 toises away and then returns to the ear in the time of one second. Now, if sound is made by circles similar to those made on water, it is certain that the motion of the air which carries the sound is 1,870 times faster than the motion of water; from which one can conclude that air is 1,870 times easier to move, more liquid, less resistant, and lighter than water.

The proofs of this **Compendium** <note>Original: *Abregé*</note> will be found with a great multitude of other speculations, and several observations and experiments in the 19 books, and particularly in the *Treatise on Observations*.

->T H E  E N D.<-

### FIRST
# GENERAL PREFACE
## TO THE READER.

**THIS** Preface contains certain remarks that will serve for the understanding of some propositions, or the sequence of the books, which may be arranged in whatever order one wishes: and because the **Printers** have not always followed the numbers at the top of the pages, and they have restarted them several times against my design—as is seen in the **7th book of Instruments**, which speaks of **Instruments of percussion**, of which the first page should have been numbered 413—I did not wish to include the table of contents for these books, for fear of making it too difficult to use, because of the different characters which would have been necessary to signify each particular treatise. For example, it would have been necessary to use the first 8 letters **A, B, C, D, E, F, G & H**, to signify the treatises on **sounds**, **mechanics**, **the voice**, **composition**, **Instruments**, **Instruments of percussion**, and the **utility of harmony**, etc. However, if one desires it, it is not so difficult that it cannot be used profitably: especially since the book on the utility that **Preachers** and everyone can draw from harmony <note>Mersenne suggests that understanding harmonic principles can help orators and preachers improve their vocal delivery and persuasive power.</note> supplies that table in some way, and each person can make one for their own use at the end of their copy.

Now, the first notice I wish to give—after having prayed the Reader to correct the printing errors—is but a repetition of what I have said in several other places: namely, that I do not desire that one believe I am convinced of having demonstrated what I propose in the propositions, even though I often speak in an affirmative tone. Everything I have said should therefore be taken as a simple narration, unless one feels compelled by the experiments or the reasons I bring forward to embrace what I propose. For example, when I explain **sound** by the movement of the air, I do not at all prevent one from positing <term>species<note>In 17th-century physics, "species" (Latin: *species*) referred to the invisible forms or qualities that traveled from an object to the senses, a concept Mersenne was moving away from in favor of mechanical movement.</note></term>, which flow through the air like heat, and in some way like light, though with time; and when I have said that there is the same ratio between sounds as between the movements of the air or of the **strings**, I leave everyone the liberty to doubt whether sounds (not being homogeneous with strings) allow their ratios and proportions to be transferred to the sounds themselves; although if one considers the manner I use to prove the ratio of the **octave** and of the other **consonances** or **harmonic intervals**, they do not depend

<summary>Mersenne introduces his comprehensive work on music and acoustics, apologizing for irregular pagination and explaining his scientific approach, which favors empirical observation over dogmatic certainty.</summary>
<keywords>General Preface, Marin Mersenne, printing errors, book structure, physics of sound, species, scientific method, harmonic intervals, preachers</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the "General Preface" to Marin Mersenne’s "Harmonie Universelle" (1636). Mersenne is explaining his scientific methodology, specifically how he derives musical laws from the physical movement of air rather than just the physical properties of instruments, and how these principles intersect with broader physics and the work of contemporary thinkers like Descartes.</meta>

not at all from the length or thickness of the strings, because I use nothing other than the movements, or <term>beats of air<note>Mersenne uses this term to describe what we now understand as sound waves or frequency.</note></term> alone; so that if there is anything demonstrable in Music, one cannot, in my opinion, proceed with a better method than the one I use in all the treatises of this work. For the number of beats of air is found everywhere, as much in strings as in bells, which tremble exactly as many times as the strings when they are in unison: for example, if the string that makes the lowest and deepest sound of my voice trembles and beats the air 40 times during the time of one pulse beat, the bell that makes the unison will tremble 40 times in the same period, whether it is struck with a hammer or only touched with the tip of a finger; as happens with the string of a Lute, which will also have 40 tremblings in that space of time, whether it is plucked very hard, or only touched by the foot of a fly, or by the wind, as I show in the 3rd book on movements and in the first 4 books on instruments.

It must be noted in the second place that there are many things in the first book that must be modified according to what is in the 3rd, and according to the experiments that everyone can perform at their leisure; and that one can draw several conclusions from the first 3 books which I have not touched upon: for example, one can show that an arrow being shot from inside a boat will appear immobile to one who is outside said boat, assuming it goes as fast as the arrow when it is shot toward the West and the boat goes toward the East; and similarly that a cannonball fired on earth toward the West would not move at all with respect to one who remained stable, while the earth turned as fast toward the East, as would happen if the opinion of <term>Aristarchus<note>Aristarchus of Samos (c. 310 – c. 230 BC) was an ancient Greek astronomer who first proposed a heliocentric model, suggesting the Earth rotates on its axis and revolves around the Sun.</note></term> were true. Now the last propositions of the 3rd book serve for the understanding and correction of the 1st and 2nd, which must not be judged in the last resort before having read said third book on motion, with its treatise on mechanics.

But it is good to add two things to this book, the first immediately before the 4th proposition on page 165, namely that one of the <note>Mersenne is likely referring to René Descartes, whose "subtle matter" theory was highly influential at the time.</note> excellent minds of this time, giving the reason for the <term>reflection of bows<note>Here, "reflection" refers to the elasticity or the "snapping back" of a bow when the tension is released.</note></term> and other bodies, considers first that all the bodies we see are filled with a certain <term>subtle matter<note>A theoretical invisible fluid that Descartes and his contemporaries believed filled all space and mediated physical interactions like gravity and light.</note></term> which cannot be seen, and which always moves extremely fast, so that it passes easily through the pores, or the small voids, in the same way that the water of a river passes through the holes of a <term>fish trap<note>Original: "Nasse", a wicker basket used for catching fish.</note></term> or a basket.

In the second place, that bodies which return after being bent have their pores so arranged when they are bent that this subtle matter can no longer pass through so easily as before: from which it happens that it strives to restore them to their first state. This can happen in several ways: for example, if one imagines that the pores of a bow that is not bent are as wide at the entrance as at the exit, and that by bending it one makes them narrower at the exit, it is certain that the subtle matter which enters inside through the wider side makes an effort to come out through the other side which is narrower: and if one imagines that the pores of this bow were round before it was bent, and that afterward they are oval, and that the parts...

<summary>Mersenne argues that musical pitch is determined by vibrations (beats of air) and explains the relativity of motion using the rotation of the Earth as an example. He also introduces a contemporary theory of elasticity based on the movement of "subtle matter" through physical pores.</summary>
<keywords>beats of air, vibrations, frequency, unison, Aristarchus, heliocentrism, subtle matter, René Descartes, elasticity, mechanics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "General Preface" to Mersenne’s "Harmonie Universelle" (1636). Following the previous page’s discussion on the movement of air and sound, Mersenne here engages with Cartesian physics—specifically the behavior of "subtle matter"—and provides experimental data comparing the motion of rolling balls to pendulums to establish laws of frequency and vibration.</meta>

<sig>A ij</sig>

of the **subtle matter**, which must pass through, are also round, it is evident that when they present themselves to enter these oval holes, they exert force to make them round, and consequently to straighten the arc, inasmuch as one depends on the other. <note>original: "matiere subtile." This refers to the Cartesian theory that the universe is a plenum filled with particles of varying sizes; "subtle matter" was the finest grade of these particles.</note> Now it seems that the subtle bodies of which he speaks can easily be understood as **atoms** that move perpetually: but we will see the physical demonstration of it when it pleases him to give it. <note>Mersenne is likely referring to René Descartes here, awaiting the publication of Descartes' broader physical theories.</note>

The second must be added to the 12th proposition, page 222, immediately before. In the second place: Having then rolled a **lead ball** in the semicircle LBK, whose radius AB is 2 feet and 7 inches, and having hung another ball of the same weight by a string of the same length, this string with its ball makes 19 returns in the same time that the ball rolling in the said quarter-circle makes only 18; so that the ball suspended by a string always gets ahead of the returns of the rolling ball by one of its cycles. But whereas the rolling ball only goes 9 times from L toward K, and only returns 9 times from K toward L before coming to rest at point E, the suspended ball goes at least 1500 times from L toward K, and returns as many times from K toward L before coming to rest at B when it is attached to the string AB. By this, one sees how much the wooden plane LBK hinders the cycles of the ball rolling upon it; for if it were so perfectly round and polished that it did not impede it any more than the plane one imagines in the air, the ball would go at least as many times, and just as high on one side and the other, while rolling as it does while attached to the string. I have said *at least*, because it would not have the hindrance of the string, which slows and slightly impedes the magnitude of the ball’s returns. Now the cord that holds the ball B suspended, being 3 and a half feet long including the ball, completes each of its turns in exactly the same time that the other ball completes each of its rollings in the circle whose radius is 2 feet and 7 inches—that is to say, each turn of its rolling lasts one **second minute**. <note>original: "seconde minute." In this period, a "minute" was the first small division of an hour, and a "second minute" was the second division, which we now simply call a "second."</note> Thus, the radii of the rolling circles are in a <term>square ratio<note>original: "raison doublée." A mathematical term indicating that the relationship between two values is based on the square of their proportions.</note></term> of the times, as we have said of the strings that hold the balls suspended.

There occurs yet another remarkable thing in the number of rollings that take place on the inner edge of two **sieves** of different sizes; namely, that the same ball makes as many cycles and returns in the sieve whose diameter is 5 feet 2 inches as it does in the one whose diameter is only a foot and a half. For example, a very round and well-polished ivory ball, the same size as the lead one, makes 20 cycles and as many returns in both sieves when allowed to roll from the top of their quarter-circles; but each cycle, which lasts one second in the large sieve, lasts less in the small one, following the <term>square root ratio<note>original: "raison sous-doublée." This refers to the inverse relationship where the time of a swing or roll is proportional to the square root of the distance or radius.</note></term> of times to spaces.

From this one can conclude that **harmonic strings** of the same thickness, same material, and same tension make as many returns as one another, regardless of the difference in their lengths; but in such a way that the entire period of all the returns of the shorter one lasts less time as it is shorter, as I have remarked when speaking of strings; for it seems that the returns of all sorts of reflections occur for the same reason: for example, when the parts of a bell...

Mersenne is currently discussing the mechanics of vibration and the movement of objects (like balls and pendulums) as they return to their "natural place" or center of gravity. This serves as a physical foundation for his later theories on sound waves and musical pitch.</meta>

...which quiver and often go back and forth before coming to rest; this movement occurs because of the excessive force that each part impresses and gives to itself to return to its natural place, much like a **ball** that falls or is suspended from a **string** shakes too strongly to stay at its center upon its first return.

---

It must also be noted, regarding what I have said in these books about the **falling of weights** <note>original: "cheute des poids." This refers to Mersenne's studies on gravity and acceleration, which were contemporaneous with those of Galileo.</note>, that it is likely heavy bodies would not pass beyond the **center of the earth**, if they became less heavy or lighter as they approached the said center, in which they have no weight. This is because their momentum would seemingly cease bit by bit, no longer finding the body disposed to receive it due to the absence of its weight. But it is very difficult to test how this truly occurs, which is why I shall say no more of it.

In the third place, most of the propositions in the **Book of the Voice** <note>One of the specific treatises within the "Harmonie Universelle."</note> deserve entire volumes, which could be written by those who have enough experimental experience to confirm all that one could desire on such a subject. However, it would be appropriate for some excellent harmonic philosophers to perform or witness for themselves the perfect anatomy of the **larynx** and all other parts that contribute to the **voice**, as well as that of the **ear**, in order to examine the movement of the **eardrum**, the **muscles**, and the **ossicles** <note>Small bones in the middle ear: the hammer, anvil, and stirrup.</note> which create or assist in hearing. For physicians do not provide us with enough light on this subject.

As for the **Book of Songs**, I have nothing to note except the great use that can be derived from it for everything depending on all sorts of encounters and combinations, and the charming remark regarding the names of two **Religious** <note>original: "Religieux." Likely referring to members of a Catholic religious order; Mersenne himself was a Minim friar.</note>...
