--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the General Preface of Mersenne’s "Harmonie Universelle." Having previously discussed the physics of motion and pendulums, Mersenne now shifts to the mathematical and mechanical applications of music, as well as the spiritual responsibilities of musicians.</meta>

...that Monsieur de Peiresc <note>Nicolas-Claude Fabri de Peiresc (1580–1637), a celebrated French scholar and polymath who corresponded extensively with the leading thinkers of his day.</note>, the honor of all Provence, sent to me—namely Father Salvator Mile and Father Louis Almerat—each of whom has in the **anagram** of his name the six syllables *ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la* <note>These are the syllables of solmisation, the precursor to modern "do, re, mi..."</note>, without changing, adding, or removing any letter. If the identity of anagrams signified a resemblance in temperament, humor, or spirit, one would judge that they would love each other greatly and that they would symbolize one another in many things; for example, that they would have the same birth, and so on. But one does not find that these anagrams, any more than a shared nationality, contribute or signify anything in the lives of men. I only add that the 720 songs <note>Original: "Chants." 720 is the mathematical result of the number of ways six notes can be rearranged (6 factorial).</note> I have provided from these six notes are enough to challenge all the most excellent musicians in the world, should they undertake to determine which is the most beautiful, the best, and the most pleasing; and then the degree of pleasure in each, and the subject for which it is most suited. One can also adapt this variety to the six different time signatures, or to the six values of the six different notes seen in the 20th proposition of the 4th Book of Composition, from the breve to the sixteenth note; or to the first six numbers, and to the six letters of a given name, to make 720 variations or anagrams. And if one wishes to see the 40,320 songs composed of the eight notes of the octave <note>8 factorial (1x2x3x4x5x6x7x8) equals 40,320 permutations.</note>, I have made an entire volume of them.

In the fourth place, the treatise on **consonances**, **genres**, **modes**, and **composition** can serve all sorts of people, whether for singing or for giving the reasons for everything that occurs in Harmony. Thus, these four books are sufficient on their own for musicians, without any need for them to read the others, except for those on instruments. And because musicians are not usually very intellectual <note>Original: "spirituels." Mersenne suggests that practical musicians often lack deep philosophical or spiritual insight, a common academic prejudice of the era.</note>, I have inserted many considerations that can serve them as books of devotion, so that instead of abusing the Harmony that God has distributed to men to praise Him, they may employ it for His honor; and so that what serves to corrupt wicked spirits may instead elevate theirs to the contemplation of divine things and make them worthy of Heaven.

In the fifth place, the books on **instruments** provide much knowledge and many experiments that are not in other books; this is why it is appropriate to read them, as one will admit when leafing through them. Now, I did not wish to describe at length several new instruments—for example, **spinets** that have an endless bow to play entire concerts of **viols**, and **organs** that pronounce syllables as well as men—so that the makers <note>Original: "facteurs," referring to instrument builders.</note> who contributed their invention may receive some fruit from their labors. It suffices to say that one can compose harmonic machines that will do more than the "talking head" attributed to Albert the Great <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280). Legend claimed he built a brass automaton capable of speech.</note>, and which will delight all those who do not know the secrets of Harmony joined with those of mechanics.

In the sixth place, I also give here the manner of dividing the neck of the **luth**, the **viol**, and other instruments to place the equal semitones <note>This refers to "Equal Temperament," the tuning system that allows instruments to play in any key by dividing the octave into twelve equal parts.</note>, so that makers can adjust the frets of several lutes in a very short time and with great ease, without searching by trial and error. Now, this method depends on the numbers in the 9th proposition of the 4th Book of Instruments, or on the first column of the **Diapason** <note>In this context, the "Diapason" refers to a scale or a measurement table for tuning.</note> of the Organs, which is seen on page 339 of the 6th Book of Instruments: namely 1000, 944, etc.

<summary>Mersenne explains the mathematical permutations of musical notes, the moral purpose of his work for musicians, and previews mechanical innovations like "talking" organs and a mathematical method for fret placement.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Mersenne’s technical instructions on the mathematical division of the lute neck. It follows a discussion on musical theory and provides a practical method for instrument makers to determine fret placement using geometric proportions rather than complex calculations.</meta>

...so that if one were to divide a line drawn on the **Lute**, from its **bridge** <term>cheualet: the wooden piece on the body of the lute where the strings are tied</term> to its **nut** <term>fillet: the strip of ivory or wood at the top of the neck where the strings pass into the headstock</term>, into a thousand parts, the second number, 944, would give the location of the first **fret** <term>touche: literally "touch," referring here to the gut frets tied around the neck</term>, and the 3rd, namely 891, would show the location of the 3rd <note>Mersenne likely refers here to the 3rd point of division; on his table, 891 corresponds to the 2nd fret.</note>. But because this division is too long and too difficult to perform—even though, once made on two rulers joined at the ends in the form of a compass, it could serve forever—it is sufficient to first mark the first fret signified by **B**. This is done by dividing the space between the nut and the bridge into 50 parts; removing 3 of these parts will show the location of the first fret, as I stated on page 202 of the *Instruments*. This being done, if one has a **proportional compass** <note>A mathematical instrument consisting of two rulers of equal length joined by a pivot, used for scaling and proportions.</note>, it must be opened so that the length from the nut to the bridge falls between the 56 of the equal parts on the two legs, because 56 is the difference between 1000 and 944. Then the difference of all the other numbers, which follow down to 500, will give all the other frets. If the proportional compass is too small, one should take an opening of double 56, namely 112. And because makers do not usually have these compasses, it is enough for them to divide half of a ruler into 56 parts, starting from the top. When this is joined with a pin to the end of another ruler, which is also divided, giving them the freedom to open like a compass, I say that if they open these two rulers so that the opening of 56 (taken with a common compass) gives the size of the first fret, the opening of 53 will give the size of the second, that of 49 will give the 3rd, and so on for the others following the small Table below. The first column contains the 13 numbers of the **Scale** <term>Diapason: here used to mean the full measurement of the string's length or range</term> divided into 12 equal semitones by the 11 numbers, which signify the 11 **mean proportionals** <term>moyennes proportionnelles: a mathematical method to find values between two extremes (1000 and 500) to ensure equal tuning</term> between 1000 and 500, which give the two extremities of the **Octave**. The 2nd column contains the numbers for the makers' compass made of the two previous rulers, which they may call the **Scale of the Necks**.

<margin>Table for instrument makers.</margin>

| I | II |
|---|---|
| 1000 | |
| 944 | 56 |
| 891 | 53 |
| 842 | 49 |
| 794 | 47 |
| 750 | 44 |
| 708 | 42 |
| 668 | 40 |
| 630 | 38 |
| 599 | 36 |
| 562 | 33 |
| 532 | 30 |
| 500 | 28 |

<column-break/>

Now, the 12 numbers in the 2nd column are nothing other than the differences between those in the first; so one must always leave the rulers open in the same way, and transfer the openings of the eleven numbers one after the other onto the necks—namely, the opening of 53 for the 2nd fret. As for the first, it must be marked as I said above, and having set the opening at the end or at the points of the rulers where 56 is found to the size of the first fret, the numbers 53, 49, etc., will give the eleven others, if one carries these openings from the first fret, one after the other, toward the bridge—that is to say, descending. And if there is any maker who cannot understand this, I will show him the **Practice** of it whenever he wishes.

I shall do the same for all those who find some difficulty in what I have said elsewhere in all the treatises on **Harmony**, provided they...

<meta>This page continues Mersenne’s technical discussion on instrument construction, shifting from the mathematical division of lute frets to the spiritual purpose of music and the description of a new keyboard instrument designed to mimic a viol ensemble.</meta>

...should resolve to use them to praise the great Master of the universal concert, in imitation of the Royal Prophet <note>The "Royal Prophet" refers to King David, traditionally considered the author of the Psalms.</note>, who exhorts us all in this beautiful verse of the 33rd Psalm:

> **Magnify my Lord with me, and let us exalt his name together.** <note>original: "Magnificate Dominum meum, & exaltemus nomen eius in idipsum." This is Psalm 34:3 in modern numbering, or 33:4 in the Vulgate.</note>

I add, nonetheless, that the proportional numbers of the 14th and 15th propositions of the first [book], and those of the 37th of the 6th book of *Instruments*, provide the division of the necks much more exactly.

In the seventh place, I wish here to describe the instrument which serves to play 5 or 6 **Viols** <term>viole: a bowed, fretted string instrument held between the legs, distinct from the violin family</term> by playing the **Harpsichord** <term>Clauecin: a keyboard instrument where strings are plucked by quills</term>, because it is very suitable for concerts. For although I have spoken of it in the 12th proposition of the 4th, and in the 30th of the 7th Book of *Instruments*, it is appropriate to add that the **endless bow** <term>archet sans fin: a mechanical device, likely a rotating wheel or belt, that provides a continuous bowing action</term> was discovered here by two young men while my books were being printed. Namely, by the one I name in the Preface of the said 30th proposition, and by a German, who used **gut strings** whose ends are so well joined with **fish glue** <note>A strong adhesive made from the swim bladders of fish, used for delicate instrument making because it allows for nearly invisible joints.</note> that they seem to be continuous. Now, this bow is stretched over two small wooden pulleys that turn on their axes and are perpendicular to the **horizon** <note>Meaning they are positioned vertically.</note>, as are the strings, in the fashion of those of a **Harp**, whose shape he has imitated. Thus, one can see through the strings all those who are behind the instrument, which makes it suitable for seeing all those who sing in the concert, and consequently the one who beats the time. This is something that should be observed in **Organs**, so that the **Organist** might see those who sing in the choir. On this point, it is good to remark that the organs of the churches of Italy are better arranged than ours, in that they can be seen equally from both sides, both of which serve for ornament and display; whereas one sees only the front of ours, because their bellows-works are in a room situated behind. But they place their bellows below in a cellar made for this purpose, so that the wind is carried by a very long wind-trunk, which rises alongside a pillar to the place where the organ is positioned between two pillars.

As for the instrument that creates the concert of viols, and to which one can give the name **Archiviole**, or any other that one wishes, the one I am describing has no other body for resonance than a large arm similar to the concave body of the Harp. And when the bow slackens, whether for replacement or by the passage of time, the pulleys are pulled to the right and left by means of screws, which hold them attached against a rod or another piece of wood.

This bow slides along a wooden ruler that crosses the instrument toward the end of the **keys** <note>Mersenne uses "marches" to refer to the keyboard keys.</note>, to which are fitted small pieces of wood, brass, or iron in such a way that they press the strings against the bow as soon as one presses them down to play. But the Frenchman has succeeded even better than the German, because the body of his instrument, being like that of the Harpsichord, resonates much better and produces such a great harmony that it leaves the listeners in admiration. His pulleys have axes parallel to the horizon <note>Meaning they are positioned horizontally.</note>, and the movement that drives the bow is composed of only a single wheel with a pulley. But it is necessary to attach a piece of **rosin** <term>colophone: a hard resin rubbed on bows to create friction against strings</term> near one of the said pulleys over which it passes, so that it may be rubbed by it. And if one fears that it might be too harsh, by reason of the continual touch of the rosin, one can move it away as much as one likes by means of a small spring or **register** <note>In this context, a mechanical slider or control mechanism similar to those found on an organ or harpsichord.</note>, like those of which...

<sig>A iiij</sig>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues Mersenne’s technical discussion on keyboard instruments, specifically focusing on the ability to transpose music, the construction of the "Archiviole" (a bowed keyboard instrument), and the invention of fixed-pitch instruments using metal cylinders.</meta>

One uses [these methods] to vary the registers of the **Harpsichord** <term>Clauecin: a keyboard instrument where strings are plucked by quills</term>, in favor of which I say that they can be raised or lowered by a whole tone, or a semitone, or several, in order to play in all the keys of the concerts, as was done by the excellent maker of Florence, Monsieur Rameriny <note>Likely Francesco Ramenghi, a celebrated 17th-century Florentine instrument maker</note>, who placed up to 5 different pitches on the **Harpsichord**, in order to accommodate and adjust it to the pitch of all sorts of songs. This can also be done just as easily on the **Archiviole** <term>Archiviole: a keyboard instrument designed to sound like a consort of viols by using a mechanical bowing system</term>, of which we are now speaking; for if one tunes it according to the equality of semitones <note>A reference to equal temperament, a tuning system that allows instruments to play in any key</note>—which have the advantage of making a new harmony heard by reason of their temperament being different from that of ordinary **Spinets** <term>Epinettes: small, often rectangular members of the harpsichord family</term> and ordinary **Organs**—and if one has 7 or 8 more keys than usual on the keyboard, one will begin the UT of *C sol* <note>The note C</note>, or the RE of *D re sol* <note>The note D</note>, etc., on whichever key one wishes, without any prejudice to the tuning.

But because the shorter and finer strings do not require such large bodies as the thicker and longer ones, if one wishes to have a perfect harmony from the **Archiviole**, its soundboard must be divided into 4 or 5 parts, so that the size of each corresponds exactly to the size of the strings, in order to imitate the different parts of ordinary **Viols**. This will in no way prevent the bow from touching all the strings, to which it must be very close so that they speak <note>In instrument making, a string or pipe is said to "speak" when it produces a clear sound promptly</note> promptly. Now, they may be made of brass as well as of gut, or indeed one can mix and twist them together, in order to vary the harmony and make it more charming and sweet.

The eighth remark of this Preface consists of the explanation of instruments that never go out of tune, which are easy to understand through the *Book of Bells* <note>Referring to Mersenne's own treatise on bells, "De Campanis," included in his larger work</note>, and by what I have said of the **Sounding Cylinders** in the 3rd book of *Movements*. For if one arranges 49 hollow or solid cylinders in the body of a **Harpsichord**, according to the harmonic ratios that I have explained in so many ways, the keys will strike these cylinders and make them sound as softly as one wishes. It is easy to mix in small bells of different lengths or thicknesses—for example, in the shape of dice or thimbles used for sewing—to vary the harmony in all sorts of ways; and for this purpose, one can make these bodies of gold, silver, brass, and other materials fit for resonating, to enjoy the mixture of metals through their sounds as well as by their fusion or melting.

Now, an instrument made of these bodies could serve as a rule, a canon, and an immobile and infallible **diapason** <term>diapason: a fixed standard of pitch, similar to a modern tuning fork</term> to regulate and tune all other kinds of instruments. Each cylinder, whether hollow or plain and solid, being carried or sent throughout the world, would be suitable for communicating the pitch of the organ, the voice, and other instruments, and for making all the musicians of the earth sing the same piece of music in the same pitch, instead of relying on the vibrations of a string, of which I speak in the 3rd book of *Instruments*, proposition 18.

The ninth [remark] belongs to organs, of which each octave can be made of 13 pipes of the same thickness, such that one will have only 4 kinds of thicknesses in the organ, as is easy to conclude by the 13th proposition of the 6th book of the *Organ*; but one cannot make the span of an octave with pipes of the same height, by the 12th proposition of the same book; now...

He also discusses the physical proportions required for the "Archiviole" and organ pipes to ensure harmonic perfection.</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the construction and tuning of instruments, transitioning from the physical layout of the Archiviole soundboard to the geometric proportions of organ pipes and a list of notable contributors to the musical and mathematical arts in 17th-century France.</meta>

Experience teaches that one must mix different lengths with different thicknesses to produce pleasing tones, which can be achieved in an infinite number of ways. However, it seems that the best of all is that of the 14th and then that of the 43rd proposition, though everyone is free to seek out others. For example, instead of giving the width of the diagonal to the pipe that descends an octave <note>original: "raison double," the 2:1 ratio which defines an octave</note> below the one that has the side of a square for its width, one can give it the width of the **mean proportional** <note>A geometric middle value; here used to determine pipe scaling</note> between the side and its diameter, which divides the double ratio into four equal ratios, just as the said diameter divides it into two equal ratios—that is to say, by half. Thus, the thicknesses, widths, or circumferences of these two pipes would be in the same ratio as a quarter of an octave—that is to say, the **Minor Third** <term>Tierce mineure: a musical interval spanning three semitones</term> composed of three equal semitones.

The tenth remark of this preface adds what I had forgotten in the 31st proposition of the 7th book on Instruments: namely, that **Jacques Mauduit** <note>Jacques Mauduit (1557–1627) was an influential French composer and lutenist</note> added the sixth string to the **viols**, which had only five previously, and that he was the first to introduce their ensemble <note>original: "concert"</note> in France in place of a **bass violin**, which people were content to join with the **Oboes** <term>Haut-bois: "high wood" or loud woodwind instruments</term>. I could also add several excellent composers to those of the said proposition, such as the **Sieur Moulinié** <note>Étienne Moulinié, a prominent composer of airs de cour</note>, who deserves much praise for the great pains he takes to make his concerts succeed to everyone's satisfaction; and the one I named in the 40th proposition of the 6th book on Organs, who is as exact and polished in his **counterpoint** <term>Counterpoint: the art of combining different melodic lines</term> as any other I know. There are several others who would deserve praise if I had knowledge of their capacity and their virtue—for example, those who are masters of the King’s Music, both of his Chapel and of his Chamber, such as the **Sieurs Picot** and **Formé**, and some others of whom I can only speak by the report of others, because I have not heard their music. The **Sieur de Cousu**, Canon of St. Quentin, is also excellent in this art, as he will demonstrate through his treatises when it pleases him. 

Now, if I wished to speak of men of high birth or quality who take such pleasure in this part of **Mathematics** that one could perhaps teach them nothing, I would repeat the name of the one to whom the *Book on the Organ* is dedicated, and I would add **Monsieur Fermat**, Counselor to the Parliament of Toulouse <note>Pierre de Fermat (1607–1665), the famous mathematician</note>, to whom I owe the observation he made regarding the two numbers 17,296 and 18,416, whose **aliquot parts** <note>Aliquot parts: the proper divisors of a number excluding the number itself</note> mutually recreate each other, just as those of the two numbers 220 and 284 do <note>These are "amicable numbers," where the sum of the divisors of one equals the other</note>, and of the number 672, which is half of its aliquot parts, as is the number 120; and he knows the infallible rules and the analysis to find an infinity of other similar ones. **Monsieur de la Charlonye**, Honorary Royal Judge Provost of Angoulême, is also very skilled in the practice and theory of this art, as is **Monsieur de Beaugrand**, Secretary to the King, who has a very subtle and universal mind and of whom I have already spoken in other parts of this work. And the **Sieur de Roberval**, of whom I have given my opinion in the advertisement of the 44th proposition of the *Book on the Organ*, in the 8th corollary of the 9th proposition of the 2nd book, and in the advertisement of the 4th [proposition] of the 3rd book on *Movements*—at the end of which one sees his treatise on **Mechanics**—also knows the theory and even the practice of Music very well; though if one wishes to learn the rules of composition and create all sorts of counterpoint, simple or figured, it is appropriate to be taught...

<summary>Mersenne discusses the geometric scaling of organ pipes based on musical intervals and provides a "who's who" of 17th-century French music and science, acknowledging figures like Jacques Mauduit for developing the viol and Pierre de Fermat for his mathematical contributions to number theory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on instrument scaling and Jacques Mauduit's contributions, transitioning into a list of the most celebrated French musicians of the era and a concluding theological reflection on music's divine purpose.</meta>

...taught by those who have long experience in this practice, such as **Mr. Raquette**, Organist of Notre-Dame in Paris, **Mr. Vincent**, and several others who teach in Paris, both singing and composition.

The eleventh remark will serve to prevent oblivion from burying the names of those who have been excellent in France in any part of this art, some of whom are still living now. First, **Thomas Champion**, Organist and Spinet-player to the King, cleared the path in what concerns the **Organ** and the **Spinet** <term>Épinette: a small, plucked keyboard instrument of the harpsichord family, popular in the 16th and 17th centuries</term>, upon which he performed all kinds of improvised canons or fugues; he was the greatest **Contrapuntist** <term>Contrapontiste: an expert in counterpoint, the mathematical art of combining different melodic lines to play simultaneously</term> of his time. His son **Jacques Champion**, Lord of La Chapelle and Knight of the King’s Order, demonstrated his profound science and beautiful touch on the Spinet, and those who knew the perfection of his playing admired him; but after hearing the **Harpsichord** <term>Clavecin: the larger, more powerful successor to the spinet, where strings are plucked by quills</term> played by **Mr. de Chambonnières**, his son (who bears the same name), I cannot express my feelings except by saying that one need hear nothing else after him—whether one desires beautiful melodies and the fine parts of harmony mixed together, or the beauty of movements, the fine touch, and the lightness and speed of hand joined to a very delicate ear, so that it may be said that this instrument has met its final Master.

As for those who have excelled at playing the **Lute**, the first rank is held by **Vosmeny** and his brother, by **Charles and Jacques Hedinton** (Scotsmen), by "the Pole" <note>likely referring to the lutenist Wojciech Długoraj</note>, and by **Julian Perichon** of Paris. To these may be added the excellent lute players living now, such as **Messieurs Gautier**, **L’Enclos**, **Marandé**, and several others, and those who compose **tablature** <term>Tablature: a system of musical notation using letters or numbers to indicate finger positions on a fretboard rather than specific pitches</term> for this instrument, like **Mezangeau**, **Vincent**, etc.

For the **Cornett** <term>Cornet: a curved woodwind instrument with a trumpet-like mouthpiece, prized for its ability to mimic the human voice</term>, **de Livet** was the most excellent for performing fanfares, as was "the Englishman" for the **trumpet**. I leave aside the others of whom I have spoken in the treatise on each instrument, in order to add that **Antoine Demurat** has had no equal in singing, for he possessed a greater natural disposition than any man in the world, by reason of the goodness, beauty, and accuracy of his voice. **Girard de Beaulieu**, Bass of the King's Chamber, sang better than any other, and the **Cornille** family—both father and son—have left posterity in near despair of ever being able to equal them.

The last remark will serve to conclude this discourse with our Savior, whom the ancient Christians represented in the form of a Shepherd carrying a sheep on his neck and holding a **panpipe** <note>original: "seryngue," referring to the Syrinx or pastoral pipes</note> or pastoral flute in his right hand, as may be seen in several figures of ***Subterranean Rome*** <note>original: *Roma Soterranea*, referring to Antonio Bosio's 1632 landmark study of the Roman catacombs</note>, for example on pages 331, 351, 369, etc.; this instrument is similar to the one I explain in the 3rd proposition of the 5th book on Instruments. They also represented Him under the image of **Orpheus**, holding a Harp in His hands similar to one of those I describe in the 25th proposition of the 3rd book. By this, they intended to signify that Jesus Christ had come to persuade men toward the true worship of a single God, in place of the 360 Gods (or rather idols) that Orpheus, son of Oeagrus and father of Musaeus, had sought to introduce, as **Justin Martyr** remarks; although he confesses, along with **Clement of Alexandria**, that Orpheus later recognized his error. They prove this by the beautiful verses they report, in which he exhorts men to join and unite themselves perpetually with God, to whom be all honor and all kind of glory forever.

<meta>This page concludes the author’s preface by shifting from a list of notable musicians to a philosophical and theological argument for music’s moral purpose. It bridges the technical aspects of harmony with the spiritual duties of a Christian artist, reflecting the author's desire to see music return to its divine origins.</meta>

Now, since all those whom I have named in this Preface have the honor of being Christians, and since a true Christian must so express the life, actions, and passions of Jesus Christ in themselves that all who see them consider them as one and the same with Him—following the custom of the ancient Christians who extended their hands in the form of a cross when they prayed, as one sees upon a great multitude of sepulchers in the *Subterranean Rome* of Bosio <note>Antonio Bosio (c. 1575–1629) was an Italian scholar whose book *Roma Sotterranea* was the first systematic exploration of the Roman catacombs.</note>; a practice which Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD).</note> expresses in these terms, *they harmonized with Christ* <note>original Latin: *modulabantur Chriſtum*. This suggests that the early Christians didn't just sing, but lived in a rhythmic, harmonious imitation of Christ's life.</note>; and which Priests still do during the Preface and the Canon of the Holy Mass—it is reasonable that they behave as Christian **Orpheuses** <note>In Greek mythology, Orpheus was a musician whose music could charm all living things. The author suggests Christian musicians should use their talent to lead souls toward God instead of worldly distractions.</note>, provoking their listeners to leave behind their unruly passions to follow reason and virtue, and to make themselves similar to Him upon whom their salvation entirely depends. 

Certainly, it is a strange thing that out of a thousand players of the **Lute** and other instruments, one does not find ten who take pleasure in singing and expressing **Divine Canticles**; and who do not prefer to play a hundred **courantes**, **sarabandes**, or **Allemandes** <term>Dances: popular secular dance forms of the 16th and 17th centuries. The author views these as "vanity" compared to sacred music.</term> rather than a spiritual air. It seems as though they have consecrated all their labor to vanity, which they pour into the heart through the ears, as if through so many funnels. I confess that I am of the opinion of the most excellent political thinkers: namely, that this species of music, which softens and unnerves courage and which blunts the sharp wit of young people, ought to be banished from Republics, as with all other things that corrupt good morals. This could be easily achieved if the Magistrates established prizes and honorable rewards for those who practiced only **Dorian Music** <term>Dorian Mode: in ancient music theory, this scale was believed to inspire solemnity, courage, and moral discipline.</term>, and the other types we have spoken of, to celebrate the praises of God and to sing the laws that serve for the instruction of children. 

I am also astonished that so few musicians value the logic of harmony, and that one sees no Academy established for this subject. For all gatherings for concerts are made only for singing; whereas, out of the two or three hours employed in this exercise, many honorable men would wish that half of that time be taken to discourse on the causes that make pieces of composition agreeable, and why certain transitions from one consonance to another, and certain mixtures of dissonances, are better than others. For example: whether one must avoid the "false relations" of the **Triton** <term>Triton: an interval of three whole tones (like F to B) known as "the devil in music" for its harsh and unstable sound.</term> or the **False Fifth** <term>Fauſſe Quinte: a diminished fifth.</term>, as do those who dare not go from the **Ditone** <term>Diton: a major third interval.</term> to the **Diapente** <term>Diapente: a perfect fifth.</term> by conjoined degrees; and why these relations are esteemed worse than those of seconds and sevenths. Whether the manner of composing of **Caurroy** <note>Eustache du Caurroy (1549–1609), a favored composer of the French court known for his technical mastery.</note> is better or more charming than that of **Claudin** <note>Claudin de Sermisy (c. 1490–1562), a famous Renaissance composer of French chansons.</note>; of two or more given melodies, which is the best; why such-and-such a sequence of consonances gives such a strong strike to the spirit; and a thousand other similar things, which would attract men of quality to concerts. Such discussions would be more capable of charming away boredom and changing the ferocity and brutality of bad temperaments to form them toward virtue than all the concerts in the world. And if one added the consideration of Heaven to this, by considering all the means there are to make the practice and theory of Music useful for salvation and to draw from it motives for devotion, one could say that it would contribute to the effect of our **predestination**, so that there would be...

# BOOK ONE
## ON THE NATURE AND PROPERTIES OF SOUND.
### FIRST PROPOSITION.
#### TO DETERMINE IF SOUND IS PRODUCED BEFORE
#### *it is received in the ear, that is to say before it is heard, and if it is different from the movement of the air.*

<image-desc>Decorative initial letter 'I' (translated from the French 'C') featuring floral and fruit motifs inside a square frame.</image-desc>

It is a common thing to ask at the beginning of the treatises one makes on the sciences whether they have some true object, and what it is; for it is to speak uselessly if one does not know what one is speaking about. It is therefore appropriate, before proceeding further, to know if Sound—which is the subject or the object of Music and of hearing—has a real existence, and what it is. For there are many who believe that Sound is nothing if it is not heard, and that it is a simple impression of the air which should not be called Sound if there is no ear to hear it and to distinguish it from other things. Certainly, if that is the case, then hearing must give it the nature of Sound, just as the imagination and the understanding give existence to imaginary thoughts and to phantoms, which are called <term>beings of reason: things that exist only in the mind rather than in external reality</term>. For my part, I consider that Sound is no less real before it is heard than light or colors and the objects of the other external senses are before they are perceived; and that Sounds would not cease to be what they are even if there were no ear. This I would always say, even if I had admitted that Sound was not different from the movement of the air.

However, it seems that Sound is something other than this movement, since we feel great movements of air, or of water, or of some other similar bodies, which produce no Sound, or which produce it so weakly that it is not at all proportional to the force of the movement—as we experience with stones thrown into the air with slings, with musket balls, with artillery shells, and in several other movements which occur when rain and hail fall, and when the water of a deep river flows without making noise.

On the contrary, there are small movements that make great noises, such as those of the larynx, of the epiglottis, and of the tongue when we speak, or those of the air that makes Organs and other Flutes sound. Nevertheless, I do not consider that Sound is different from the movement of the body which strikes the Drum or the Membrane of the ear <note>The "membrane of the ear" refers to the eardrum.</note>; for it is not necessary to add a quality of the third species, which is commonly called a <term>passible quality: in 17th-century physics, a quality that can be physically sensed or can act upon a sensory organ</term>, since the movement of the air suffices to explain everything that is done by Sounds. For as soon as this movement has struck the membrane of the ea-

<summary>Mersenne begins his treatise by arguing that sound is a physical reality existing independently of the listener and is fundamentally identical to the movement of air. He addresses counter-arguments regarding why some large movements are silent while small ones are loud, concluding that air movement alone explains auditory phenomena.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, physics of sound, perception, air movement, auditory sense, beings of reason, eardrum, musical instruments</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether sound exists independently of the ear, moving into the physiological mechanics of hearing and the physical definition of sound as air movement.</meta>

2 Book One

ear, which encloses the **internal air**, and the **spirits** <note>In early modern science, "spirits" (animal spirits) were believed to be a very fine, vaporous fluid that traveled through the nerves to transmit sensory information to the brain.</note> that serve hearing, the movement of the external air is communicated to the internal spirits, either by means of the **small bone** <note>original: "petit os"; likely referring to the ossicles of the middle ear, such as the malleus or incus.</note> which is suspended from the auditory nerve and strikes another small bone as if on an **anvil**, or in some other manner, which Physicians ought to treat in more detail.

As for the difficulty of large movements that seem to produce no Sound, and small movements that make great Sounds, it can be said that the air is not as agitated in these large movements as one thinks; for when one strikes the air with a stick, with the hand, or with stones, arrows, bullets, etc., it yields easily, insofar as there is no body that prevents it from escaping. But it receives a greater violence from the movement and resistance of the organs that serve speech and Music, than by the movement of any other sort of body, the noise and whistling of which is not heard from as far away as speech.

One must therefore conclude that all movements made in the air, in the water, or elsewhere, can be called Sounds, insofar as they lack only an ear delicate and subtle enough to hear them; and one can say the same thing of the noise of thunder and of the cannon in regards to a deaf person, who does not perceive these great noises: for the movement or the trembling that he feels is not called sound, except insofar as it is capable of being felt by the spirits of hearing. In this way, Sound can be defined as *a movement of the external or internal air capable of being heard;* I said *or of the internal*, because of the noises that are made inside the ear. But it is difficult to find precisely what makes the movement of the air capable of being heard; for when I consider that a string of gut or of brass stretched in the air, and attached to two walls with nails or pegs fixed in the wall, and touched by the finger, a bow, or a quill, makes almost no noise, and that being stretched over the bridges of a **Lute**, a **Viol**, or a **Spinet** <note>A spinet is a smaller, harpsichord-like keyboard instrument where the strings are plucked by quills.</note>, it makes a great noise, and yet it is the same percussion of the air: that the wind split and cut by a piece of wood similar to that of the **mouth** <note>original: "lumiere"; the sharp edge or "fipple" of a flute where the air is split to produce sound.</note> of a flute pipe makes only a light whistling, and when it is followed by the body of a flute, it makes such a great noise, this leads me to conclude that what makes this movement capable of being heard is nothing other than when it shakes a quantity of enclosed air capable of shaking its prison, and of communicating itself to the neighboring external air until it arrives at the ear.

From this it follows that bodies that are the most airy are also the most **resonant**, and that the most terrestrial and heavy bodies are less so, as generally wood is more resonant than metals when they are used for the soundboards of instruments: and that among metals, lead is the least resonant, and among woods, the fir—the lightest and most airy of all—is also the most resonant, and the solid and heavy beech is less so: and among firs, the driest and most divested of its terrestrial humidity is found to be the most resonant. Now one must note the term used to express this quality of bodies, which makes them multiply the first percussion of the air until it is rendered capable of touching the senses of hearing, namely ***resonants***, as if one were saying "sounding again," for this word expresses the sound that comes to our ear, which is not the first Sound, but the echo multiplied from the first air that touches the string until that which touches the ear—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical nature of sound, specifically addressing the phenomenon of echoes and providing a defense against philosophical objections that sound is something other than the movement of air.</meta>

# Of the nature & properties of Sound.

...; and what we call *Echo* is Sound returned and sent back by the instrument that multiplies the Sound, and reflects it just as mirrors reflect light.

We can explain the multiplication of Sound by that of the heat of the air which is heated by a great fire, in as much as we do not feel the heat of the fire immediately, unless we touch it, but rather that of the heated air: in the same way, no ear can sense any Sound other than that which is multiplied, and which proceeds from the first. All this does not prevent Sound from being called a <term>collision or beating of air</term> <note>The author is defining sound as a physical event (percussion) rather than an abstract quality.</note>, which bodies make in the medium that receives the movement, and which is struck or broken and divided by the bodies that produce or receive the movement, since this collision is the cause by which we perceive this movement, when it alters or moves the spirits of hearing <note>In 17th-century physiology, "spirits" were subtle fluids in the nerves that allowed the senses to communicate with the brain.</note>, and because the cause can receive the name of its effect.

## PROPOSITION II.

*To determine how movement and Sound occur, and why it is that several very fast and rapid movements make no Sound that can be heard, such as the movements of several wheels and other bodies that move in the air or in the water: and that several very small movements make great Sounds.*

**T**his Proposition will serve to answer the objections that can be made against the preceding one, and will show why we hear great Sounds where the movements seem to be very small. Those who say that Sound is different from the movement of the air bring forward several reasons, the first of which is that the object of hearing must be a quality, like that of the other senses, and that movement is a <term>common object</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "common sensibles" are things like shape or movement that can be perceived by more than one sense, whereas "proper sensibles" like color or sound were thought to belong to one sense alone.</note> of all the senses. The second, that air cannot penetrate walls, through which one hears noise. The third, that two men could not hear the words they spoke at the same time, for the reason that the air cannot receive two contrary movements at the same time: and that there is no appearance that the air is moved in so great a space as that in which one hears the voice. The fourth, that several small movements of air often make more noise than larger ones, as I said at the beginning. But it is easy to respond to these difficulties, for it is enough that the object of each sense be proportioned to the organ, and to the power of the soul which is touched by it through the mediation of the senses, without it being necessary to attach it to quality rather than to quantity: although one could say that the movement of the air, water, or some other body has the quality of making itself heard: but this consideration adds nothing new to the movement of the air, which is just as much movement without the ear as when one supposes the ear, although one does not call it Sound until it has struck the <term>drum of the ear</term> <note>The eardrum or tympanic membrane.</note>, upon which it imprints a movement similar to itself; this does not prevent it from having the entire nature of Sound, even though it may never serve an ear.

It must therefore be said that Sound, being simply considered in the capacity of Sound, is nothing real but a simple consideration and affection of movement.

<sig>A ij</sig>

<summary>This page defines sound as a physical collision of air and explains echoes through the analogy of reflected light. The author defends the "movement of air" theory of sound against traditional philosophical objections by arguing that sound is a specific perception of movement rather than a unique substance or quality.</summary>

<keywords>echo, acoustics, movement of air, eardrum, spirits of hearing, physics of sound, Aristotelian philosophy, sensory perception</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the theory that sound is the physical movement of air by refuting common scientific objections. It uses analogies of water ripples to explain how sound waves interact and travel across vast distances.</meta>

### Book One

If one examines the object of the other **Senses**, one will find that they are no more qualified than **Sounds**; for example, the object of taste and smell consists of the evaporation and exhalation of <term>small bodies</term> <note>These "small bodies" refer to corpuscles, reflecting the 17th-century belief that all senses are triggered by physical particles hitting the sense organs.</note> that emerge from the object that one tastes or smells: the object of touch is nothing other than the quantity of figures and their properties, such as the soft, the hard, the polished, etc.

As for the second reason that is put forward, I admit that one cannot hear through walls if there is no place through which the air can communicate, or if the walls are not shaken by the **Sound** made by the person who is enclosed, or who is outside: for if the walls vibrate, they will communicate the movement of the interior air to the exterior, or from the exterior to the interior. Now, it is not as difficult as one might imagine for a man who is enclosed between four walls to imprint some sort of movement upon them by the force of his voice, or of some other **Sound**: for moved air, finding no exit, has great effects, and experience shows that **Sound** is greatly diminished by the interposition of a wall, or of some other solid body. It seems to me, therefore, that one must conclude that the walls do not vibrate strongly enough when one cannot hear the sound: but I will speak more fully of this difficulty in another place.

The third reason has no force, for we experience that one hears sound even when the wind is contrary, and consequently that the movement of the air caused by the wind opposes the movement that we call **Sound**; and this contrariety which hinders the **Sound** can be so great by reason of the violence of the winds or other noises, that one will not hear it at all.

When two or several men speak at the same time, the air retains the impressions it receives from each of them, just as calm water receives those of stones thrown into it; for one notices that they make different circles, which extend little by little to the edges, and which are nonetheless not as distinct nor as remarkable as if one had thrown a single stone: but the difficulty of these circles deserves a particular discourse. It is for the same reason that the voices of two or several men speaking at the same time are more confused and less intelligible than when they speak one after the other.

As for the space in which the movement of the air or the **Sound** extends, one must not be surprised if it is very large by reason of the little resistance made by the air, as is experienced with <term>artillery fire</term> <note>Cannons were the loudest man-made sounds of this era; their reports were often used in scientific experiments to measure the speed and distance of sound.</note>, which agitates it up to twenty or thirty <term>leagues</term> <note>A league is roughly 4 kilometers, meaning the author claims sound travels over 100 kilometers.</note>; perhaps even the movement made by the collision of two bodies goes as far as the end of the air, that is to say, up to the <term>firmament</term> <note>In early modern cosmology, the firmament was the boundary of the physical heavens or the sphere of the stars.</note>, or higher, if it extends higher, just as the circles made by stones thrown into the water go as far as the edges; for it is as easy to explain this movement as it is to explain in what manner a stone being thrown into the **Ocean** causes all parts of the **Ocean** to move, so that the part of the water that the stone causes to rise extends everywhere to restore the <term>equilibrium</term> of the water; for if it extended only to the neighboring parts, they would be higher than the most distant ones, which would lose their balance and would no longer be poised.

And one could say that if the **Ocean** covered the whole earth, as it did before God had separated the waters from it, and if it were calm, that the stone thrown into it under the **Arctic pole** would make circles which would grow...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that sound is a physical movement of air. After addressing objections about sound passing through walls, the author now refutes the idea that sound requires "intentional species"—a philosophical concept of invisible mental images—by comparing acoustics to fluid dynamics in water and the mechanical behavior of projectiles.</meta>

-># Of the nature & properties of Sound.<-

...would always extend as far as the Equator, and which (perhaps) would always diminish as far as the Antarctic: but this difficulty requires another place for discussion, and besides, it is not necessary that the same thing happens in the air as happens in the water, especially as we are not outside the air, as we are outside the water.

The last objection supposes something false, for since movement and **Sound** are not at all different, the **Sound** is all the greater and stronger as the movement of the air is more violent; so that whenever one hears a great sound, one must conclude that the movement of the air is great. But if one considers the magnitude and violence of the movement only by the force exerted in the air, or in some other fluid body, one is often mistaken; this is because the air must be restrained, enclosed, broken, and reflected by the meeting of two solid bodies. If it is merely pushed from one side and has a free exit on the other, it will make little noise, as happens with the <term>arrow</term> and with <term>arquebus balls</term> <note>An arquebus was an early type of long gun; the author is noting that even fast-moving bullets are relatively quiet because they don't "trap" the air.</note> which move through the air and do not make a **Sound** proportionate to their speed. This is because the air which gives way suffers little violence in comparison to that which resists and encounters bodies between which it is enclosed, as is experienced in the movements of a <term>carter’s whip</term>, which makes a great noise because of the <term>snap of the cord</term> <note>Original: "regain de la chorde." This refers to the rapid "recoil" or snapping motion of the whip tip that compresses the air.</note> which traps the air.

One can here add several things belonging to the extent of **Sound**, which is called the <term>sphere of its activity</term>, and which are the reason that one hears from further away, as is experienced with beams and pipes; for when one strikes the end of a beam, or speaks into a pipe, the **Sound** carries further and more easily than it would without the aid of these bodies. But these considerations must be reserved for another place: for it is enough now to conclude that **Sound** is produced when the external movement of the air reaches the <term>auditory nerve</term>, that is to say, the part of the organ of hearing which receives the first strikes of the movement of the external air, to carry them to the mind which then forms a judgment.

The same thing must be said of water regarding fish, who hear our noises when the circles in the air strike the surface of the water, which creates other circles down to the ear of the Fish, just as circles in the water that make a noise imprint them into the air up to our ears, when we hear a noise made in the water. One must also conclude that there is no need for <term>intentional species</term> <note>In Scholastic philosophy, "intentional species" were thought to be invisible, non-material images or "likenesses" of objects that traveled through the air to the senses. The author argues they are unnecessary because physical air movement explains everything.</note> for **Sound**, since the movement of the air is sufficient, and we know that it does not carry itself in an instant like light: for there is no reason to say that these "species" would need movement or time to be carried, since they have no contrary. That is why I shall not speak of these images, or <term>intentional species</term> of **Sounds**, but only of the movements that make us perceive them: which will bring greater clarity and ease to our discourse, and perhaps greater satisfaction to the Reader.

Nonetheless, I do not wish to entirely reject all kinds of <term>intentional species</term>, whether of **Sound** or of other objects, which several people propose in order to establish a more delicate connection between the [sensory] power and the object than that which occurs by means of external natural, material, and bodily qualities, as if it were necessary to strip them of what they have that is too...

# Book One

...coarse, in order to elevate them to a more eminent and spiritual degree of being, so that I might have no disagreement with ordinary philosophers, and so that what I shall say in these books on Music does not depend on mere opinion, but is founded upon the truth of experience and reason. Now, I will explain more fully and exactly the strength and weakness of **Sound**, and several other difficulties in another place, for it is sufficient to have touched upon them in these first two Propositions, whose clarification and solution depend on several other Propositions. But since I have said that **Sound** is nothing other than the movement of the air, we must see whether this air is external or internal to the bodies that produce the **Sound**; and whether it is so necessary that no **Sound** can be made without one of the two, and then we will explain in what manner it occurs.

### COROLLARY.

Because I desire that the perfect Musician be knowledgeable in Philosophy, and that he should know the different ideas our ancestors had regarding the nature of **Sound**—so that no discussion of harmony can be started in any company he encounters for which he cannot provide a reason—it must be noted for his benefit that Democritus, Epicurus, and several others of their sect believed that **Sound**, which is created by the meeting or the striking of all kinds of bodies, is nothing other than a movement, or a projection of small bodies composed of <term>atoms</term> <note>Democritus and Epicurus were ancient Greek "atomists" who believed everything was made of tiny, indivisible particles.</note>. These atoms leave the bodies that make the **Sound**, just as rays leave the Sun; or they are already in the air, and being struck by the movement of bodies, they spread out in all directions through the <term>pores</term> <note>The author refers to the microscopic gaps or "voids" believed to exist between particles of air.</note> or the small voids of said air, until they meet no more void and are stopped by the small corpuscles or atoms that compose the substance of the air. So, following this opinion, one can imagine a great multitude of tiny invisible bodies, or atoms, flying through the air after it has been struck, going forth to affect all the ears they encounter in their path, in order to bring them news of what has happened in the air or in the bodies from which they departed, and of which they are the ambassadors, or the images and representations.

## PROPOSITION III.

->*Determining whether Sound is the movement of external or internal air, which is within the body that produces the Sound: and whether Sound can be made without the movement of one or the other.*<-

THIS Proposition seems very difficult to me because it is impossible to perform the necessary experiments for this subject, as will be seen in the continuation of these discourses. But in order to begin with what is most certain and most evident: I say first that the external air is sufficient to create **Sound**, provided it is agitated or struck strongly enough, as happens in all kinds of encounters. For all the noises made by the winds are nothing other than the different agitations of the air, which can occur in several ways, each of which requires its own particular discourse. But because it is believed that all kinds of bodies enclose and contain air within their pores, and

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of sound, specifically whether it is caused by the air outside or the air trapped inside a vibrating body. It moves from the theories of the ancient atomists to the Scholastic view of the four elements.</meta>

# Of the Nature and Properties of Sound.

...that ordinary philosophers hold that every mixed body is composed of the four Elements—namely of earth, water, air, and fire—one may add that the air trapped within a body similarly produces a **Sound**, since it is agitated as strongly as the body in which it is enclosed, whether it forms an essential part of said body or merely fills the small cavities called <term>pores</term> <note>In the 17th century, "pores" referred to any tiny, invisible spaces within a solid material.</note>. Now, this movement of the internal air does not change the **Sound** in terms of its being <term>grave</term> or <term>acute</term> <note>original: "graue & à l'aigu". These terms refer to low and high musical pitches.</note>, but it modifies and affects it with certain qualities or particular configurations that allow us to distinguish the **Sound** of one body from that of another, as I shall say elsewhere.

I do not see, nonetheless, that it is necessary to add this movement to explain the different qualities of **Sounds**, insofar as they can be attributed to the different shapes of the bodies, some of which are more or less polished or rough than others, even if the eye or hand cannot notice the differences. For experience shows, by means of crystal and glass lenses, and by concave mirrors—both spherical and parabolic—that surfaces which seem very polished and clean are actually uneven and filled with little valleys and mountains. From this, it happens that the **Sounds** of all kinds of bodies are almost always different in some way, even though they are at <term>unison</term> <note>Unison occurs when two sounds have the exact same pitch.</note> and are as loud as one another. Those who say that the interior air brings several differences to the external **Sounds**, or who compose the **Sound** from the movement of the interior and exterior air (where one is like the image or clothing of the other), can also add that the water and fire within bodies contribute to the difference in **Sounds**, since these two elements are susceptible to movement. For those who hold that fire is one of the elements composing bodies are obliged by their maxims to confess that fire is more mobile than air, and consequently that it must at least bring as great a difference to **Sounds** as the movement of air.

They may further say that the different **Sounds** produced by the same bell, or the same string, at the same time come from the different elements of which both are composed; and that the most <term>grave</term> and material **Sound**, which appears the strongest, is made by the earth; the second by water; the third by air; and the fourth by fire. Or, if they produce only three **Sounds**, as most often happens, one must attribute the first to earth and water, the second to air, and the third to fire. This being established, they might say that no body moves without creating a concert of three or four parts, each representing its particular element. But I do not wish to dwell here on these considerations, both because I believe that **Sound** has no need of movements other than those of the external air, and because there will be many other places where this opinion can be examined more specifically.

As for the movement of both these types of air, no one doubts that it is necessary; for although some believe it is not the air that creates the **Sound**, but rather the bodies moving within the air, they nevertheless admit that it is necessary for them to move. This cannot happen without the external and internal air moving similarly, unless we consider this movement in a <term>vacuum</term> <note>The "vacuum" or "void" (vuide) was a highly debated topic—whether space could truly be empty of all matter.</note>, which I shall discuss later, or in water, where movement creates **Sound**, as experienced with bells, whose **Sound** is more <term>grave</term> in water than in air by a <term>tenth</term> <note>A "tenth" is a musical interval spanning an octave plus a third.</note>.

# First Book

in the same manner, as I shall discuss elsewhere. For if the mere movement of water is enough to produce **Sound**, then the movement of air is not strictly necessary—although one might argue that the internal air within the <term>pores</term> <note>Tiny, invisible channels within a solid material.</note> of a bell moves while underwater, and that this is what creates the sound. Alternatively, one might attribute the sound to the entire body of the bell as it moves and its parts vibrate; however, this difficulty will be clarified by the question that follows.

## PROPOSITION IV.

### *Determining whether Sound can be produced in a universal or particular vacuum.*

We may consider two kinds of <term>vacuum</term> <note>original: "vuide". In this era, the existence of a total vacuum was a subject of intense debate among scientists like Descartes and Pascal.</note>: namely the universal and the particular. The first is nothing other than the total absence of all physical bodies in the world, which would occur if God ceased to preserve the bodies He created; for nothing would remain but the space they occupied, which is commonly called *imaginary* space. Nevertheless, one can consider another vacuum slightly less universal than the previous one: namely, a vacuum where air usually resides. If the air were removed from its current place, whether by being annihilated or moved elsewhere, it would leave the concavity of the <term>Firmament</term> <note>The sphere of the heavens or the sky.</note> entirely empty of air.

The second kind of vacuum is that which one imagines in the place of a specific portion of air. This could only happen through a force that separates the air and simultaneously prevents it from reuniting. But no one could achieve such a division except for Him whose power is greater than the momentum of all created Nature and the inclination it has for its own preservation—for which the perpetual union of all its parts is believed to be necessary. Now, it is just as difficult to know if **Sound** can be produced in a particular vacuum as in a universal one; but because **Sound** presupposes movement, we must first see if one or several bodies can move within a vacuum. For if such movement is not possible, we must conclude that **Sound** cannot be made there. Because this difficulty is not yet resolved and the question remains <term>problematic</term> <note>In 17th-century logic, a "problematic" question is one where both sides have plausible arguments and no definitive proof yet exists.</note>, I say that if a quantity of air moves in a vacuum in the same way it does when joined with other parts of the air, it will produce **Sound**, even if it cannot be carried to any ear. That is to say, its movement will have everything necessary on its part to be perceived by the ear as the quality of **Sound**. The same can be said of the internal air of bodies moving in a vacuum. Now, it is not difficult to explain how air or other bodies could have "reflexive" movement—that is, a movement composed of back-and-forth vibrations—within a vacuum. For the strings of a **Lute** placed in a vacuum, being pulled from their straight line, would vibrate at least as strongly as in the air, since their movement would not be slowed down at all. But since there is no vacuum in nature, and it is perhaps impossible, it is enough to have touched upon this difficulty without needing to examine others usually proposed: for example, whether a stone would descend perpendicularly toward the center of the earth through a vacuum, or whether <term>projectiles</term> <note>original: "missiles". Here referring to any object thrown or shot through space.</note> thrown into a vacuum would move perpetually, and several others which we may speak of elsewhere.

<header>On the nature and properties of Sound.</header>

### COROLLARY.

It is easy to conclude from what we have said until now, that **Sound** has no other medium than the external air, or other fluid bodies that surround the sounding bodies—such as water, wine, or the internal air which is part of said bodies—unless one argues that Sound exists in every part of the body by which it is produced.

---

### PROPOSITION V.

*To explain in what manner the air moves when its motion creates Sound, and which motions do not create Sound.*

We live in the air like fish in water, but with this difference: we cannot leave the air or reach its surface as they do. Fish often jump out of the water or stay upon it, but we always have more than fifty thousand leagues <note>A league was a common unit of distance; the author reflects the 17th-century belief that the atmosphere was vastly deeper than it actually is.</note> of air over our heads, for it extends to the Moon, and perhaps to the <term>Firmament</term> <note>The sphere of the heavens or the stars.</note> and beyond. Now, since we do not see the air—which could be called the "water" or "sea" of men and other animals, and which perhaps differs from water only in being more <term>rare</term> <note>In historical physics, "rare" means less dense or more thinned out.</note> and lighter—it seems we cannot better explain or understand how air moves when it sounds than by how water moves when bodies move within it and strike it with violence. 

For we must not only imagine the movement seen on the surface of water, when it forms circles that grow larger from the center where a stone was thrown to the edge of the vessel containing it; we must also notice if it makes similar movements all the way to the bottom, and if these circles extend through the entire depth or "solidity" of the water. This can be concluded both by the **Sounds** made in the air and those made in water, for they are heard equally from all sides. Although it is more difficult to test this in water than in air, we see that in the air, rockets and fireworks that make their noise a hundred <term>fathoms</term> <note>original: "toises". A unit of length roughly equal to six feet.</note> high are heard equally from all sides, both above and below.

One can nevertheless test this in water: for if several people swimming between two depths, or diving, have one person ring a bell underwater, and everyone hears the **Sound**—even though some have seven or eight fathoms of water above them and others only one or two—one can conclude that the circles seen on the surface are likewise formed throughout the entire mass of the water. Consequently, water and air create circles in every part of their depth when they are struck or pressed hard enough to make a noise.

Some imagine that the same part of the air that is struck and creates the **Sound** divides itself into an infinity of tiny particles, similar to the <term>atoms of Democritus</term> <note>Democritus was an ancient Greek philosopher who proposed that the world was made of tiny, indivisible particles called atoms.</note>, which spread out in a circle to carry the **Sound** in every direction. But this is not necessary, and there is no reason that can persuade us that the part of the air being struck detaches itself from the air to which it is

...continuous, so as to join another part of the air two or three thousand paces away: it is enough that it shakes the continuous air, and that it communicates the same movement it received, though more weakly and with diminishing force. For we experience in all bodies that are continuous, that one cannot move, push, or pull one of its parts without the others moving similarly, even though there is a great difference between the movement of bodies that are hard and firm, such as stones, metals, and wood; and those that are soft and fluid, such as air, water, and all sorts of liquids. This is because it is not possible to pull, push, or move one part of a hard body without all the others moving, as we experience when pushing a stone or a stick, because their parts do not yield to one another, as do the parts of air. None of the air’s parts could be moved without its entire <term>solidity</term> <note>Here meaning the entire bulk or volume of the air.</note> moving, if one part did not yield to the other.

Now it is very difficult to explain how this <term>yielding</term> <note>original: "cession." This refers to the way particles move out of the way to allow others to pass.</note> takes place, and in what manner the air and water restore themselves and return to rest after they have been struck and agitated. For if the part that is struck becomes <term>rarefied</term> <note>Made thinner or less dense.</note>, the others must <term>condense</term> <note>Become more tightly packed.</note> to make room for it; this would happen even if it did not rarefy at all, because it is pushed out of its natural and ordinary place. Therefore, it is necessary for the others to yield, because the parts of bodies cannot <term>penetrate</term> <note>A reference to the physical principle that two things cannot occupy the same space at the same time.</note> one another, and each needs a particular place different from that of others. 

For even though one can imagine that a drop of water poured onto other water spreads out without the need for all the other parts to move, nonetheless this cannot be done when it is added beneath the surface of the water, since all the upper parts must rise to make room for it. This would happen to the air if some new part were added to it, since it encloses and surrounds us; and because the part of the air that is forced changes place—that is, it moves closer to or further from the immobile point that one can imagine in imaginary spaces, or at one of the **poles of the world** <note>The fixed points of the celestial axis in 17th-century cosmology.</note>—it is necessary that all the upper parts yield to make room for it, whether it goes up or down, or right or left, unless one says that it enters into their <term>pores</term>. But we do not know if air has pores, and even if it did, the entire bulk or surface of the air that is struck or pushed cannot enter into said pores (which some believe to be <term>void</term> <note>Empty of all matter; a vacuum.</note> of every kind of body), for they are not as large as the air being pushed or struck.

There seems to be more likelihood in saying that the other parts of the air condense to yield to the momentum of the agitated part, though it is almost impossible to imagine how the compression or condensation of parts of the air can take place if it does not contain a vacuum. But the difficulty will be easier if one does not dwell on the vacuum, or on rarefaction and condensation: for one can say that when a part of the air has been struck, the other neighboring parts immediately succeed it in its place, and that the entire mass of the air moves when one of its parts changes place—as happens in baths where one washes, where all the water moves with every movement of the body. This is why I believe that those who are in the Heavens can perceive the movements of the air that happen here, though they are very weak when they reach the Heavens: for if one is forced to admit that a—

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>On the nature & the properties of Sound. 11</header>

...if a part of the water being moved in the middle of a vessel causes all the water to move, why can one not conclude the same thing of the air, which is a species of less coarse water, and which is contained within the <term>Firmament</term> <note>In 17th-century cosmology, the "Firmament" often referred to the vast expanse of the sky or heavens.</note>, or in the immensity of the Universe as if in a very large vessel, which is a work worthy of the Wisdom & the power of God.

### PROPOSITION VI.

->*Sounds have the same ratios between them as the movements of the air by which they are produced.*<-

If the nature of Sound is not different from the movement of air, as I have said in the first two Propositions, it is not necessary to prove this sixth one; but because many add a new quality to these movements, I say that it is always true, whatever quality or <term>intentional species</term> <note>A Scholastic philosophical term referring to the "image" or "form" of a sensation as it travels through a medium to the senses.</note> one wishes to add, insofar as it follows the differences in the movement of the air, which makes the Sound loud or weak, low or high, clear or obscure, according to the different <term>beats</term> <note>original: "battemens." Here, this refers to the frequency of vibrations.</note> of the air. This is experienced in the strings of instruments, & in organ pipes, whose Sounds appear lower the fewer times they beat the air, and higher the more times they beat it; so that if one compares two quantities of air, equal or unequal, where one is beaten four times while the other is beaten twice, one will perpetually find that the first Sound will be the <term>double</term> <note>In musical theory, a 2:1 ratio represents the interval of an octave.</note> of the other, and that the one will have as many degrees of highness as the air from which it comes has been beaten in frequency: but I reserve the experiments of the strings for the book on stringed instruments, & those of the pipes for the book on Organs.

As for the other differences & circumstances of Sound, such as strength or weakness, they come from the same movement of the air differently affected: for example, when of two quantities of air, which are beaten as many times as each other in the same duration, that which is larger makes a greater noise, which appears thicker, fuller, more massive & more filled; so that one can measure the thickness of the Sound, & say that it has all sorts of dimensions, like bodies; insofar as it follows, or is the movement of a body, namely of the air, or of other bodies whose movement is capable of Sound: for if the quantity of the air which is moved is very small, it renders the Sound small, slender & thin: if its movement or its beats last a long time, it is long, if they last a short time it is short, etc.

From this it comes that one can say of a weak and small voice, that it resembles a line, or a thread that has no support, as one says of a line of water that flows gently through a channel; and that the voice which is strong and well-supplied, even though it be high-pitched, is similar to a brass wire, which is firm and hard, and which supports itself: but I shall explain all these differences more exactly in the Proposition which follows, & in the 16th.
