### PROPOSITION VII.

->*Explaining how low and high Sounds are produced, and what makes them loud or weak.*<-

Although I have spoken of these two differences in the preceding Proposition, they nevertheless deserve to be explained more fully, because they serve as the foundation for Music, which considers the low and high of sounds more particularly than their other qualities. But it must be noted here once and for all that these two terms *low and high* <note>original: "graue & aigu"</note>, which the Greeks call *low* <note>original: βαρὺ (bary)</note> and *high* <note>original: ὀξὺ (oxy)</note>, signify that the Sound is hollow, deep, and low; or that it is high and sharp, if it is permissible to use these terms, for the French language is not yet so rich and fertile that it does not often need to borrow terms from the Greeks and Latins, or to employ metaphorical ones when explaining the sciences. The Latins say *Gravity & acumen* <note>original: "Grauitas & acumen," meaning heaviness and sharpness.</note> and the Greeks *lowness* <note>original: βαρύτης (barytes)</note> and *height* <note>original: ὀξύτης (oxytes)</note> to signify the depth and height of Sounds; and we can say "the gravity" of the Sound, but we have no correlative word that signifies the opposite to express the high Sound: for "acuity" <note>original: "acuité"</note> is not in common use. This is why we will henceforth say "the low," or "the gravity," and "the high" of the Sound (even though lightness is the opposite of gravity, and bluntness is the opposite of acute) in order to adapt our speech to common usage.

Now, there is no other cause for the gravity of Sounds than the rarity of the <term>beats</term> <note>original: "battemens." In 17th-century acoustics, this refers to the frequency of vibrations or oscillations of the air.</note>, that is to say, the small number of shocks and vibrations of the air. For sounds are all the more low as the number of beats is fewer; and because there are no low Sounds except in comparison with higher ones, and consequently one cannot establish a "low Sound" if one speaks simply and absolutely, it must only be noted that high sounds are produced by a greater number of beats or vibrations of the air. There is no high Sound that cannot be low in comparison to a higher one, just as there is no low Sound that cannot be high if it is compared to a lower one. This reasoning is confirmed by the experience of strings, whose Sound is all the more penetrating and high as their vibrations—or their back-and-forth movements—are more frequent, whether one uses a very thick or a very thin string, and whether it moves a little or a lot of air. From this it follows that high Sound does not come from the speed of the movement, nor the low from slowness, since it can happen that a movement fifty times slower will produce a Sound fifty times higher than another movement fifty times faster, as I demonstrate elsewhere; this is because a <term>Lute</term> string moves fifty times faster at the beginning of its movement than it does at the third or fourth moment after it has been touched. Here it must be noted that I use the word *Moment* to signify a very short time, which is equal to a <term>second</term> <note>original: "seconde minute d'heure." Mersenne is defining the modern "second" as the 3,600th part of an hour.</note>, that is to say, to the 3,600th part of an hour, which corresponds to a moment or a beat of the heart or the pulse, because this measure is suitable for explaining the measures and other circumstances of Music.

The second part of this Proposition belongs to the strength or the weakness of Sound, which similarly depends on the air, as I have already said in the preceding Proposition, because every time a larger...

# On the Nature and Properties of Sound.

...if a quantity of air is struck with a greater, or equal speed than a smaller quantity, the Sound is greater. Now this greatness can be taken in three ways, following the three dimensions of bodies: namely in length, in width, and in thickness.

As for length, one can say that of two strings equal in thickness, the one that is longer and yet is at a unison with the other produces a Sound that is greater in length, because it strikes more air, by reason of striking a longer portion of it; this occurs with the longer strings of the Theorbo <term>Theorbo: a large, plucked string instrument of the lute family with an extended neck supporting long bass strings</term> played open <note>original: "touchées à vuide," meaning played without pressing down on the frets</note>, when they are tuned to the unison of the shorter strings. It is more difficult to explain the width of Sounds, unless one says they are wider when the surfaces of the bodies hitting the air are wider; but as this width of bodies is not without their solidity, it belongs as much to the thickness of Sounds as to their width. For example, when a thicker string strikes the air, as happens with the thick strings of a Lute, it hits a larger surface of air than a thinner string of the same length, but the volume <note>original: "solidité"</note> of the air that corresponds to said surface is also greater, and consequently, thickness always accompanies width.

Now to return to the strength and weakness of Sound, it must be concluded that they have the same ratio to each other as the quantities of air that are struck the same number of times, provided the bodies are made of the same material; so that a string which strikes four times more air in the same amount of time makes a Sound four times greater than one that strikes four times less. Consequently, the strings of instruments sound all the more loudly the further they move from their straight line <note>This refers to the "amplitude" of the vibration—how far the string is pulled back before being released</note>, as we shall demonstrate elsewhere. The same thing must be concluded regarding the Voice, which is stronger as the lungs send more air to the larynx.

But here I encounter a difficulty, which consists in knowing why the Sound of a string stretched in the air does not make such a great Sound, or such a great noise, as when it is stretched upon an instrument: and why a string made of hemp stretched on the same instrument does not make as much noise as a string made of gut or brass <note>original: "leton"</note>, even though they are all at a unison, and equal in thickness and length, and move as much air as the others. To which I respond that the string stretched in the air has only the simple Sound, which vanishes suddenly because there is nothing to retain it; whereas the string stretched on instruments has the preceding Sound—which one may call direct—and the resonant Sound of reflection, which is preserved in the hollow of the instrument and sent back by the soundboard <term>Soundboard: the thin wooden face of an instrument (like a lute or violin) that vibrates to amplify the sound of the strings</term>, which greatly reinforces the Sound. Now, in the books on instruments, one may explain why, of several soundboards of equal size and of the same or different material, some resonate better than others, and why there are some instruments that are duller and others more resonant; and similarly why, of different strings stretched to a unison, some sound louder than others, even though they strike an equal quantity of air with an equal speed. I will only say here that a portion of the air enters into the pores of the hemp string, by which it is struck more softly, and that the many small filaments on the surface of this string, or several other inequalities, render the Sound darker, softer, weaker, and duller: to which one

### PROPOSITION VIII.

->*Sound does not communicate itself in a single moment throughout its entire extent, as light does, but rather over a period of time.*<-

Experience shows that all natural actions do not occur in a single moment, nor in an imperceptible amount of time, and that there are some which require time. For heat does not enter a subject unless it is first prepared, and light extends throughout the sphere of its activity in an instant—or if it requires some time, it is so brief that we cannot notice it. However, Sound can only fill the sphere of its activity within a span of time, which is longer as the place where the Sound is made is further from the ear. This is experienced in several ways, and particularly when one sees that the axe or the mallet of a woodcutter (or others who strike a body) has already struck two blows by the time one hears the first. This happens when one is at a distance of five or six hundred paces <term>pace: a unit of distance based on a walking step, roughly 2.5 to 3 feet</term>, or more.

Now, several experiments would need to be conducted to determine if the delay of Sound follows the size of the spaces; for example, if a Sound made two thousand paces away is only heard two seconds <note>original: "fecondes minutes," a seventeenth-century term for seconds</note> after it was made, and if it always maintains the same proportion in its delays. Because winds and the various dispositions of the air carry Sounds faster or slower, nothing certain can be established on this subject. Nevertheless, if one wishes to perform the necessary experiments, one must move half a league <term>league: a historical unit of distance, usually about 3 miles or 4.8 kilometers</term> away and have a musket or artillery piece fired, and then do the same thing at a distance of one league, marking the time that passes from when the flash is seen until the report is heard. Or, if one wishes to use four stations, one must first move a quarter-league away, secondly half a league, then three-quarters, and finally a full league, in order to see if each of these four equal distances delays the Sound as much as the others.

Now, this experiment must be repeated several times, particularly when the wind is favorable or contrary, and when the air is full of fog and vapors, or when it is calm, clear, and serene. Afterwards, one must observe the difference in the speed of Sound across these different conditions, and note if Sound travels faster from top to bottom than from bottom to top; in open countryside than across mountains or valleys; over the water of rivers or the sea than over land, etc. For different locations bring great differences to Sounds, as was noted at the Siege of La Rochelle <note>The Siege of La Rochelle (1627–1628) was a major military event in France; the author uses contemporary military observations to study acoustics</note>, from which here are the observations made very precisely by one of the Captains.

When one is on the same horizon <note>meaning the same elevation or level ground</note> as the place from which the shot is fired, and there is a valley in between, the report is heard much better than if one were inside the valley itself. A battery cannon having been fired on the second of February between six and seven o'clock in the morning, the Sound was not heard until three seconds after the fire was set, even though the Northeast wind carried the Sound, and the weather was se-

# Of the nature and properties of Sound.

...rain; the cause of which is attributed to the coarseness of the sea air and the dampness of the powder. Nevertheless, the noise of the same piece was heard on the same day, between one and two o'clock in the afternoon, at the second pulse beat <term>pulse beat: used by 17th-century scientists as a makeshift stopwatch; one beat is roughly equivalent to one second</term> at a distance of two hundred paces. And at two o'clock in the afternoon in clear weather, with the wind carrying the Sound, a falconet <note>a small, light cannon</note> was heard as soon as its smoke was perceived from 1,000 paces away.

The Sound of a cannon piece carrying a twelve-pound ball, firing from one thousand five hundred paces at three o'clock in the afternoon in clear weather aided by the wind, and placed upon a curtain wall <note>a defensive wall between two bastions</note> over the water, was heard at two pulse beats. The Sound of a musket shot fired at fifty paces over the water, with the wind being halfway against it and the weather overcast, was heard at the fourth beat; even though another musket shot fired from 1,000 paces downwind, in dark and overcast weather an hour before daybreak near the sea, was not heard at all; this occurred at the same time for two shots fired at La Rochelle and Ladon, from which the observer was 1,200 paces away.

Now, a musket shot fired at one hundred paces is ordinarily heard in two pulse beats, provided that the powder and the primer catch well.

The Sound of the cannon which was atop the **Chain Tower** <note>La Tour de la Chaîne, one of the famous medieval towers guarding the harbor of La Rochelle</note> was only heard at 2,000 paces in a valley after the eighth pulse beat at two o'clock in the afternoon, in clear weather.

From 3,500 paces, with a light wind bringing the Sound, at three and four o'clock in the afternoon, three or four pieces—both small and large—were only heard after ten pulse beats, which make up nearly the sixth part of a minute.

I will provide several other experiments with the cannon when I speak of the force of Sound and the speed of the movement made by cannonballs; for it suffices to note here the great variety in the speed of Sound. Experiments for this are very difficult to verify, especially since one cannot perceive the fire in broad daylight—which serves as a guide at night—and the smoke that one notices is not perceived as quickly as the flame. As for the night, the air is differently disposed than by day; that is why one cannot conclude the speed of Sound made during the day by that of Sound made at night. However, one could use another signal for the day: for example, one could raise a piece of scarlet cloth, or some other brilliant color that can be seen from far away.

But an objection can be made here against the definition I gave of Sound in the first and second Propositions: namely, that if it is only a movement of the air, it should only be heard when said movement reaches the ear. There is no appearance that it is faster than the initial movement of the bodies that produce it by their striking; and yet, Sound travels much faster than said bodies. This is demonstrated by the movement of a **Lute string**, whose vibrations do not cover a space of more than one or two feet from the beginning until it comes to rest; and yet, one hears the Sound from more than a hundred paces as soon as it is touched. From this it seems necessary to conclude that this Sound, which travels so quickly, cannot be the movement of the air made by the striking of the string—which has no other speed than that of the string—since they begin, continue, and cease together.

To which one can first respond that those who posit *intentional species* <note>original: "especes intentionnelles," a Scholastic philosophical theory that objects send out "forms" or "images" of themselves through the air to the senses</note> of Sound, or who believe it is a quality of the third...

sixth species; <note>In 17th-century natural philosophy, "species" referred to the physical forms or images that traveled from an object to the senses.</note> have the same difficulty to resolve, insofar as these species accompany and suppose the movement of the air, and consequently they cannot travel faster than this movement. Secondly, that the air, being very easy to move because of its fluidity and its lack of resistance, moves much faster than the bodies that give it motion.

Now, one can observe the speed of the movement of air by the movement of arquebus bullets, <note>An arquebus was an early type of portable long gun.</note> cannonballs, pall-mall balls, <note>Pall-mall was a popular 17th-century game, a precursor to croquet, played with mallets and wooden balls.</note> and several other bodies which are violently pushed through the air, and which go as fast as, or faster than, the movement of air caused by gunpowder or a mallet. For if the ball flying through the air arrives as quickly at someone five hundred paces away as the Sound made by the mallet, one can say that the Sound travels as fast as the ball. And if the arquebus bullet goes faster, as one concludes when seeing birds fall dead from the branches of trees before the noise or Sound of the shot is heard—even though the ear is close to the said arquebus—one can observe how much slower the movement of the air, caused by the explosion of the powder, is than that of the bullet. It would further be necessary to examine if the movement of the powder or the mallet is as fast as that of the bullet or the ball; and assuming that the striker lets go of the mallet, which keeps for some time the same motion given to it when striking, whether it would go as fast as the ball. This can also be applied to the arm and the hand that throws a stone or some other body into the air; for since these bodies have no other motion than that which they receive from the percussion, it is (it seems) necessary that the mallet and the arm move at least as fast as the missiles. Consequently, if the mallet left the arm, or if the arm left the body, both would move for some time through the air as fast as the ball or the stone. But this difficulty will be explained in the 13th Proposition.

### PROPOSITION IX.

->*Sound does not depend as much on the bodies by which it is produced, as light does on the luminous body.*<-

It is very easy to prove this by experience, for even though the bodies that produce the Sound no longer tremble at all, and they remain immobile, those who are so far away that the said bodies stop moving before they hear the Sound they have made, nonetheless hear the Sound carried in the air while the bodies that made it remain motionless. And though the woodcutter may rest, one nevertheless hears the blow with which he struck the tree or the wood, because the air that was shaken does not stop as soon as the blow. It must, however, be noted that Sound lasts for barely a moment when bodies remain immobile, as is experienced with musical instruments; for as soon as one touches and stops the string of the Lute or the Viols <note>The Viol (or Viola da Gamba) was a popular bowed string instrument of the Renaissance and Baroque periods.</note> with the finger, one no longer hears the Sound. This is because the shaken air only strikes the ear in passing without stopping, and the ear cannot easily notice its properties and circumstances unless it is struck several times, as I shall prove elsewhere.

As for luminous bodies, their light vanishes and is lost as soon as they...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sensory perception, moving from the speed of projectiles and sound to a comparison between the persistence of light and sound, including how these physical processes relate to the nervous system and divine order.</meta>

are withdrawn or extinguished: so that no light remains either near or far, even though it seems that one sees some remnant of light after having looked at the Sun, for the reason that the optic nerve which has been affected does not lose in an instant the disposition and the alteration it has received. Wherein it must be remarked that we would always see light, or other objects, if the same alteration of the said nerve remained always in the same state; which would similarly happen to the ear, whose internal noises—which some call *<term>tintoins</term>* <note>original: "tintoins"—an early term for tinnitus or ringing in the ears.</note>—move and alter it in the same way that external noises, which are in unison with the internal ones, would alter it. This must be carefully noted in order to explain the manner in which **Demons** can represent all sorts of objects to us both by day and by night, even though there is nothing of everything that is seen; this is what is called *charming*, <note>In the 17th century, "charming" or enchantment was often explained physically as the manipulation of the humors or the nervous system to produce hallucinations.</note> for it is only necessary to alter the nerve, which is the principal organ of the external senses, in the same way that it would be altered by light or by other objects: which is very easy if it is only a matter of rarefying or condensing it: but I shall explain this more amply in another place.

Now the reason why Sound remains longer in the air than light after their causes are removed is not too easy to explain, especially since we do not know if light or illumination is created by a movement of air, like Sound, because we cannot observe this movement due to its speed, for the reason that we do not have a sense subtle enough to judge this movement. One can nevertheless say that it cannot subsist in the air without the presence of the luminous body, because it is entirely attached to it as weight is attached to stones; but Sound does not depend on the bodies from which it was made, because it does not serve them as a property, for its own subject—namely, the air—is of a different nature, and moves long after the rest of the bodies by which it was moved and struck.

Certainly, if the air can only be illuminated insofar as it is rarefied, and if rarefaction cannot happen without local movement, one can conclude that illumination or light is a species of movement; but the more particular consideration of this subject belongs to **Optics**, in which it must be seen if light is the soul of the air and of other diaphanous <note>Transparent or translucent.</note> bodies, and if it can be called the universal soul of the world, which is in some way similar to death when it is deprived of the said light.

To which I add that if one takes the air for the body that produces Sound, then Sound depends as much on this body as light depends on the Sun, since it is nothing other than the movement of the air, and movement cannot exist without the mobile object of which it is the movement.

### COROLLARY.

If all the things of the world must serve us as steps to raise ourselves to God, the dependence that light has on the luminous body, and that which Sound has on the air, must not hold the lowest rank; since these two qualities remind us that we depend more on God than they do on their causes or their subject, and that we have the same obligation to illuminate and teach those who have need of our help, and to serve as...

### PROPOSITION X.

->*Explain in what way Sound is more subtle than light, and if it reflects.*<-

It is easy to prove that Sound is more subtle <note>In 17th-century science, "subtle" (subtile) refers to the ability of a substance or force to permeate and pass through the tiny pores of solid matter.</note> than light, since it passes through opaque bodies; for one hears the Sound that is enclosed within vessels of earth, lead, iron, wood, and all other sorts of opaque materials, even though the rays of the Sun cannot enter therein, and the light that is enclosed inside cannot get out. From this it follows that a single sheet of paper placed between the eye and the Sun blocks its ray, but it does not block Sound, which passes through walls and penetrates opaque bodies as easily as diaphanous <note>Diaphanous: an early scientific term for transparent or translucent.</note> ones, although both diminish its strength and vehemence. 

But it is difficult to know why light does not pass through opaque bodies as well as Sound does: for it has not yet been demonstrated that the pores and fibers of diaphanous bodies are more aligned with one another than those of opaque ones; and the parts of gold are at least as pure as those of glass. Furthermore, pores do not seem necessary to give passage to light, unless one first grants that light itself is a body <note>A "body" (corps) refers to a material substance. At the time, scientists debated whether light was a physical substance or a quality/movement.</note> that cannot exist with another body in the same place; which is contrary to experience, which shows that all the parts of a crystal or a glass are entirely filled with light that penetrates everything that is perfectly diaphanous, just as oil does when spread upon paper or cloth, leaving no part of it that it does not infect and saturate.

This is perhaps what gave cause for some Philosophers to believe that light is nothing other than a very clear and very subtle oil, which insinuates itself into all the illuminated parts of every body: but they would have to explain why light leaves no stain nor trace after it, as oil does, and why it penetrates only diaphanous bodies, seeing as oil penetrates opaque bodies as easily as diaphanous ones. One can also say that Sound is more universal, because it moves and shakes all sorts of bodies, and it travels as well into dungeons and darkness as into the brightest places: but I shall speak later of the other comparisons between Sound and light.

As for the reflection of Sound, it is perceived in the Echo of Bells, of Voices, and of other Sounds that respond two, three, or four times, and which teach us that Sounds reflect just like light when they meet firm and hard bodies, whether diaphanous or opaque, which resist them, even though the ray of the Sun reflects only off opaque bodies. But it is difficult to explain the true reason for these reflections, and why the...

### COROLLARY.

If one wished to relate all the actions in which the ear is more subtle <note>In this context, "subtle" refers to the ability to detect fine details or penetrate hidden spaces.</note> than the eye, and consequently all the instances where Sound is more subtle than light; it would be necessary to make an enumeration of everything that can be heard and learned in darkness and at night, and of everything that can enter into the mind by means of the ear alone, and consequently it would be necessary to nearly transcribe all the sciences found in books, and in the minds of all the men on earth, provided that one excepts the science of colors and light. But this work includes several other things that serve this subject.

## PROPOSITION XI.

->*Sound represents the size and other qualities of the bodies by which it is produced.*<-

Experience shows the truth of this Proposition, for the gravity <note>original: *gravité* - In early acoustics, "gravity" refers to the depth or low pitch of a sound, the opposite of "acute" or high pitch.</note> of Sounds follows the size of the bodies by which they are produced, as one sees in the thickest or longest strings of Spinets, the Lute, and other instruments, in the largest organ pipes, the biggest Bells, the largest Cannons, and all sorts of bodies. So that one can conclude that bodies are always larger when the noise they make is bigger, hollower, deeper, and duller, as happens with the waves of the sea, which make a louder noise than those of brooks and rivers. This similarly occurs with deep voices which testify to the thickness of the vocal artery <note>term: *artere vocale* - An archaic term for the trachea or windpipe.</note>, or the size of the glottis, as I will show in the book on the Voice.

Sound represents yet other qualities of bodies which make it clearer, or darker and duller: cleaner or more confused; harsher or softer, etc., because it is very difficult to find two bodies whose qualities are perfectly equal, even though they are of the same material and size: from this it follows that Sound can serve to notice the difference between all sorts of bodies, even though the other senses judge them equal, as is experienced with many pistoles <note>A gold coin of the era.</note>, *quarts d'écu* <note>original: *quarts d'eſcu* - A French silver coin worth a quarter of a crown.</note>, and other pieces of money, which are so equal in weight, size, and shape that the eye notices no difference, and yet they have their [distinct] Sounds...

### PROPOSITION XII.

*To determine in what proportion Sounds diminish from the place where they are first made until they cease entirely.*

Since all natural agents produce their effects in the form of a circle or a sphere, and light can serve us as a model for speaking of other natural qualities, it must be concluded that Sound extends equally in all directions, as does a drop of oil poured onto a sheet of paper or cloth, or like the circles made in water into which a stone is thrown; and that Sound diminishes as the spaces increase. Now, the surface area of these spaces is in a squared ratio <note>original: "raison doublée." In early modern geometry, a "double" or "squared" ratio describes how area increases relative to the linear distance.</note> of the distance of the Sound from the bodies by which it was first produced, and consequently Sound diminishes in Geometric proportion, as I demonstrate by this figure, which represents a part of the sphere of activity

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a horizontal cone expanding from point A on the left. The cone intersects two circular cross-sections. The first cross-section, closer to A, is bounded by points B at the top and C at the bottom, with point D at its center on the axis. A point F is marked on the axis between A and D. The second, larger cross-section is bounded by points E at the top and G at the bottom, with point H at its center. Point K is marked on the edge of this larger circle. The diagram illustrates how the area of the section increases as the distance from the source point A increases.</image-desc>

that must be assigned to Sound, in which A represents the place where the Sound begins. A H, which is double A D, shows that the Sound, having come as far as E G, is wider, and consequently weaker than when it is at point B C, since the triangle A E G is four times larger than A B C, insofar as all similar figures are in a squared ratio of their homologous or similar sides. This is why one can say that it is four times weaker at E G than at B C, especially since the cone A E G is eight times larger than the cone A B C, since similar cones are in a cubed ratio <note>original: "raison triplée." This refers to the relationship between the volume of a solid and its linear dimensions; doubling the height of a cone results in eight times the volume (2 to the power of 3).</note> of their bases.

Now, it is very difficult to perform the experiments necessary to know this diminution, because of the different dispositions and changes of the air which prevent certainty. This is why one must here follow reason rather than experiment, as one does when speaking of light. And because it is demonstrated in Optics that light diminishes in proportion...

# Of the nature and properties of Sound

<warning>A large ink stain on the right side of the page obscures portions of the text in the first paragraph.</warning>

...geometric, and that there is no reason which prevents this manner of diminution <note>Diminution refers to the fading or weakening of sound intensity as it travels.</note> from being suited to Sounds, since they extend and diminish as naturally as the aforementioned light, and they act upon the sense of hearing as light acts upon the eye, it is reasonable to conclude that they diminish in **geometric proportion**, that is to say, proportionally across equal spaces. But to understand this diminution, it must be noted that the actions of natural causes can, first, diminish equally over equal distances; this would happen if a fire heated four times more at four paces than at sixteen, and if its heat always diminished by an equal part over an equal distance. Now, this is called *Arithmetic* proportion, insofar as its differences are equal. Secondly, they can diminish unequally over equal distances, as when one says that light is four times weaker at 20 paces from a candle than at 10 paces; or equally over unequal distances, as if said light were only twice as weak at 40 paces as at 10 paces, and though the distances increased in a double ratio, the light only diminished by equal parts. In the third place, they can diminish proportionally through proportional spaces, as would happen to light if it diminished in the same geometric proportion as the intervals; that is to say, if at 20, 40, and 80 paces it became weaker by 20, 40, and 80 <unclear>parts</unclear> than it is at 10 paces. I do not wish to add the fourth manner <unclear>which</unclear> has no regulated proportion: especially since one cannot have knowledge of it, even though it might suit Sounds, owing to all the changes in the air.

Now it is easy to judge to which manner we should relate that which I have given to Sounds, assuming that the air is equal and uniform; for since it follows the ratio of the planes <note>The author is referring to the surface area of the expanding sphere of sound.</note> through which the Sounds pass, and since the distances are in a <term>sub-duplicate ratio</term> <note>A mathematical term meaning a square root ratio; for example, if the area of the sound wave increases fourfold, the distance has doubled.</note> of their planes, their diminution belongs to the first part of the second manner.

If someone had an ear sharp enough to discern by how much the Sound is stronger or weaker at all sorts of distances, it would be easy to choose the true diminution; for if he found it half as strong at 20 paces as at 40, and four times stronger at 10 paces than at 40, the diminution would occur in the same ratio as the spaces increased. This, it seems, cannot happen, because equal causes would not act equally, and the weaker causes would act more powerfully than the stronger ones, as can be demonstrated by these two lines, each of which is divided into three equal parts.

Line A: 8 — 7 — 6 — 5
Line B: 4 — 3 — 2 — 1

For if A represents a Sound whose strength has 8 degrees, and B represents another which has only four degrees of strength—that is to say, it is lesser by half—when A diminishes by one degree in each space, B will similarly diminish by one degree in the same space. # Book One

...apply to the fourth and fifth [intervals], and to all other spaces. But when the diminutions are in a double ratio <note>original: "raison doublée," referring to a 2:1 geometric proportion</note> to the distances, no inconsistency follows; for while Sound A, which has eight degrees of force, diminishes across the three preceding spaces following these numbers 8, 4, 2, Sound B, which has four degrees, diminishes according to these numbers 4, 2, 1. Now, the same must be said of the increase of Sounds, which is similar to composition and multiplication, just as diminution is similar to division.

### COROLLARY.

When it has been assumed in Theology in what proportion grace, merits, and the other virtues of the Righteous increase, it is very easy to make the calculation; for if the grace of those who cooperate with all their power increases in **geometric proportion**, for example in double proportion, one must double the first term (which signifies the first grace) as many times as there are actions. For instance, if the Righteous person cooperates twenty times a day with the grace of God, he will have at the end of the day 243,902,008,171,709,440,000 degrees of grace, since this number is the 20th term of the doubling progression, which one can always double until one has as many terms as actions. <note>The author uses this massive number to illustrate the exponential power of "geometric progression" in spiritual matters, a common teaching tool in 17th-century natural philosophy.</note>

By this, Musicians can know how much they would merit if they never sang or played instruments without dedicating all their actions to the honor and love of God, and consequently how much they multiply their sins when they dedicate them to vanity, or when they do them with bad intention.

### PROPOSITION XIII.

->*To determine whether Sound is faster than the movement of the bodies by which it is produced.*<-

This difficulty has already been proposed at the end of the eighth Proposition and referred to this one, in which we must first bring forward some experiments regarding the bodies that produce Sound, so that we say nothing against the Phenomena and Appearances of Nature. Now, we cannot know the speed of the movement by which Sounds are made more exactly than by considering that of **Lute strings**, or other instruments, since it is perceptible enough to be noticed. For if one stretches a gut string to the **unison** <note>Two sounds at the same pitch.</note> of an open two-foot **Organ pipe**, it is very certain that it makes no more than 150 vibrations <note>original: "retours"</note> in the space of one **second** <note>original: "seconde minute d'heure," literally a second-minute of an hour.</note>, which lasts as long as a heartbeat or pulse—that is to say, the 3,600th part of an hour. Secondly, it is certain that it makes enough noise to be heard from very far away when it is plucked one **line** <term>A line (ligne) is an old French unit of measurement, approximately 2.25mm or 1/12th of an inch.</term> out of its ordinary position. Thirdly, one hears it from at least 100 **Royal feet** <term>The "pied de Roy" (Royal foot) was a pre-metric unit roughly equal to 32.5 cm, slightly longer than a modern English foot.</term>, which are worth 20 **geometrical paces** or 40 common paces, while the pulse beats once. And finally, the distance of its vibrations always decreases from the first, which is one line, until the last, which does not have $\frac{1}{100000000}$ of a line for its diameter, as I shall demonstrate in the book on **Stringed Instruments**. From this it follows that the string does not travel the distance of...

### On the Nature and Properties of Sound.

150 lines <note>A "line" (ligne) was a traditional French unit of length, equal to 1/12 of an inch.</note>, while the pulse <note>The original text uses "poux," a 17th-century spelling of "pouls" (pulse).</note> beats once, and the Sound of the string travels as far as forty paces, each of which is two and a half Royal feet <note>The *pied de Roy* (Royal foot) was the French standard unit of length, approximately 32.5 cm, slightly longer than the modern English foot.</note>, and consequently Sound is faster than the movement of the body by which it is produced, for 150 lines do not make thirteen Royal feet.

And if one removes the space of the diminishing returns from the first until the 150th, one will not find even six feet for all the movements of the said string: now forty paces contain more than three times six paces, which is why one can conclude that Sound goes at least three times faster than the movement of the bodies by which it is produced. But the reason for this greater speed of Sound must be taken from the nature of air, which always moves at the same speed, whatever violence it endures at the beginning; for whether it is struck as strongly as a cannonball and thunder, or as weakly as a Lute string, or the larynx and lips, the Sound it makes always travels at the same speed, as long as the air carrying the Sound remains the same; because air has a certain disposition to move always at an equal speed after it has been struck, like the Lute string, whose vibrations always keep an equal speed, however strong an impression one may bring to the said string, as long as it has the same tension: so that one can call this disposition of the air *tension*, since there is nothing that serves us better to explain the uniformity of its movement: although it seems that this solution contains another great difficulty, namely that the Sounds of all strings should be in <term>unison</term> <note>Unison: when two notes have the exact same pitch.</note> with each other, since they are made by an equal movement of the air, and that Sounds have the same ratio between them as the movements by which they are produced, as I said in the sixth Proposition.

To which I respond that it does not follow that all movements of air are equal in all things, even though they are equal in speed, and that the air which makes or carries the <term>high Sound</term> <note>original: "Son aigu," often translated as acute or high-pitched.</note> is otherwise formed, figured, or moved than that which makes the <term>low Sound</term> <note>original: "Son graue," referring to a deep or grave pitch.</note>, whether the circles of air carrying the high Sound are more frequent and closer to one another, or the small shocks of the air strike the eardrum of the ear more often, as the string that makes the high Sound strikes the air more often than the one that makes the low, although the movements of the latter may be much faster than those of the former, as happens when one compares the beginning of the low Sound with the end of the high, which may be made by a movement a hundred times slower than the low, as I will show elsewhere.

It must therefore be noted that the highness of Sound does not come from the faster movement of the bodies or the air, but from the frequency or speed of the returns or reflections of the said air alone, or of the bodies that strike and divide it. This is perhaps why it is said that the object of Music is the *sonorous number*, because Sound is all the more high as the air is struck more times, and the number of these strikes is nothing other than the low and high, and the ear cannot judge the tone it hears if it has not been struck as many times by the air as it was struck by the string or other bodies, so that one can say that the action of hearing is nothing other than the counting of the strikes of the air, whether the soul counts them without us perceiving it, or whether it feels the number that touches it: for Plato believes that it is a harmonic number—

<summary>This page argues that sound travels significantly faster than the physical vibration of the object producing it and posits that pitch (high vs. low) is determined by the frequency of air vibrations ("sonorous number") rather than the speed of the air itself.</summary>

### PROPOSITION XIV.

*Determining whether Sound passes through transparent and opaque bodies, and how it is aided or hindered by all sorts of bodies.*

I propose this difficulty to explain how Sound passes through wood, stones, metals, and other bodies, after having assumed the experiments which show that the noise of bodies enclosed in other bodies is easily heard. For if one encloses a stone or some other body in a glass vial, or in some vessel made of wood, tin, stone, or other material, and they are sealed so tightly that the air cannot escape, one easily hears the noise made inside. And if one softly strikes the end of a beam sealed into the two walls of a hall, one hears the blow at the other end of the beam, even though the walls enclose the beam and prevent the outside air from entering the hall and carrying the Sound to the other end from the outside.

Now, I stated in the second Proposition that Sound made in enclosed places, such as between four walls with no opening, communicates to the outside through the trembling of the walls, which are so shaken by the internal air that they imprint a similar movement on the external air, which carries the Sound to the ears; and that if the Sound is so weak that it cannot shake the walls or the other bodies containing it, it cannot be heard from the outside. But because many cannot imagine that a man’s voice, or other noises heard through the aforementioned bodies, are powerful enough to shake them, one may add that the communication of internal Sound occurs by means of the air within the <term>pores</term> <note>Small gaps or channels within a solid material.</note> of the wood, metal, walls, and other bodies through which the Sound is heard. Consequently, the internal air of these bodies is often as easy to move as the external air, as is experienced with beams: if one strikes the end so lightly that the Sound cannot be heard in the open air along the length of the beam, it can still be heard at the other end of the said beam if the ear is applied to it, even if it is very long and so enclosed that the external air cannot carry the Sound along its sides. This shows that all its parts have been shaken by the said blow, or that the internal air it contains in its pores receives the movement from the exterior, or that the Sound is carried by <term>intentional species</term> <note>In Scholastic philosophy, "intentional species" (original: *especes intentionnelles*) were the forms or "images" of things that traveled from the object to the senses without transferring matter.</note>, which penetrate all sorts of bodies just as <term>spirits</term> <note>In 17th-century science, "spirits" often referred to extremely subtle, fluid-like matter, rather than supernatural beings.</note> do. But it would be necessary to test whether all types of wood, when struck at the end, carry Sound as easily as one another; and whether stones and metals do the same thing; and finally, by how much some carry it more easily than others. For if the more porous ones carry it further, or make it more audible, even though they are longer and heavier, one must conclude that the air in the pores moves and produces the Sound. And because the internal air constitutes a part of the bodies, and the air of each pore shakes the part of the wood that separates one pore from another (assuming the pores are not continuous, and that they are separated from each other by means of small membranes,

One must nevertheless admit that the bodies situated between the ear and the place where the Sound begins usually greatly obstruct Sounds, as is noted in Churches, whose vaults so confuse and obstruct Sounds that one has difficulty hearing the bells being rung in belfries and towers. Likewise, when one is enclosed in a room surrounded by several houses, as happens in the middle of large Cities, one barely hears the cannon shots fired on the <term>city moats</term> <note>original: "fossez de la ville"; these were the defensive ditches surrounding a fortified town.</note>; which is also experienced when mountains or rocks hide the place where the Sound is made. Now, it would be necessary to test by how much each interposed body obstructs Sound more than another, and if water, being of the same thickness as earth or stones, obstructs it more or less than they do.

As for the help that Sounds receive from bodies, it seems nothing can be determined without conducting several experiments, although one can say in general that all concave bodies increase it, reinforce it, and carry it further, because they prevent the air from dissipating. This is seen in all sorts of <term>ear trumpets</term> <note>original: "cornets"; early acoustic hearing aids designed to funnel sound into the ear.</note> used by the deaf, and in channels and underground places, which greatly increase the voice. But I will speak of all the ways of reinforcing the voice in several other places; therefore I only add here that the reason for this reinforcement of Sound must be taken from the quantity of air shaken and preserved in the cavities of the earth, and of other bodies used to multiply Sounds. However, the cavities must be proportioned to the force given to the Sound from the start, which must be large enough to shake the entire mass of air and overcome all other obstacles; for our Sounds do not make the entire Sphere of air resonate (although it is very difficult, and perhaps impossible, to know if each Sound fills and shakes it) because it is too vast, and they are too weak.

Nevertheless, if one supposes the size of the air and the quantity that a given voice fills—and that voices always shake a quantity of air that is greater as they are stronger—it is easy to conclude what the strength of the voice must be to shake the entire mass of air up to the <term>Firmament</term> <note>In the scientific context of the 1600s, this refers to the expanse of the heavens or the sky.</note>, as I have shown in the 44th Physical question.

## PROPOSITION XV.

->*The perceptible Sphere of Sound is larger as it is stronger and greater: but two or more Sounds are not heard twice or several times as far as one of them.*<-

This Proposition contains two parts, the first of which is easy to prove, since the extension of Sound follows the violence with which it has

...has been produced, but it is difficult to know by how much one Sound is greater and stronger than another. It seems that of two weights falling upon a bell—for example, two hammers striking a clock—the one that weighs twice as much makes a Sound twice as great; but it is easy to be mistaken in this matter, for it may happen that the lighter weight will make a greater Sound if it is better proportioned to the bell than the heavier one, as I will show in the book on <term>Bells</term> <note>The author, likely Marin Mersenne, dedicated an entire section of his work to the acoustics of bells and their mathematical properties.</note>. But since it suffices here to suppose that two or more Sounds can be diminished or increased according to a given <term>ratio</term> <note>original: "raison"; in 17th-century mathematics, "reason" or "raison" was the standard term for a mathematical ratio.</note>, and that the greatness and strength of the Sound follows the quantity of air that is struck, as I have already said; I add that the Sound must be four times as strong to have its sensible sphere double <note>Meaning the sound must be four times as intense at the source to be heard twice as far away.</note>. For since the sphere of light keeps this proportion, and we have nothing more perceptible and better regulated than light in Nature, we can conform the proportion of other things to its own. This is why I conclude that just as one must join four candles of the same size to give as much light as one of the candles when one moves twice as far away from the four as from the one, so similarly one must strike four times as much air at the same time to hear the Sound twice as far away. That is to say, the ratio of the strength of Sounds must be the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>original: "raison doublée"; this is the geometric term for squaring a ratio. If the distance is 2, the ratio of sound required is 2 squared, or 4.</note> of the ratio of the distances. 

For just as it takes four surfaces of flame, each equal to the surface of the flame of one of the candles, to fill the base of a cone double in height with as many rays and light as the base of the <term>sub-double</term> <note>original: "sous-double"; meaning half the height.</note> cone is filled by a single candle; likewise, the strength of the Sound that must fill the base of the double cone must be four times as great as that of the Sound which fills only the base of the sub-double cone, insofar as these two bases are in the duplicate ratio of the height of their cone, as is seen in this figure, in which **A B C** represents the cone illuminated by a single candle. **A E G** is the cone double in height; the line **A D** is the height of the smaller, and **A H** is that of the larger.

Now, since the diameter **B C** of the base of the cone **A B C** is half the diameter of the base of the cone **A E G**, and since the planes or the areas of circles are in the duplicate ratio of their diameters, and since the diameter **E G** is double the diameter **B C**, just as the axis **A H** is double the axis **A D**, it follows that the area of the circle **E K G** is quadruple the area **B F C**. Consequently, four times as many rays of light or Sound are required to fill the base **E K G** as to fill **B F C**. But if the strength of a single ray of Sound diminishes in proportion as it moves away from its source, it is not enough that it be four times stronger at its beginning to make an equal impression from twice as far away: for example, if it diminishes in the same proportion as the space increases, one must conclude that it must be six times stronger at its beginning to be heard as easily from twice as far away; for since

### On the nature and properties of Sound:

distance, it is necessary to put four together, because four equal lights placed together are contained under a surface that is only double the surface of one of the said lights taken separately and individually. For light must be considered as a body, inasmuch as it is never without a body that serves as its vehicle and subject; but because Sounds cannot unite like light, light serves more to show their imperfection or their irregularity than to make one understand their nature and their properties. Although one can say in general that the strength of Sound is in a *duplicate* or *sub-duplicate ratio* <note>In 17th-century mathematics, a "duplicate ratio" refers to the square of a ratio. This is an early observation of the inverse-square law: to double the distance of audibility, the intensity must increase by four (the square of two).</note> of the distances: that is to say, it must be four times stronger to be equally heard from a double distance, and that the same Sound is four times stronger when it is heard from twice as far away.

As for the second part of the Proposition, it supposes that Sounds are made by different bodies in various places; and because they do not unite among themselves as a single, sole, and total cause, and they produce their effects separately, one does not hear them from as much further away as they are greater in number, even though they are all of an equal strength. This does not happen with light; for four candles illuminate the same space more strongly than when they are joined together, inasmuch as they have a greater surface area (as is demonstrated in *Geometry*, since four cubes, each of which is one foot, have much more surface area than the cube that contains all four of them) and they unite their strengths as well as if they were all joined together, which does not happen with Sounds.

Now, one can here report several comparisons used by those who explain the 52nd Problem of the eleventh [section], and the 2nd of the 19th Section of **Aristotle** <note>Referring to the *Problemata*, a collection of questions and answers attributed to Aristotle that was a standard text for studying natural phenomena like sound and music in the Renaissance.</note>, and particularly those of the circles formed in water into which one throws one or more stones: for even though the circles are stronger and appear more at the beginning when one throws several than when one throws only one, they do not extend as much further as the number of stones is greater. And if seven or eight men joined their strengths to throw a stone, it would not go 7 or 8 times further than when it is thrown by a single man, even though each of the others has an equal strength.

From this, it is easy to conclude that the union of forces spoken of in *Mechanics* is different from the union of Voices, since mechanical force increases as much by the union of several distinct forces as if there were only a single force containing them all. It is similarly different from the union made by grains of wheat or sand, which are seen from further away all together than each of them can be seen; for one can see a heap of these grains from two *leagues* <note>A league was a common unit of distance, roughly 3 miles or 4.8 kilometers.</note>, even though one cannot see one of the grains from a hundred *paces*; but one cannot hear the Sounds or the voices of several people from two leagues, even if the voice of each could be heard from a hundred paces, and even if there were as great a multitude of voices assembled as there are grains in the said heap.

It is nonetheless a certain thing that several equal Sounds make more noise and are heard from further away than one of the said Sounds, but it is difficult to know by how much this distance is greater, and to perform the experiments that are necessary to decide this difficulty.

<summary>The author explains the mathematical relationship between sound intensity and distance, arguing that while sound follows an inverse-square logic similar to light, multiple sound sources do not combine their reach as effectively as mechanical forces or the visibility of physical objects.</summary>

### PROPOSITION XVI.

*To determine if Sounds have all kinds of dimensions—namely length, width, and depth—and what the other properties, or Accidents, of Sound are.*

Even though the three dimensions of quantity are found only in bodies, strictly speaking, one can nonetheless observe them in bodily accidents <note>In 17th-century philosophy, an "accident" refers to a property or quality that belongs to a substance but is not essential to its nature, such as the sound produced by a string.</note>, particularly when they follow the said dimensions and when they strike the senses differently when the quantity or the shape of the bodies is different. This happens with Sounds, as I have already remarked, for they are thin and slender when the bodies from which they are produced are thin and subtle; but they are large and massive when the bodies are large and thick, as is experienced with the strings of instruments and with Organ pipes.

Now the first dimension, which consists of a simple length, can only be considered in Sound in two ways: namely, when it lasts for a short or long time, or when it comes from a very small body—for example, from the *chanterelles* <note>The "chanterelle" is the highest-pitched string on a lute or similar stringed instrument.</note> of the Luth, and from the smallest strings of the Spinet. From this, it follows that it penetrates easily, because it is subtle like the edge of a knife and sharp like a needle.

The first way is the foundation of all measures and times used in Music and in Rhetoric, and consequently in the Rhythmic <note>The author refers to "Rythmique," the ancient Greek study of rhythm and meter in poetry and music.</note> of the Ancients, which varies time in a great multitude of ways, as I shall show elsewhere.

As for the width of Sound, it is more difficult to explain, especially since we have no instruments that consist of different widths that are not at the same time accompanied by different depths; nonetheless, one can say that Sound is wide when the body from which it comes is wide, since it follows the conditions of the bodies by which it is produced. Furthermore, a Sound can be called wider when it is stronger, as happens when one sings in the same tone <note>original: "mesme ton" - here meaning the same pitch.</note> once louder than the other; though this difference belongs rather to the strength of the Sound. But one can still find another manner of this width in the thickness of Sounds, which consists in being fuller and more massive in the same tone. This happens when the Treble <note>original: "Dessus"</note> and the Bass sing in unison: for the Sound of the Bass is much more massive and fuller. This always occurs with Bass voices, which cannot make a unison with the Treble or with other parts without being fuller and better supplied. This is similarly observed in strings, where the largest has a wider and fuller Sound than the smallest, even though they are in unison. Now, although one might say that this quality of Sound belongs to depth, since it makes it more massive and corporeal, we nonetheless reserve "depth" to explain the gravity <note>In this context, "gravity" refers to low pitch.</note> of Sound, which consists in the slowness of movement, and which is the reason we say that the voice of a man who sings Bass is hollow, low, and deep, and that he has a "good hollow" to his voice.

This is why one can call Sound *deep, or low, and high, or acute*, by reason of the bodies being large and thick, or small and thin; although one...

one might say that Sound is all the larger, thicker, and more massive as it is more acute <note>Original: *aigu*. In this period, "acute" refers to high-pitched sounds.</note>, if one measures this thickness by the multitude of movements, just as one measures the density of bodies and of light by the multitude of parts and rays; for Sound is all the more acute as it is produced by a greater multitude of movements considered in the same time <note>Mersenne is suggesting that high-pitched sounds are "denser" because they involve more vibrations per second, a precursor to the modern understanding of frequency.</note>.

But we shall speak further of these dimensions in the treatise on the bodies of instruments that produce Sound. Therefore, I come to its other accidents, which are almost as numerous as the external differences of the bodies that produce it, among which there are several properties that have not yet been known.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now, among the qualities of Sound, all of which depend on the manner in which bodies press, rub, and strike the air, those that give names to Sounds—harsh, shrill, rough, sweet, clear, muffled, etc.—are the principal ones after the low and the high. For as for the others that bear the character of the bodies by which they are produced, one cannot establish a science of them, because they are almost infinite in number; for if the surface of one body has even a single pore in its surface that is not in the surface of another body, they will produce different Sounds, even though they are perfectly similar in all other things, inasmuch as the pore in the one causes the body to strike the air differently than if it did not have the said pore. The same must be said of the small cavities or eminences <note>Projections or high points on a surface.</note> found in many bodies, because the effect is always different when the cause brings some difference to its production.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As for the harshness and shrillness of Sounds, it comes from the inequality of the surface of the bodies that strike or divide the air, as happens with the noise made by filing iron or some other metal; for the file breaks the air into as many parts as it has grains and eminences; and when the divided and broken air strikes the spirits of the auditory nerve <note>In 17th-century physiology, "animal spirits" were thought to be a subtle fluid that carried sensory impressions through the nerves to the brain.</note>, it imprints its movement upon them, which gives them as much displeasure as harsh flavors give to the tongue, and as rough, raw, and poorly polished surfaces give to the touch. From this comes the fact that the pronunciation of the words that signify this quality has something unpleasant about it, in order to represent naturally what it signifies, as one perceives in pronouncing (*brute, rude, aspre*, etc.) <note>Original French terms for brute, rough, and harsh.</note> because of the letter R. But I shall speak of pronunciation and the meaning of words in the Book on the Voice, where I shall show if there can be a natural language.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The quality of roughness is difficult to explain in Sounds, and particularly in the Voice, especially since one does not see how the air breaks or divides itself in the <term>larynx</term> <note>The hollow muscular organ forming an air passage to the lungs and holding the vocal cords.</note> and in the <term>glottis</term> <note>The part of the larynx consisting of the vocal cords and the opening between them.</note>, or in the palate and in other parts of the mouth of those who have harsh and rough speech. It seems, nonetheless, that all these qualities that make Sounds unpleasant are nothing other than the deformity of the movements of the air; Sound is sweet when it moves uniformly, and rough, harsh, and shrill when it moves in two or several different ways at the same time. This can be proven by the Sound of two or several flutes, or <term>Organ</term> pipes, which are slightly distant from a <term>unison</term> <note>Two sounds are in unison when they have the same pitch.</note>; for although their Sounds, taken individually and separately, are sweet and agreeable, they are nonetheless rough and disagreeable when they are joined together, because their movements strike [the ear] differently—

Mersenne refers to "animal spirits," which 17th-century physiology imagined as subtle fluids in the nerves that carried sensory information to the brain.</note> are dissipated and torn, or divided against their natural order and inclination.

Harsh sound <note>Original: *L'aigre*. ### PROPOSITION XVII.

->*To determine why one hears better by night than by day: and if one can know how much hot air is more rarefied and lighter than cold air: and by how much it is lighter than water.*<-

It is first necessary to assume the truth of experience, upon which all seem to agree: namely, that one hears sounds and noises made at night better, more distinctly, and from further away than those made by day. However, one ought first to experiment in places far removed from noise, such as deserts, to see if a sound made there would be heard further and more clearly. For the multitude and confusion of different noises made during the day in cities, or in other inhabited places—whether by men, birds, or other animals—prevents one from distinguishing Sounds as easily by day as by night. This provides one of the reasons why one hears more clearly at night: the ear is less attentive to any particular Sound when it is filled with other Sounds. This is something it has in common with the eye, which sees one point of its object more confusedly when it looks at a great multitude of things at the same time. The other reason, reported by Aristotle in the 33rd Problem of the eleventh Section <note>Referencing Aristotle’s *Problemata*, a collection of questions on various scientific and philosophical topics.</note>, is taken from the fact that the eye and other senses are distracted and occupied by their objects while it is day. From this it follows that the ear is not as capable of hearing, because the multitude of spirits that serve it at night are dissipated and distributed to the other senses to serve their actions; for the ear is less fit to perform its functions when it has fewer spirits.

But we must see if—all things being equal on the part of the ear, and the air being no more disturbed by day than by night (as might happen in places four or five leagues <note>A league (lieue) was roughly 4 kilometers or 2.5 miles.</note> away from all kinds of noise)—the night alone is the cause of hearing Sounds more easily through some new disposition of the air. Anaxagoras <note>An ancient Greek Pre-Socratic philosopher.</note> believed, according to Aristotle, that the rays of the Sun make a noise during the day by heating and rarefying the air, and that this noise, filling the ear, prevents it from hearing other Sounds. Now, although this opinion is rejected by many, it nevertheless has some appearance of truth if one assumes that illumination occurs through movement, since Sound can be considered wherever movement is encountered; and because one cannot demonstrate that the Sun's irradiation...

<note>This page begins mid-sentence, continuing the thought from the previous page regarding the movement of light.</note> is made without movement, one cannot consequently prove that it makes no noise in the air. As for the night, the air is deprived of the said rays, and of the noise they can make. And if one adds that the rays are nothing other than small bodies similar to the atoms of *Democritus* and *Epicurus* <note>Ancient Greek philosophers who proposed that the world is made of indivisible particles (atoms) moving through a vacuum.</note>, which fill the air and slip into the small voids they encounter there, one can say that the air is thicker and coarser by day than by night, during which sounds carry more easily because of the void they find there, which serves as their vehicle and the medium through which they reach the hearing.

I consider, nonetheless, that the air is rarer <note>In 17th-century physics, "rare" refers to air that is less dense or expanded, as opposed to "dense" or "condensed."</note> by day than by night, for light and heat rarefy it, and cold restricts and condenses it; and one might say that sound makes a stronger impression in the thick air of the night than in the rare air of the day, just as light makes a more powerful impression in a transparent medium that is denser and whose parts are more pressed together. Now, it is certain that air becomes rarer through heat, as is demonstrated in the **Thermometer**, or calendar glass <note>An early type of thermoscope or weather-glass used to observe temperature changes through the expansion and contraction of air.</note>, in which the air dilates and fills much more space when it is heated than when it is cooled: unless one says that just as much goes out of the glass as seems to be restricted within, or that it enters the rising water, or that it passes between the water and the glass, as happens with bottles full of water that are spilled. But it is easy to prove all these answers false if one examines the experiment of the said **Thermometer** and several others like it. That is why one must conclude that the air is thicker at night than by day, whenever it is warmer by day than by night; for if one compares a warm night with a colder day, the air of that night is rarer than that of the said day. From which it follows that one should hear sounds more distinctly on that day than at night, if the density of the air is the cause of one hearing the sound more clearly. 

But because it is almost impossible to recognize whether the thickness of the air is more suitable than its rarity for aiding sounds, I think the best reason why sounds are heard better at night than by day is that the mind is not as distracted at night as by day, and that it occupies itself more strongly with what it embraces. From this it follows that the pain of the sick is more troublesome and harder to bear at night than by day, because the mind attaches itself only to the consideration of the pain, from which it is not diverted at night by the variety and multitude of objects, unlike the day, which seems much shorter and more bearable because of the visits of friends and the occupation of the other external senses, which pull the mind away from the pain.

The second part of this Proposition contains a very great difficulty, namely, how much air is rarer and lighter than water; which has not yet been known until now. As for condensed and rarefied air, one can say that their weights have the same ratio to each other as their lightnesses, and consequently that when one uses a **Thermometer** in such a way that the air inside a room fills twice as much space as the air outside, this air is twice as dense, since the density of a body is nothing other than when there are many of its parts in a small space, so that it is all the greater the more parts there are in the same place. This being established, I say that one can find how much water is denser and heavier than air, insofar as weight follows density, as we...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on air density from page 31, moving from the theoretical observation of "rare" versus "dense" air to practical experiments for weighing air and comparing its density to other substances like water, iron, and gold.</meta>

# First Book

<note>This page begins mid-sentence, continuing from the observation that density is tied to the amount of matter in a given space.</note> experienced in all kinds of bodies, which are all the heavier as they are more tightly packed within themselves, and as they have more parts in an equal space; for example, gold is twice as dense, fuller, and more compact than iron, and nineteen times more dense than water; from this it follows that it is twice as heavy as the one, and nineteen times as heavy as the other, and consequently that it would take nineteen times as much water, and twice as large a volume of iron as gold, to weigh equally. And if the air that expands in the **Thermometer** <note>Also called a "weather-glass," these early 17th-century devices were open to the atmosphere and reacted to both temperature and barometric pressure.</note> fills twenty parts, each twentieth part will be twenty times lighter than the same air when it is reduced to a space twenty times smaller by condensation. Now, one will find the comparison of two different airs—for example, a cold air and a hot air—if one prepares two large boxes, or cases made of very light wood, one of which can be closed and sealed so precisely that air can neither exit nor enter: and the other remains always open, and both are of the same weight. For when they have been weighed in a cold and condensed air, such as that outside in winter when it freezes, and that air has been enclosed in one of the boxes, if they are brought into a room where the air is two or more times warmer, and consequently more <term>rare</term> <note>In this context, "rare" means less dense or thinned out, the opposite of "condensed."</note>, and they are weighed again, one will find that the one in which the dense and cold air is enclosed will weigh more than the one that is open, whose air is equal in rarity to that of the room.

From this, one will easily conclude how much more the one weighs than the other; for example, if the enclosed air weighs one ounce more than that of the room, and each case contains four cubic feet of air, one can say that the enclosed air weighs two ounces, and that of the room one ounce, assuming the <term>Thermoscope</term> <note>A scientific instrument that shows changes in temperature, a precursor to the modern thermometer but without a standardized scale.</note> demonstrates that the air of the said room is twice as rare, and consequently twice as light as that outside. And then one can compare the weight of these two kinds of air to water, and to all other bodies, whether liquid and soft, or hard; for example, if a cubic foot of water weighs 90 pounds <note>original: "liures"</note>, it will be 720 times heavier than the outside air, and 1440 times heavier than that of the inside; and because gold is to water as 19 to 1, it will be 2736 times heavier than the air of the room.

One can also use another means, namely a large piece of wood, which must be placed in equilibrium in the air of the room; for if a piece of lead is twelve times smaller than the piece of wood, and one weighs both in the outside air which is twice as cold and dense, and consequently heavier, these two weights will no longer be in equilibrium. For the piece of wood being twelve times larger than that of lead, it will press against and displace <note>original: "leuer" (to lift/displace)</note> twelve times more air; and consequently it will be that much lighter in this air than in the other, by the total weight of the air equal in size to the said piece of wood. For example, if it takes four cubic feet of air to equal the wood, and that air weighs one ounce, the said wood will weigh one ounce less outside than it did inside, as **Archimedes** demonstrates in the seventh Proposition of the treatise he wrote on **Solid Bodies** <note>Referring to Archimedes' work *On Floating Bodies* (*De iis quae vehuntur in humido*), specifically the principles of buoyancy.</note> that are weighed in liquid or moist bodies. But I shall speak further of the weight of air and water in several other places.

<header>On the nature and properties of Sound.</header>

## PROPOSITION XVIII.

-> To determine why one hears outside sounds better when one is in a <-
-> room, than one hears those made in the room when one is outside. <-

IT is a certain and well-tested fact that one hears noises made outside much more clearly when one is in a room or enclosed elsewhere—whether the windows are closed or open—than one hears from the outside those noises made within the room, even if the internal noises are much greater and stronger than those outside made in the open air. This is why Aristotle proposes this difficulty as a certain experience in the 37th Problem of the eleventh Section <note>original: "37. Probleme de l'onziesme Section." This refers to Aristotle's *Problems*, a collection of questions on various scientific and philosophical topics.</note>, although there are several details that require new experiments. For example, one ought to test how much greater the noise and the sounds made inside houses must be than those outside to be heard equally, and by how much noises made in rooms parallel to the ground outside are heard more easily than those made in high rooms and other elevated places.

Now, one can say that sound from the outside is heard better from within because the air entering through the windows undergoes several reflections and is reinforced, as if it encountered some place suited to creating an <term>Echo</term> <note>The author suggests the room acts as a resonator or "sound trap" that amplifies the incoming noise.</note> in the air of the room which it moves, and upon which it imprints a greater vibration <note>original: "branle." Literally a "swinging" or "shaking" motion, used here to describe the physical movement of air particles.</note> because it cannot escape from the "prison" that encloses it. This happens even when the windows are closed, though not as notably as when they are open. It must be remarked, however, that outside noises are heard less the further one is from the windows, particularly if one moves off to the side toward the corners of the room.

But when one is outside, noises from inside the house are heard only with difficulty and often with confusion, because the sound from within reflects several times against the walls of the room before exiting, and that which exits in a straight line is small in quantity and has difficulty setting the entire mass of the outside air in motion. Furthermore, those who are outside are most often on a lower plane than that of the room, which prevents the sound from traveling straight to them. One could consider several other reasons for this effect, but because they depend on the different circumstances of the place where the sound is made and heard, everyone will be able to find them by considering the layout of each location.

-> Namely, whether sound is heard better from bottom to top, than from top to bottom. <-

Many maintain that one hears the voice of a <term>Preacher</term> <note>The acoustics of pulpits and churches were a primary concern for 17th-century scientists studying the reach of the human voice.</note> or an Orator better from bottom to top than from top to bottom, when one is equally...
