<header>Book One</header>

...distant, but several experiments would have to be carried out in different places, particularly in churches, some of which are without vaults, while others are vaulted or paneled; and then in open places, as happens when one preaches in the open countryside, in order to see if someone at the top of a tree would hear less than someone on the ground when they are at an equal distance.

As for ordinary churches, it can be said that the vault and several other parts, whether of the pulpit or the walls, reflect the voice downward, which makes it more intelligible. However, because temples can be so arranged that they reflect the voice more upward than downward, and because no one has experimented accurately enough to know if one always hears sounds from a low place better when they originate from above, nothing certain can be concluded in this matter—unless one says that the breath of the majority of the listeners who are below makes the air denser <note>The author uses "groissier," suggesting that the humidity and warmth of a crowd's breath physically thickens the air, which was believed to help "hold" or conduct sound waves.</note>, which retains the voice better or multiplies it; although one might say to the contrary that the air above, being thinner and more purified, is more suited to carrying the voice.

Now, it is easy to know the place from which one hears the voice best, provided that no artifice is used; for he who is on the same plane as the preacher and who stands directly facing his mouth will hear the best of all, assuming he has as good an ear as the others. And if one wishes to judge the different places when they are equally distant, and when they make an equal angle with the straight line that serves as the axis for the voice and the sound, one will hear equally well, provided that <term>reflection</term> <note>The bouncing of sound waves off surfaces like walls or ceilings.</note> does not favor one more than the other.

## PROPOSITION XX.

### *Sounds hinder and interfere with one another when they meet.*

This can be understood in several ways, for a weak and slow sound can meet a strong and rapid sound—as when a man's voice is weak, or when it meets a strong voice, or when a deep voice meets a high-pitched one. Similarly, two or more high or deep, weak or strong voices can meet; now, voices and sounds hinder each other in all these ways, as we experience when two or more people speak at the same time. As for different lights, they help rather than harm each other; for if one places two candles at the two ends of a room or a table, one sees more clearly in the middle of the two than one sees in the same place if one is removed. And if there were a second sun in the West on the horizon when ours begins to rise, we would see more clearly than we do. Nevertheless, the meeting of different lights has something similar to that of sounds; for just as the greater sound prevents us from perceiving the lesser—which it swallows up and suppresses—likewise the greater light withdraws the lesser from us, as we experience when a candle is lit at high noon. This happens similarly to all the objects of the other external senses, which can be so preoccupied and affected by one of their objects that there is no more room for others, as we notice with odors that are sometimes so bad that they...

# Of the Nature and Properties of Sound

...hinder all the good ones; there are likewise good ones that are so strong and excellent that when they have penetrated as far as the sense of smell, it cannot be offended by the bad ones encountered while it enjoys the others.

The eye can similarly be so filled with light, the tongue with flavors, and the sense of touch with cold or heat, that the eye will see nothing else, the tongue will be unable to feel any other flavor, nor the touch any other object: for all the senses are so limited that they cannot pass the boundaries prescribed for them. Now, just as there are smells and flavors that hinder each other more than others, there are also Sounds that interfere with each other more than others; and we can conclude in general that large and vehement Sounds hinder weak and small Sounds more than the latter hinder the former.

But it is more difficult to know if high-pitched <note>original: *aigus*</note> Sounds hinder high ones more than low-pitched <note>original: *graves*</note> ones, and if low ones hinder high ones more than themselves; if unisons and consonant Sounds <note>The author refers to "consonances," or intervals that sound stable and pleasant to the ear.</note> hinder each other less than dissonant ones; if Sounds from different instruments interfere more than those from the same instruments, and by which Sounds the voice is more or less hindered. In my opinion, one can say that unisons hinder each other the least of all, particularly if they are equal in strength and in all other things, because this great conformity causes them to embrace, unite, and maintain one another rather than destroy each other; although one could say they hinder each other more in that they cannot be distinguished from one another, by reason of the perfect union they have together, being similar to two equal lights that mix so perfectly that one cannot discern one from the other. One can say the same of two heats, two smells, or two similar flavors—and even of two friends, if we move into the realm of morality—who are so alike in their actions and their wills, when friendship is very perfect, that they seem almost like the same thing; so that friendship is the cause of equality or identity, unless identity or equality is the cause of friendship. This is observed in Sounds that form a unison and unite together because, being equal and almost the same thing, they preserve and mutually reinforce each other.

One might also say in this manner that all Sounds forming some musical chord hinder each other more than dissonant ones, inasmuch as they blend better together and it is more difficult to discern them from one another than when they are dissonant; for they unite as much as they can and approach the Octave from which they are distant, or the unison, which is similar to friendship—the boundary of all the world's perfections. But if one speaks of the hindrance Sounds receive from each other insofar as they fight and are contrary, the more dissonant they are, the more they offend the ear and the mind <note>original: *l’esprit*</note>, which, being a lover of peace and rest (the causes of all kinds of good), holds the contrariety and conflict of Sounds in horror—unless it is a mind that takes pleasure in disorder and discord, such as the spirit of the damned, which is in eternal disorder and desires all things to be like itself. From this, it is believed that Music and its consonances can only displease an ill-formed and disordered mind, like one that allows itself to be too easily carr—

[exci]te to anger, vengeance, and envy. Now we will see in another place which dissonances are the most disagreeable, or which make the best chords <note>original: *accords*; referring to harmonious combinations of notes</note>. One can now consider whether high-pitched <note>original: *aigus*</note> sounds hinder each other more than they hinder low-pitched <note>original: *graues*</note> ones; in any case, it is certain that a louder and stronger sound prevents another sound from being heard more than otherwise, unless the consonances <note>musical intervals that sound stable and pleasant</note> are the cause of the opposite. For the unison—although its individual sounds are weaker than those of a dissonance—prevents them from being distinguished more than discord prevents the distinction of its own parts, as we have already remarked. 

Now, the reason for these hindrances is found in the difference and opposition, or the uniformity, of the movements <note>original: *mouuemens*; referring to the physical vibrations or pulses of air</note> by which the air is struck, divided, or broken in various ways. This causes the air, when it moves forward from one side to carry the sound, to be hindered by another movement coming against it, which delays or stops it entirely if it is powerful enough. We experience this with contrary winds, where the stronger hinders and strikes down the lesser; for natural causes that are opposed are like enemies waging war, where the stronger overcomes the weaker, which yields and obeys it. From this one can conclude the beautiful order found in all creatures, and the obedience we owe to the Superior Powers.

The same thing is observed in Choirs, where the Divine Office <note>the daily cycle of prayers and psalms sung by the clergy</note> is sung in unison, for the stronger voices cover the weaker ones and prevent them from being heard. Likewise, the sound of drums, large bells, tan-mills <term>tan-mills: industrial mills used to grind oak bark into a powder called 'tan' for tanning leather; these were notoriously loud</term>, or paper mills, and generally all kinds of great noises and violent sounds prevent weaker and smaller sounds from being heard and distinguished.

## PROPOSITION XXI.

->*Sounds, and consequently voices, can serve to measure the earth, and to make known news of what is happening throughout the world in a short time.*<-

This Proposition is very easy to conceive if one considers that Sound is not carried in a moment, but needs time to pass from the place where it is made to the extremity of the sphere of its activity, and that one can know the distance from which it can be heard. We find an example of this in Cleomedes <note>a Greek astronomer from the 2nd century AD</note> in the second book, where he says that the King of Persia had stationed men from Susa to Athens when he was waging war in Greece, so that he could let the Persians know what was happening in his army. These messengers were placed on high grounds and received the voice from one another in such a way that all kinds of news were known within the space of two days, or forty-eight hours. 

I know that other ways can be used to make news known as quickly as by the voice, for torches can serve for that purpose, which was similarly practiced by the Persians, as Aristotle testifies in the book *On the Universe* <note>original: *liure du monde*, referring to the treatise *De Mundo*, historically attributed to Aristotle</note> chapter 6, where he says that Cambyses, Xerxes, and Darius used torches to know everything that happened in Asia, as if they had been present everywhere. One can also make news known very promptly with cannons and arquebuses <note>an early type of long gun or matchlock firearm</note>, not only by their noise, but also

### On the nature and properties of Sound.

serves the same purpose, for the arrow carries a letter a hundred or two hundred

length. Some have made use of pigeons for this purpose, such as Hirtius and 
Brutus, as reported by Pliny in Book 10, Chapter 37; which Dousa explains

> *Of what use is a watchful siege, or citadels,*
> *Or ramparts, when a messenger of the sky*
> *Travels through the pathless stretches of heaven?*
> <note>original Latin: "Quid vigil obsidio, quid arces, / Aut valla prosunt per spatia inuij / Eunte cæli nuntio?" These verses by Janus Dousa refer to the 1574 Siege of Leiden, where carrier pigeons were used to coordinate with relief forces.</note>

He means to say that pigeons were used during the Siege of Leiden to carry
news. It is held that those who went to see performances in the theaters
began these messages made by pigeons, which they carried in their bosoms 
in order to send word to those who remained at home about what was being 
done on the stage. The sailors of Egypt practice the same thing, as do those 
who live between Gaza and Cairo, according to the reports of Belon and 
Boterus. Swallows and crows can also carry letters, as Marrhen, King of Egypt, 
demonstrated according to Aelian, Book 6, *On Animals*, Chapter 7. But I do 
not wish to amuse myself by recounting all the ways one can use dogs, cats, 
other beasts, and birds to carry letters, so that I may return to the sound 
of which we are now speaking. Sound seems to have some advantage over 
other methods—if not in speed and subtlety, then at least because it 
explains thoughts better, particularly when one speaks. The trumpet 
can also serve for speech <note>referring to coded trumpet calls</note>, but 
cannon shots can be heard from much further away; these can be used to 
warn those who are distant of everything happening where one is, as is done 
at sieges, battles, and other enterprises to give the signal and to 
command whatever pleases the leader of the army. I leave aside other 
sounds, such as that of the drum, harquebuses <note>early portable long guns</note>, 
whistles, and bells, which are used in towns in times of war to alert 
the guards to the number of men appearing in the countryside.

Now, I say that with all these sounds, or with whichever one is preferred, 
one can measure distances on the earth; for knowing from what distance 
the trumpet or the bell can be heard, one will know how far away it is. 
One can also moderate, soften, and weaken sounds so that they measure 
whatever distance one wishes. For example, if the sound of the drum is 
heard from a league away, one could strike it so softly that it is only 
heard from a hundred paces. It would be more difficult to weaken the sound 
of the cannon, because it cannot make noise unless there is a certain 
quantity of gunpowder; and if one gives too little breath to trumpets 
and cornets, they will not be able to sound. One can find through 
experience and reason how much the breath or the strike must be 
diminished so that the sound is only heard from a given distance; 
and those who wish to survey <note>original: *toiser*; to measure or survey dimensions</note> 
by sounds could establish an art by means of certain instruments, some 
of which would be heard from six feet, others from 20, 100, 1000, and so on.

Now, to send news by means of sounds, and to know the speed of the voice: 
for example, how many minutes pass from the moment the sound is produced 
until it is heard at a distance of a league, half a league, a hundred paces, 
fifty paces, or some other space, one must perform several ex...

# Book One

experiments, and that the one who speaks or who produces some sound be seen by the one who is far away, and that he make some sign with a stick, or with his hand, or in some other manner at the same time that he produces the sound, so that the one who measures its speed may know how much time has passed since the signal was given, or since the production of the sound, until the sound was heard by the one who is far away. The one who speaks, or who produces the sound, can also observe the time if the other person makes known by some sign the moment at which he begins to hear the sound. But the experiment will be easier and more certain if a third person notes the signs and the time, because the one who speaks and the one who notes the time may be hindered and distracted while speaking or making the sign; furthermore, the more people there are, the more certain the experiment will be, for they can compare their observations and take a proportional time between those who are in disagreement.

The movement or beating of the heart could serve as a measure of time, for breathing is more uncertain than the beating of the <term>pulse<note>original: "poux," an archaic spelling of "pouls." The author suggests using a steady heartbeat as a biological stopwatch.</note>, especially since breathing depends more upon our will. Let us suppose then, for example, that a healthy, well-tempered pulse beats three times before one hears a sound made five hundred paces away; one could afterwards measure a minute of time by the various beats of the pulse, in order to know how far the place where the sound is made must be to be heard within one minute of an hour. For if one has knowledge of this minute and of the distance, one can conclude how much time is needed to make news known throughout the whole world by means of sounds or the voice. I know that different conditions of the air, the winds, and the terrain of the earth can bring a great variety to this. But the difference in this speed is often imperceptible within the space of five hundred paces, although one hears the sound with more or less intensity depending on winds that hinder or help. I suppose now that the pulse beats three times before one hears a sound made at 500 paces, and that there are 66 beats of such a pulse in one minute of an hour; I say then that the pulse beats at least 18 times before one hears the sound of a cannon, an arquebus, a trumpet, a bell, a hammer, thunder, or some other instrument distant by one league from us. Consequently, a sound that was strong enough to be heard across the entire earth could only be heard in the time it would take for the pulse to beat 129,600 times—that is to say, in one entire day and eight hours, 43 minutes, and nearly 42 seconds. From this, one can conclude how many days would be needed to hear a sound from the Antarctic to the Arctic Pole; for since there are 14,000 <note>The author's calculations here regarding the "14,000 diameters" of the earth and the league measurements reflect 17th-century geographical estimates which differ significantly from modern values.</note> diameters of the earth, each of which has 12,291 leagues, it would take fourteen thousand times as much time to hear the sound from one pole to the other as it would to hear it across the entire diameter of the earth. But sound cannot last so long, nor be so loud that it could be heard from so far away, unless God willed to produce such a sound—which He will perhaps do when the Angels sound the Trumpet on the great Day of Judgment to call all those who are dead. Now, it is necessary to add the time that messengers employ to speak together and to communicate the news to one another, and to know how far the posts, or voice stations, are distant from one another; these must only be 500 paces apart, so that there are six in each league.

As for the time the messengers spend speaking to each other, one can take one minute...

We cannot place stations from one pole to the other, nor make a sound loud enough to be heard from 32,074,000 leagues away—which is the distance from the Arctic Pole to the Antarctic; and this sound would not be heard until the pulse had beaten 57,731,200,000 times, which is to say in the space of 144,323 hours. For the number of pulse beats divided by 4,000—which is the number of beats it makes in one hour—gives the aforementioned hours; these, being divided by 24, give 6,013 days and 11/24, which is to say almost half a day. Now, if one divides these 6,013 days by 365, one will have 16 years that would pass before hearing at the Arctic Pole the sound that was made at the Antarctic, and besides that, 173 and a half days that remain after the division. Consequently, one cannot hear a sound travel the entire circuit of the <term>Firmament<note>The sphere of the heavens or the stars; here the author is calculating the time sound would take to travel around the perimeter of the known universe.</note></term> except in the space of 52 years and 18 days—but I shall speak elsewhere of the speed of sound.

### PROPOSITION XXII.

->*One can use the sounds of every musical instrument, and the different*<-
->*movements given to them, to discourse upon all sorts of subjects, and to*<-
->*teach and learn the sciences.*<-

**T**HIS Proposition is excellent, for it teaches the manner of discoursing on all things while playing instruments, even if the one who plays them or hears them played is mute; for one can discourse with another while playing the Organ, the Trumpet, the Viol, the Flute, the Lute, and other instruments, without anyone being able to understand the discourse except the one who knows the secret, which can be practiced in several ways.

In the first place, if the instrumentalist and the listener use a <term>tablature<note>A form of musical notation indicating instrument fingering rather than musical pitches.</note> that contains all the letters of the alphabet: for each sound will express each letter. For example, the three notes or the three voices found in <term>G, re, sol, ut<note>Syllables from the hexachord system (solfège). 'Ut' is the precursor to the modern 'Do'.</note></term> could serve for these three letters R, S, V, etc. And the listener, having his lute or his tablature before his eyes, will clearly see the words that the player forms with the sounds of his instrument, to which he can respond by playing another instrument. But it is easy to speak together without a tablature if one uses the eight or fifteen sounds of a <term>mode<note>A precursor to the modern musical scale system.</note></term>—for example, those of the first mode for the first fifteen letters, and the eight sounds of the second mode for the rest of the letters; or if the twenty sounds of the twenty joints of the <term>harmonic hand<note>original: "main harmonique." A mnemonic device used by medieval and Renaissance musicians to find the notes of the scale on the joints of the hand.</note> express the twenty letters of our alphabet; for one can leave out S, Y, and K, as we shall say elsewhere.

There are a thousand other subtleties and industries that can be found by means of sounds; and two or more persons can so accustom themselves to the sounds of instruments that they will speak familiarly of everything they wish without anyone being able to understand them. One can also express entire words and periods through sounds, for preludes, the sequence of airs and songs, and the arrangement of modes and the perfect system have a resemblance to orations and harangues, particularly when the...

# Book One

A musician creates cadences and passages in an appropriate manner, making use of <term>rhythmics<note>The study of time, meter, and proportion in music and poetry.</note></term> according to the subject he treats. Now, this manner of discourse can be practiced across the whole expanse of sounds—that is to say, across a distance of one hundred, two hundred paces or more—for the sound of a trumpet is heard from much further away. Consequently, sounds can serve as messengers and secret letters when the person to whom one wishes to write is only half a <term>league<note>Approximately 4 kilometers or 2.5 miles.</note></term> or one league away, from where one can hear bells or the trumpet.

One can also make use of the <term>drum<note>original: "Tambour"</note></term>, even though the sound it produces is not capable of harmonic intervals; for the variety of rhythmic movements with which it is customarily beaten can serve as characters. For example, one can use the five beats of the fourth <term>paeonic movement<note>A classical poetic foot; the "fourth" variety typically ends with the long syllable.</note></term>, which is represented by three shorts and one long (o o o -), for the first four letters A, B, C, D; and the first species of the same movement, which is the previous one reversed (- o o o), for the four letters that follow, namely E, F, G, and H. The "dissolved" <term>choriambic<note>A poetic foot consisting of a long, two shorts, and a long syllable.</note></term> movement, or <term>pyrrhic-anapest<note>A combination of two short syllables (pyrrhic) and two shorts followed by a long (anapest).</note></term>, which is composed of four short movements and one long, can express I, K, L, M, N. Some call this the "French" movement, because the French ordinarily use this movement when they beat the drum, as seen here: o o o o -. O, P, Q, R can be expressed by the <term>minor ionic<note>A poetic meter consisting of two short syllables followed by two long syllables.</note></term> movement, where the first two movements are short and the last two are long, as seen here: o o - -. The Swiss use this when they beat the drum. Finally, the choriambic movement, in which the first and last movements are long and the second and third are short, as seen here: - o o -, can complete the alphabet by expressing these last four letters S, T, V, X. One can use these same movements on bells, trumpets, the lute, the viol, the organ, and other instruments, and adapt them to torches and every sort of <term>signal<note>original: "signal"</note></term> that can be perceived by the eyes, the ears, the touch, the imagination, and the reason.

But one can practice the same thing more subtly by expressing everything one wishes—whether in French, Hebrew, Greek, Spanish, Italian, or any other language—with four different sounds or movements, which can be varied in twenty-four ways to serve as twenty-four letters. For the numbers 1, 2, 3, 4, being multiplied by one another, make twenty-four different conjunctions, which are found in the four aforementioned movements and in every <term>quaternary<note>A set of four things.</note></term> of different things. The reason for this is that as many changes occur in each place as there are things proposed, and each thing can be placed as many times in each rank or place as the next lowest number can be changed. From this, it follows that three movements can have six different changes, since two are changed twice; for the third can be placed twice at the beginning, twice in the middle, and twice at the end. And if one adds a fourth movement, it will be found six times in the first place, six times in the second, six times in the third, and six times in the fourth place.

The example of these changes is seen in the <term>diatonic tetrachord<note>A series of four notes following a specific pattern of intervals, here represented by the solfège syllables *ut, re, mi, fa* (modern C, D, E, F).</note></term>, *ut, re, mi, fa*, which can express our twenty-four letters. This can also...

These 24 changes show that one can create twenty-four different melodies with four strings of a <term>spinet<note>A small, keyboard instrument of the harpsichord family.</note></term>, four organ pipes, or any other four sounds, without repeating the same sound twice. The <term>fifth<note>An interval of five scale degrees (e.g., C to G).</note></term> provides one hundred and twenty <note>original: "six vingt"</note> entirely different melodies; the major or minor <term>sixth</term> provides 720; the <term>seventh</term> 5,040; and the <term>octave</term> 40,320. From this, it follows that one can deliver entire <term>orations<note>original: "harangues"</note></term> using only a <term>fourth<note>The interval between the 1st and 4th notes of a scale.</note></term> on the lute, the organ, the bells, the trumpet, etc.; that with an octave one can express all the <term>Chinese characters<note>17th-century Europeans estimated there were roughly 40,000 to 80,000 characters in the Chinese language.</note></term>, provided they do not exceed the number of forty thousand three hundred and twenty; and that he who knew all the species of plants, animals, minerals, and stones could express them and teach all the sciences with all kinds of musical instruments.

Now, one can conclude from this discourse how many different melodies exist within the range of a double, triple, or quadruple octave, and so on to infinity. I will only note that the number of melodies that can be found in fifteen sounds, or in a double octave, is expressed by the following number: 1,307,674,368,000 <note>This massive number is the result of 15! (15 factorial), the number of ways to arrange 15 unique notes.</note>. An even greater number of melodies would be found in the octave if it were permitted to repeat each sound twice. Now, it would take at least twenty hours to pronounce this diversity of melodies that can be made within the span of an octave; for eight sounds can only be sung in the space of time that the pulse beats twice, assuming it beats 66 times in one minute. One can also measure time by breaths, if each breath lasts five pulse beats, as is believed; for assuming this, we breathe thirteen times in one minute, and in one hour 792 times. But I shall speak of all these combinations in the *Book of Songs*.

->*The strength of sounds is multiplied by the various rhythmic movements given to them, and by the quality of the bodies and the strikes by which they are produced.*<-

**T**he first part of this Proposition, which pertains to <term>rhythmics<note>The study of time, meter, and proportion in sound.</note></term>, is very certain; for experience teaches that the sound of the trumpet or of some other instrument animated by an <term>iambic<note>A rhythmic foot consisting of a short beat followed by a long one.</note></term> or <term>anapestic<note>A rhythmic foot consisting of two short beats followed by a long one.</note></term> movement touches our spirits more vividly than when its movement is...

# Book One

<warning>Dark gutter and top left corner obscures margins slightly.</warning>

<term>spondaic<note>A rhythmic foot consisting of two long or stressed syllables.</note></term>. The same thing is observed in the beats of the drum, upon which the <term>pyrrhic-anapest<note>A complex rhythmic movement combining two short beats (pyrrhic) followed by a short-short-long pattern (anapest).</note></term> movement being observed, one sees the French soldiers march; and the Swiss march under the <term>minor ionic<note>A rhythmic pattern consisting of two short syllables followed by two long syllables.</note></term> movement; but we shall speak more amply of Rhythmics and the effects that the different movements of Sounds have in the treatise on rhythms, which are common to all sorts of Sounds, and consequently to the voice and to speech, which must have different movements following the different passions that carry us away, or that we wish to display.

From this it comes that rhythmic movements are called the soul and the force of Sound, just as the various figures of Rhetoric are the soul of the oration; for as the iron or steel that arms the <term>lodestone<note>A naturally magnetized mineral; the author uses "aymant" (magnet).</note></term> multiplies its force and vigor—unless one believes that they merely show their strengths, which they could not express without the presence of the lodestone—in the same way, Rhythmic movement, which is hurried and light, that is to say, which has several brief beats, such as the dissolved <term>Choriambics<note>A metrical foot of four syllables: long, short, short, long. "Dissolved" refers to a variation where the long beats are broken into shorter ones.</note></term> or the pyrrhic-anapests, multiplies the force of Sound so sensibly and so powerfully, that it would be difficult to believe it if one had not experienced it.

The second part is also proven by experience, which shows that a vessel made of good metal, like that from which Bells are made, and that a silver vessel has a Sound more penetrating and more lively than a leaden vessel. Those who play the <term>Spinet<note>A small, plucked keyboard instrument, similar to a harpsichord.</note></term> remark that strings of gold or silver produce a different effect than ordinary ones: and one could experiment with the same thing on Trumpets of gold, silver, steel, and all sorts of metals, or of horns and wood, in order to notice the difference of Sounds in all sorts of Trumpets, Organ pipes, Flutes, and Flageolets.

It would also be necessary to experiment with all species of strings on Lutes, Viols, Lyres, and Harps, and to make these instruments of all sorts of wood, horns, and metals, in order to observe the diversity of Sounds; and if the body of a Drum were of gold or silver, and the skin were that of a Bear, a Tiger, or a Lion, the Sound of the Drum would be different from that of the ordinary.

The third part is further proven by experience, for when one strikes some body gently, the Sound made by the blow does not strike the spirits with such force, and does not excite them so powerfully as when it is greater and more violent: and this violence is sometimes so great, that the Sound causes loss of hearing, deprives the listeners of reason and judgment, disturbs or corrupts the wine in the cellars, causes children to die in their mothers' wombs, and breaks the glass of houses, etc., as one experiences with the noise of thunder, artillery, bells, winds, and storms.

This difficulty is quite easy to resolve, for if one stretches two strings of equal thickness and length, and of the same material, and the length of one is to that of the other as the diameter of the circle is to its circumferen-

# On the nature & properties of Sound.

as the side of a cube is to the side of a double-sized cube, the Sounds of
said strings relate to one another like the lines, and consequently they re-
present the <term>squaring of the circle<note>A famous mathematical problem of constructing a square with the same area as a given circle using only a compass and straightedge; it was eventually proven impossible in 1882.</note></term>, and the <term>doubling of the cube<note>Also known as the Delian problem, this involves finding the edge of a cube that has twice the volume of a given cube.</note></term>. One must con-
clude the same thing regarding all other types of lines and bodies, though 
they be <term>incommensurable<note>Quantities that do not have a common measure, like the side of a square and its diagonal.</note></term> and irrational, which can be represented by 
Sounds of the same proportion: but if one hears these Sounds together, they create
Dissonances which will be all the worse as the strings, or the lines 
that the Sounds represent, are more irrational. From this it follows that the Disso-
nances coming from Sounds made by strings incommensurable in len-
gth are not as bad as those made by strings incom-
mensurable in power, because the latter are more difficult to compre-
hend than the former. Now, it is easy to represent all of Geo-
metry in this manner by means of Sounds, but it is even easier to represent A-
rithmetic, insofar as all numbers are measured by unity, and con-
sequently they are all commensurable.

One can see in the treatise on the Lute, upon which the tone is divided into two semi-
tones, and the Octave into twelve equal semitones <note>This refers to equal temperament, the tuning system most common today, which was highly debated in the 17th century.</note>, by how much the Consonances 
and the Dissonances of this division are different from those that follow the 
harmonic proportion of numbers, which I explain in several places, 
and by how much the Sounds that follow Arithmetic proportion are 
sweeter than those that follow the Geometric.

It is also quite easy to conclude that one can represent everything that is 
in the world, and consequently all the sciences by means of Sounds, for 
since all things consist of weight, number, and measure, and 
Sounds represent these three properties, they can signify whatever 
one wishes, with the exception of Metaphysics, which separates all propo-
sitions from sensible and intellectual matter, and which refines them to 
such a point that they make us envision the sovereign beauty of the being of beings. 
From this it follows that the perfect Musician can invent dictions, and a per-
fect language, which naturally signifies things, and that he can teach 
the sciences without using any language other than that of a Lute, or some 
other instrument, as I shall show more amply in another place.

And if someone had an ear good enough and learned enough, he could dis-
cern and recognize the proportions of all sorts of lines by means 
of Sounds, and consequently he could explain all the propositions 
of Geometry by playing whatever instrument he wished, or by singing, 
provided that he could do with his voice everything that can be done on instru-
ments. But there is no man who has an ear delicate and subtle enough 
for this subject, unless it be the perfect Musician who has not yet appeared.

Since we have spoken here of the squaring of the circle, and the duplica-
tion of the cube, it must be noted that the latter was found by means of o-
ne or two parabolas, and by the hyperbole and the ellipse, which are the three 
principal sections of the cone, and that it can still be found by the circle: 
but the former has not yet been encountered, or at least it has not been 
published; though several have come very close to it, and Molther <note>Gottfried Molther (1591–1650), a mathematician who worked on the squaring of the circle and related problems.</note>

To which he adds that one obtains the <term>squaring of the circle<note>The challenge of constructing a square with the same area as a circle.</note></term> in terms more precise than those of Archimedes, when one takes three times the diameter and the fifth part of the line that <term>subtends<note>A geometric term meaning to extend under or be opposite to an arc.</note></term> a quarter of the circle; for the size of the circumference found by this method is 314,142, which differs from what he believes to be exact by only 17 units. In contrast, the measurement of Archimedes—who places three and 1/7 times the diameter into the circumference—is off by 126 units. ### COROLLARY II.

If the ratio of the vibrations—or the returns of the string—is the same as that of the length of the strings (as we shall show in the following book), it seems that the number of returns of a string equal to the diagonal of a square must be <term>incommensurable<note>Having no common numerical measure; an irrational relationship.</note></term> to the number of returns of a string equal to the side of that same square. Thus, we can provide as many irrational numbers between them as there are incommensurable lines. Consequently, Music can achieve more than Arithmetic and equals itself to Geometry—though one might respond that each vibration or return is a movement, and that none of the movements of these two strings are commensurable and rational. At the very least, there are two that can have no common measure, unless one considers the "power" of these movements like that of lines, saying they are commensurable in power. Now, one may further consult the 34th Question of the *Physicomathematics*, in which I show whether one can establish a new science named <term>Psophology<note>From the Greek "psophos" (noise/sound); a term proposed for the scientific study of sound.</note></term>, as well as several other difficulties I treat in that work, which will serve for the understanding of this Proposition.

### PROPOSITION XXV.

->*To know in what ways Sound is different from light, and in what ways it is similar to it.*<-

We have already shown some of the differences and resemblances between Sound and light. For example: that Sound does not communicate itself in an instant like light (in the eighth Proposition); that it does not depend as much on the bodies by which it is produced as light depends on a luminous body (in the ninth Proposition); and in other propositions, how Sound is more or less subtle, whether it reflects in the air, and if it increases or decreases as light does. So here we need only supply what has been omitted.

I say first that just as light makes the different colors of bodies appear to us according to the different angles of incidence and reflections it makes upon their surfaces, Sounds similarly make the different qualities of bodies appear by means of the movement of the air that touches and strikes their surfaces. Indeed, one can say that colors are nothing other

# On the Nature and Properties of Sound.

...nothing other than the different immersion and reflection of rays, just as **Sounds** are nothing other than the different movements of the air.

2. Light is invisible just like **Sound**, for we see only colored surfaces, which represent as much as they can the Sun or other luminous bodies. This is proven by the glass of polished mirrors, which represent the Sun so perfectly that it is difficult to distinguish the reflection from the true Sun; indeed, if all bodies were equally polished, one would see nothing else but the Sun, no matter where one looked. Now, it is easily proven that light is invisible in itself by the light gathered at the focal points where concave mirrors and lenses of glass or crystal burn. This light cannot be seen at all unless it is reflected by opaque bodies that make it visible. On this point, one may nevertheless consider that the light gathered by a **parabolic mirror** at a single point in the air is not seen because it sends no ray to the eye—which only sees the surface of the Sun when it is placed exactly in that illuminated point. Now, one might just as well say that no <term>accident<note>In the philosophy of this period, an 'accident' is a quality or property (like color, heat, or sound) that cannot exist on its own but must be "sustained" by a physical substance.</note></term> is perceptible any more than light is, except by means of the bodies that sustain these accidents and give them <term>extension<note>The physical space an object occupies; the author is arguing that properties need matter to have size or presence.</note></term>. They can only possess this extension by means of **quantity**, which, if taken away, would cause them to perish or be reduced to a single point—unless the Author of Nature performed a miracle similar to that by which He can place and preserve all bodies in the same place, and reduce the entire world into a single point.

It is in this way that **Sounds** make the movement of the air perceptible, and they allow us to notice several qualities of bodies that we can only know through them. If one considers the nature of light very attentively, one will perhaps find that it is nothing other than a movement of the air which carries with it the image of its first mover—namely, the luminous body—to make it perceptible to the eye under the name and appearance of color or light. Likewise, **Sound** is nothing other than the movement of that same air, which carries with it the qualities of its <term>efficient cause<note>The primary source or agent that sets something in motion; in this case, the object striking the air.</note></term>—namely, the bodies that move it—whose image it makes us apprehend under the name and appearance of **Sound**. And just as one could tell how many pores and rough or polished parts are in the surface of bodies that reflect light if one knew the number of reflected rays and the manner in which each one sinks and immerses itself into the solid mass of the body and reflects back to the eye; in the same way, one could know all the inequalities of the surface of bodies that strike the air if one knew all the properties of the movement of the air that strikes the ear in the form of **Sound**.

3. Just as light cannot be preserved without the actual influence of the luminous body, so too **Sound** cannot be preserved without the movement of the air. For the experiment that **Cesare della Galla** <note>original: "Cesar la Galla." An Italian philosopher and professor (1571–1624) who debated Galileo on the nature of light.</note> reports in his book *On Light*—namely, that **calcined stones** <note>These are likely "Bologna Stones," a type of barium sulfate that became famous for its phosphorescent properties after being heated (calcined).</note> which are of the nature of arsenic and very caustic (which **Galileo** showed him), when exposed to the secondary light of the Sun, conceive a light which they still preserve in the darkness—proves nothing other than that said stones receive a certain alteration and disposition from the Sun's light. This makes them fit to illuminate for a short time, like a burning coal, until they have lost the disposition that made them shine. And perhaps...

<summary>The author argues that light and sound are both invisible movements of air that carry the "images" or qualities of their sources to our senses. He uses the example of phosphorescent stones mentioned by Galileo to argue that light, like sound, cannot exist without a source or a physical medium.</summary>

# Book One

that every body has a similar power to shine if one knew the disposition it requires for that, as happens with rotting oak, agaric <note>A wood-decaying fungus that can exhibit bioluminescence.</note>, glow-worms, sea water, whiting, herring, rays, and cooked cod, and several other fish that shine at night. But it is quite difficult to recognize to what point a proposed body must reach to be rendered luminous. Be that as it may, it is no easier to preserve **Sound** without movement than **light** without the luminous body; and one should place no stock in what some have boasted of being able to lock up a Sound, a song, and a concert in a chest, at the opening of which one hears the same concert that had been made a long time before. One can nonetheless construct instruments that will perform all sorts of concerts at the mere opening of some hole, and at the slightest movement one makes, as I will show in the books on instruments.

4. Just as we do not know the force that rays must have to be perceived by the eye, likewise we do not know how fast or violent the movement of the air must be to make an impression on the ear, and to be apprehended under the quality of **Sound**: for although we experience that this or that movement of bodies makes a sensible Sound, nonetheless we do not notice the smallest movements that create Sound, and we do not know how the air moves. Regarding rays, we experience that very few are needed to see, and that they suffice even when they are very far from luminous bodies, as we notice with those of the **Stars**, from which they are distant sixteen million, thirty thousand leagues <note>The author uses historical units; a league was roughly 2.5 to 3 miles. This calculation reflects the astronomical scales understood in the 17th century.</note> when they enter the eye: which does not prevent them from being very strong still; from which we conclude that the ray is in no way diminished by distance, for if one had a **mirror** good enough and large enough to gather as many rays of a star in the space of a line <note>original: "ligne". A 'line' was a traditional unit of length, approximately 1/12th of an inch (about 2.25mm).</note> as there are rays of the **Sun** in broad daylight over the same space, we would see as clearly at midnight as at midday in that small space. Now, one may here consider that every point of the body that makes the Sound sends out rays of the said sound all around itself, and that it fills the solid sphere of the air it affects, just as every point of a luminous body does: from which it follows that we receive parallel rays from these two accidents, and other rays that are not parallel. As for the former, we only receive them the width of the ear or the eye; but we receive the others from the entire width of the sonorous and luminous bodies; so that these rays serve us much more than the parallel ones, which are so few in number that there is no mirror that can burn, or allow one to read by the reflection of only the parallel rays of the Sun, and if there were only that part of the Sun that enlightened us, and all the rest were hidden, we would never see anything by the force of that single light, which would not appear at all: this is why it is necessary for the glass of a mirror to receive the rays from the other parts of the Sun to make them burn: from this it comes that they never gather into a single point, even if the glass is perfectly **parabolic**, and that they make a small circle: but it is difficult to know how many parts of the Sun must be uncovered to be seen and to burn: be that as it may, I draw a new resemblance between light and Sounds, and say

In the fifth place, that one will hear the Sound just as well from afar as from near, if

### On the nature & properties of Sound.

one gathers as many movements of air by means of a mirror, while said air moves, to make them reflect to the place where the ear will be, just as one sees as clearly by the light of a candle from far as from near, by reason of the reflection of the same mirror, or the refraction of lenses; but we will speak more fully of this resemblance in the twenty-fourth Proposition.

6. Light makes us notice the properties and qualities of bodies more sensibly than **Sound**, which is why it is more useful: from this it follows that it is more difficult to live without light than without **Sound**: although if all movement creates **Sound**, it is not only difficult, but entirely impossible to live without **Sound**, since life cannot subsist without movement, even though it can be preserved without light, as the blind testify, provided that the heat which is necessary for life does not perish. And if light is only a movement of air, one can say that it is different from **Sound** only insofar as it affects the eye and not the ear.

This seems to be what Virgil intended to say in the second book of the *Aeneid*: *Then a clearer fire is heard* <note>original: "Tum clarior ignis auditur"</note>: and in the sixth book, *And dogs were seen to bark through the shadow* <note>original: "Viſaque canes latrare per vmbram"</note>, as if **Sound** and light, and the eye and the ear were but one and the same thing. And it is noted in the 20th chapter of Exodus, verse 18, that the people saw the voice of God and the **Sound** of the Trumpets, although this seeing was done through the ears. Indeed, one can say that one sees a thing better when one reads the description of it, or when an eloquent man speaks of it, than if one saw it with the eyes, as is experienced in reports, and in the descriptions of the entries that Kings make into cities, and those of Cities, ballets, and several other things, of which the sight is often less satisfying than the ear.

From this it follows that one can say in general that the sense which discovers a greater multitude of properties of the bodies proposed, or which discovers the same properties more clearly, deserves the name of "eye," or of "sight," by reason that by "sight" one understands the sense which discovers objects and their properties most clearly: and that the mind, which discovers and understands all sorts of objects and properties, can receive the names of all the senses; as happens when one says that one tastes, touches, sees, and hears the discourse and the reasons of someone.

7. It is difficult to know if the movement that makes light moves the air with more or less violence than that which makes **Sound**, or to put it better, if luminous bodies move it more strongly than sonorous bodies: for although the movement of **Sound** appears stronger to the ear than that of light, of which it is not capable of judging, one can also say that the movement of light appears stronger to the eye than that of **Sounds**. And then one must not only judge the violence of movement by the external agitation, for although the movement that the heat of fire makes in the hand is so violent that it cannot suffer it, and that it can reach such a point that it entirely corrupts it, nevertheless this movement does not appear on the exterior.

Now the movement of light is, it seems, more subtle than that of **Sounds**, and penetrates deeper into the substance of the air, which it fills with a certain liquor similar to a very subtle and clear oil <note>The author refers to the 17th-century concept of the "aether" or a subtle fluid that mediates physical forces like light.</note>, which moves

# Book One

in such a way that it affects the eye and the optic nerve, which begins to discover all external objects as soon as the moved air has introduced itself into its pores to imprint a similar movement on the internal air of the membrane that is called the *aranea* <note>The arachnoid membrane or vascular layer of the eye, which early modern scientists believed played a role in transmitting light to the internal "spirits."</note>.

This also happens to the external air agitated by **Sounds**, for it goes to strike the drum <note>The eardrum or tympanum.</note>, the internal air, and the nerve of the ear to make the hearing a participant in what occurs outside, so that the internal man draws the external to himself, and the microcosm <note>A common Renaissance term for the human being, viewed as a miniature version of the universe (the macrocosm).</note> makes use with pleasure of everything that is in the macrocosm, in order to rise afterward to the knowledge and love of the eternal Creator, who is the end of both one and the other world.

### COROLLARY.

It is easy to find the other agreements and differences between **Sound** and light if one understands what I have said in this Proposition, and in the others: this is why I only add that one can imagine that all creatures are similar to movement, as their perpetual changes and alterations testify: following which one can say that the whole world is but a **Sound**, which serves us as a word and a sermon to make us relate everything in the world to He who gives it movement, and to warn us that we must use it only for His glory, and according to His holy will. I leave aside several other comparisons of light and colors with **Sounds**, consonances, and concerts which I explained in the second book of the treatise on *Universal Harmony* <note>The author refers to his landmark work *Harmonie Universelle* (1636).</note>, in the sixth Proposition of the *Book of Songs*, and in several other places of this work, in order to speak of their reflection.

## PROPOSITION XXVI.

->*To explain how the Echo, or the reflection of Sounds, is produced.*<-

I have already shown in the tenth Proposition that **Sound** is reflected; therefore, it is only necessary here to explain how it reflects, and consequently how the **Echo** is produced: which would be very easy if the reflection of **Sounds** occurred like that of light, which Geometers regulate in Catoptrics <note>The branch of optics dealing with reflection, usually from mirrors.</note> according to the different incidences of the ray that falls on bodies whose planes are straight, concave, and convex: but because air is subject to several foreign movements, which often prevent it from moving in a straight line—which does not seem to happen to light—it is not possible to regulate **Echoes** as infallibly as the reflections of light, although we must use them to explain those of **Sounds**.

For one must always take that which is more constant and better regulated to relate to it that which is more variable, so that the rule and measure may be certain, since one cannot reason as one ought if one has not some assured principle, and some firm and unshakable point upon which the discourse is supported, like the balance on its center, in order to examine by right reason everything that falls under the discourse. I say then, first, that **Sound** reflects according to the angles of incidence <note>The angle at which a sound wave hits a surface, which determines the angle at which it bounces back.</note> that it makes on the bodies which

### Of the nature & of the properties of Sound.

...are reflected; for example, if the **Sound** is produced at point **A**, and it falls upon the wall, or upon the plane **D E** at point **C**, it will reflect to point **B**, because the angle of reflection **E C B** must be equal to the angle of incidence **D C A**, and consequently the **Echo** will be produced at point **B**, and along the entire line **B C**.

<image-desc>A small geometric diagram in the top right margin illustrates reflection. Two lines intersect at point C. A horizontal line is labeled D on the left. Points A and B are located below the horizontal line, and rays from them meet at C and extend upwards towards points H and I.</image-desc>

Secondly, I say that it is not necessary for the surface to be concave or hollow, nor for the reflecting body to be empty or hollow to produce an **Echo**, since all kinds of surfaces can reflect **Sound**; this can be confirmed by the **Echoes** that occur in forests and in woods by the sole reflection made by the leaves, the branches, and the trunks of trees, and by those made by rocks, simple walls, columns, and pillars of stone, wood, or other material. But it is very difficult to understand and explain how the reflection of **Sound** is made by concave surfaces, which gather as much light into a single point, or into a very small space, as falls upon them; for example, if the reflecting surface **B a C** is parabolic, it sends back all the light that falls upon it to the point **e**, so that if it receives 100,000 rays, point **e** contains them all: from which it happens that a body placed at point **e** is burned, or suddenly melts, by reason of the great multitude of light which rarefies the air of said body so much that it cannot subsist, and is constrained to yield by the dissolution of its parts.

<image-desc>A diagram showing a parabolic arc labeled B a C reflecting parallel vertical lines (rays) to a single focal point 'e'. The parallel rays are labeled at the bottom with letters M, N, L, G, H, I, K. The apex of the parabola is 'a', and a point 's' is marked above it. Points 'o, p, q' are marked along the curve of the parabola. Point 'D' is located on the central axis below point 'e'.</image-desc>

Now, although it is very difficult to imagine how all the light that passes through the plane **B C** (even if one supposes it to be as wide as the Heavens) can be gathered into a point, given that there is no point in said surface that is not covered and filled by it, and consequently that said light is continuous without any pores and without any void, and that this gathering at point **e** cannot be done without the penetration of an infinity of rays which condense unto infinity—nevertheless, it seems to me even more difficult to understand how the entire solid mass of air that strikes the mirror **a C B** is reflected at point **e**. For one might say that light is an accident <note>In 17th-century philosophy, an "accident" is a property or quality (like color or light) that depends on a substance to exist, rather than being a physical substance itself.</note>, which is not so determined by location that it cannot occupy and cover sometimes a larger place and sometimes a smaller one: but air is a body <note>A physical substance or matter.</note>, whose different parts cannot naturally penetrate one another: and even if it had an infinity of small empty spaces, it nonetheless cannot be reduced to a point like light. And it has never been experienced that air reflected by a concave body is thicker <note>Meaning more dense.</note> at the point of reflection than in any other place, unless one says that the **Sound** heard at said point testifies to the thickness of the air, just as the heat of light shows that of the rays: to which one can in no way respond, because one would experience this thickness of the air with the hand, for it would be much greater than is necessary to change into water, or to cause the ca- <note>The text likely continues with "canals of the ear" or "cavities."</note>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the physics of Sound, transitioning from the mathematical reflection of waves off parabolic surfaces to a critique of existing theories. The author argues for a geometric understanding of acoustics, comparing it to Optics, while introducing a 1625 case study of echoes observed near the Marne river.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>Book First</header>

...ons, caves, and rocks. That is why I conclude that the **Echo** is not produced in concave places by the reflection of several parts of air into a single point, or into a small space, and that it is very difficult to know how it is made, unless one explains this reflection like that of flat bodies, which happens when the air that strikes the plane returns to the ear by the same path, when it falls perpendicularly on the plane, or by the opposite side, when it strikes it obliquely.

It is similarly difficult to explain how the air retains the same motion from the time it was moved by the bodies that make the **Sound**, until all the returns it makes while reflecting; and whether it is the same air that returns, or another different [air]: which has led several to resolve to posit images, or *intentional species* <note>original: "especes intentionnelles" — In Scholastic philosophy, these were non-material "forms" or images thought to travel from an object to the senses to facilitate perception.</note> of **Sound**, in order to avoid these difficulties and to cut the knot they could not undo. But since they are forced to admit that these [species] follow or accompany the motion of the air, from which they cannot so easily detach themselves that they do not imitate its slowness and other qualities, and since they encounter the same or greater difficulties everywhere, it is not necessary to admit these new diminished and "intentional" beings, although everyone is free to use them in research and in the solution of difficulties. Now, there are too few things known about the **Echo** to make of it a science as certain as **Optics**; and it seems one cannot make portable **Echoes** that reflect **Sound** as regularly as mirrors reflect light—or at least the art of it is not yet invented—which is why it suffices to report some particular observations on this subject.

If someone can make **Echoes** that respond seven, fourteen, or twenty times, as some have been noticed in Italy, in France, and elsewhere, and others where the last responds louder than the first, as has been noticed sometimes; or if one could make those that respond with something other than what was said—for example, responding in Spanish when one speaks in French—or which respond in another tone, for example at the **Octave** higher or lower, or which respond only at night, or at noon, or at certain hours of the day, as some say they have observed; and finally, if someone finds the art of arranging **Sounds** in as many ways as one can arrange light by means of different shapes and the polish one gives to all sorts of bodies (of which I shall speak in the book on the **Voice**, where I will show how one must describe the ellipse, the hyperbola, and the parabola to reflect **Sound** and to assist the voice), he will be able to make a new science of **Sounds**, which will be named, if one wishes, *Echometry* <term>Echometry: from the Greek 'echo' and 'metron' (measure), the science of measuring or calculating the behavior of echoes.</term>, or the measurement of **Sounds**. But I shall speak again of the **Echo** and of reflection after having given here several observations that an excellent mind made on the Marne in the year 1625.

Remembering the promise I made you upon leaving Paris in the month of October in the year 1625, I have set about fulfilling my obligation at any cost. But knowing the fleeting humor and the difficult access of the **Echo**—Nymph of the air, daughter of Juno, Naiad, Dryad, or...

# On the nature and properties of Sound.

**Oread** <note>original: "Orcade," likely a reference to an Oread, a mountain nymph in classical mythology</note>, you will excuse me for not having demanded from her the rent for the woods, meadows, rivers, gardens, houses, and mountains that she occupies. For this bad debtor often left her lodgings, or hid herself to say she was not there. This greatly tormented her creditor for a month, as he never ceased seeking her in the morning, at noon, in the evening, and at night, in fair and foul weather; for he always watched for her, spying for the opportunity to speak with her. This usurious Nymph has informants everywhere, and great correspondences in the woods, riverbeds, marshes, islands, cellars, Churches, bell towers, streets and long stretches of walls, wells, farmyards, manure pits in the middle of farms, wine presses, and courtyards filled with large barrels, canals, aqueducts, underground works, bowers, plaster vaults, ruins, large squares—such as ports and pastures—the arches of gates and bridges, rocks, and the boundaries of hills and high mountains: what I have been able to learn from the **Echo** is as much as a Mariner could do, who seeks a new world with his Compass, whose trembling assures him more than all the sorts of guides he could have.

The manner of investigating the nature of this image of the voice is twofold: namely, by operation and practice, or by speculation and Philosophical Theory. Theory is drawn from the three principles of generation—namely **matter, form, and privation** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, these are the three components required for change or creation</note>—or from the four causes, or the universals, or the **ten Categories** <note>Aristotle’s list of "predicaments" or ways of classifying all things: substance, quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position, state, action, and affection</note>. Practice consists of walks, where two stones struck against one another serve to spare the voice, while noting the reverberations that act as the preparations, the harbingers, and the quartermasters marking the lodging and the dwelling of the **Echo**. And then one uses a geometric plan to trace the shape of the places, using the **Geometric pace of five Royal feet** <note>A "pied de Roy" (King's foot) was approximately 32.5 cm; a geometric pace was a standard unit of measure in early modern surveying</note>. One then follows step by step what one seeks in every part of the **Sphere of activity**, where there is more or less force, until one arrives before the reflecting body, to see what the vocal line is, at what point it begins to act, where it ends, what time is most suitable for the **Echo**, and what the intervals of pronunciation and repetition are—with a watch in hand, or with circular arm rotations, by which one marks the difference between pauses and intervals. But at the end of the day, I recognize that another **Pan** is needed—that is to say, a man more universal than I am in every other sort of knowledge—to catch this fugitive,

> *Who flees to the willows, and wishes to be seen before [she hides].*
> <note>original: "Quæ fugit ad salices, & se cupit ante videri." From Virgil’s Eclogues, III.65</note>

and who is not known otherwise than by pursuing her in her flight and in her den. This is what might make me call her a substance rather than an **accident** <note>In philosophy, a "substance" exists by itself, while an "accident" is a property that can only exist within a substance (like color or shape)</note>, since she is nothing but air that has received the impression of such and such words, which man communicates to her when he pushes from his lungs air animated by articulated syllables.

In effect, the prompt or late coming and going, and the clash of the air broken by a collision of bodies, shows clearly enough that **Sound** is not a simple accident, but a substance. This substance is not always the same in species but in genus, since it does not always return the same **Sound** or the same tone. For it alters and changes it often by reason of the disposition and the shape of the islands, the small arms of rivers, the marsh holes, the scythes, and the grassy fields that disguise the **Sound**, like a mirror that is imbued with...

# Book One

...some color, and which communicates its affection to all those who approach it.

Regarding the quantity and the length of the Echo's vocal line, I find that to clearly hear a two-syllable word, a distance of twenty-five to thirty <term>toises</term> <note>The toise was an old French unit of length, roughly equal to 6.4 feet or 1.9 meters.</note> is required, and the location must not be wide and open, but enclosed by some continuous wall or ditch. I encountered another at one hundred <term>geometrical paces</term> <note>A geometrical pace was a standard measurement in early science, typically equaling 5 feet.</note> which is somewhat weak, and feels exhausted by the length of the path through the brushwood, the hedges, the old ruins, the cottages scattered here and there without any order, the trees, the palisades, the gardens, and the farmyard of the estates; this sound finally ends in a well-situated corner of a building, which has earth behind it up to half its height. It repeats briefly, though distinctly, 4, 5, 6, and seven syllables and more, such as *colintampon*, *abdenago*, *l'amerabaquin*, *parafaragaramus*, and *I sing of arms and the man* <note>original: "arma virumque cano," the famous opening line of Virgil’s Aeneid.</note>. It can be heard from six score <note>120</note> geometrical paces when one climbs onto mounds three to four feet high; otherwise, it is so faint that it becomes mute and plays deaf.

Our Echometry <note>The science of measuring echoes.</note> has an advantage not found elsewhere, not even in optics: namely, that it passes not only through transparent media, but also through all sorts of opaque bodies. This echo is accompanied by many other Echoes, which speak first according to their shorter distance, borrowing nothing from one another. When the voice is directed toward the south, the woods and the house—which is quite resonant—begin the sound; then, depending on the force of the person shouting along that same vocal line, the other echo from behind the house (which comes from the river and the willows) repeats. There is one echo twenty-five toises to the side that says nothing, whatever noise one makes, having no communication whether one stands parallel to it or tries to cross its path. But if one turns the face to the Southwest between the two Echoes, one hears three or four, each repeating according to its range. There are two that repeat all at once, without one being able to distinguish their intervals. I found an Echo sixty geometrical paces along a street, striking a Bell Tower eight toises high, which repeats two to three syllables; it pronounces them distinctly and clearly without much force. And if one strengthens the voice, one picks up another echo which is in a house built in the shape of a bracket <note>original: "en potance," referring to an L-shaped building layout.</note> in front of a farm. There is another in a wine press, with a courtyard and a thatched-roof house, built on three sides near a resonant street, which is sixty-four geometrical paces away and repeats three or four syllables, provided they are pronounced quickly; for the interval between the repetition and the pronunciation is imperceptible.

The Echo consists only of a relationship, since at least two terms are needed for this image of the voice. Another person with more leisure than I could expand upon the parallels between Optics and Echometry to show the affinity and the relationship between them. However, I am content for now to hold to the thought of Averroës <note>A 12th-century philosopher from Islamic Spain whose work on light and sound influenced European science.</note>, who represents the nature of the Echo to us like the circles produced in water by means of a small stone; for one bit of water touches the next, impressing upon it the circular figure, until, having encountered the bank, the circles return toward the place from which they started. Upon this relationship, pushing our Echo further, one may ask if there are reciprocal Echoes, and how they

### Of the Nature and Properties of Sound.

...make, to which (leaving a more extensive experiment to be conducted by another) I respond that I have found some echoes at one hundred and forty geometrical paces, where the sound from bottom to top was stronger than the one from top to bottom. This was true even though, in the first instance, there was a small wood between two dwellings and a courtyard with niches, which greatly aided the Echo from top to bottom. This still leaves me in doubt and prevents me from clearly deciding on the affirmative; for such a thing, I would require an inquiry by a "jury of ten" <note>original: "enqueste par turbes," a historical legal procedure where a group of witnesses (a "turbe") was called to testify to the existence of a common custom or fact.</note> or twenty witnesses on-site, composed of curious persons, to ensure the certainty of my claim.

Regarding quality, there are some echoes very well-directed at fifty paces, while others are faint and weak at 80 and 100 paces, being too distant. There are hoarse ones with a cracked sound, resembling a grieving and moaning man who has been struck on all sides. When laundry is beaten by the river, one hears an Echo from both sides among the islands and the willows; the Echo terminates in an open field toward a marshy stream, above which there is a small hill that lifts the Sound and alters it slightly. Rain, too, sometimes contributes to this change and this disguise of the voice.

The action is no less admirable than all the rest that could be said of the Echo, in which one can examine both the efficient cause and the manner in which it is formed, as well as the effects it can produce. As for the first, we do not doubt that the human voice is the cause of the articulated Echo, after the air from the lungs, having exited, successively imprints upon another air whatever pleases the man, who plays with this Element as well as he does with everything else here below.

From this, we see that from every category one learns what belongs to the Echo. Now, if one considers Dioptrics <note>The study of the refraction of light through lenses.</note> and Catoptrics <note>The study of the reflection of light by mirrors.</note>, one will find a great conformity between our lines of action serving the Echo and both the refracted or broken ray, which passes through bodies, and the consideration of the reflected ray. But to create an Echo, a certain force of voice is required, which, after having searched from side to side, returns from whence it started; if not along the same vocal line, at least within the quarter of the circle where the speaker stands. This is what Aristotle intended to teach in his second book *On the Soul* <note>original: "de l'Ame," referring to Aristotle's treatise *De Anima*.</note>, where he represents the reflecting body like a hollow vessel, which is susceptible to everything, or like a ball, which, being pushed against a solid body, returns to the side from which it was thrown, with as much violence as pleases the one who throws it. It is from this shock and this collision of air that Sound originates, which gave the Indians that "Panic terror" of which Polyaenus <note>A 2nd-century Macedonian author who wrote *Stratagems in War*.</note> speaks in his *Stratagems*.

Pausanias <note>A Greek traveler and geographer of the 2nd century AD.</note> says that the Megarians had given Diana the name of "Guardian" for this very reason: when the Persians were ravaging Greece and their country, they addressed an Echo during a dark night and believed it was the enemy responding in mournful cries. They fiercely attacked a resonant rock, and after launching all the fury of their courage and their spears upon it, they were captured the next day and led away as captives. Others, fleeing to Thebes toward Mardonius <note>A leading Persian military commander during the Persian Wars.</note>, recognized the effects of a deceptive Echo, which, while giving fear to one, gives pleasure to another who knows how to make good use of it—such as for Music, and to make the voice clearly heard without much shouting.

Now, seeing this collision of air, one can say that it endures; which has so strongly...
