# Book One

...pleasing to the Poets, that they have built their concepts of the Echo upon it, calling her the daughter of the air, a fleeting Nymph, wild, wandering, mocking, disguising the voice, disdainful of answering when questioned, plaintive and grieving; this occurs because of the diversity of the impression received in the air. The Echo’s particular affinity consists in better repeating syllables containing the sounds **A** and **O** than those where **E**, **I**, and **U** are found; the reason for this is easily drawn from the different openings of the mouth of the speaker, who pushes out more or less air at one time than another.

The locations contribute greatly to the knowledge of what we seek, such as plaster vaults, the small cabinets at the ends of gardens, bowers, resounding Churches, the arches of great bridges over rivers, the cellars of houses, and niches and replastered walls; woods filled with brushwood, cottages, gardens and palisades, islands filled with willows, meadows, and the tracks of marshes. The ingenious Architect leads and places the Echo in gardens and woods, taking advantage of the benefit that nature presents to him, as the Architect of the Olympic gallery and the seven towers of Byzantium once did. <note>The author likely refers to the "Echo Alley" at Olympia or the legendary sounding towers of Constantinople mentioned by ancient writers like Pliny.</note>

As for the Poets, they speak of the Echo as a Nymph overcome by despair, which caused her to turn into a mountain, complaining that having evaporated her blood through the hardness of heart of a Narcissus, she feels her body harden into Rock and her stomach broaden and arch into a cavern; having nothing left but a voice obedient to the passion of another to bear witness to what she was, and that men would seek and follow her as much as she had fled and chased after them, promising to take revenge upon the waters, upon which she would cast and grind charms that, through their magical accents, would torment her Narcissus and those who had despised her.

What do you think of this poetic discourse? Are we not now at the point of seeing the Echo resounding in stones and upon the water, and of practicing a natural Magic through all the circles we make, and by the comings and goings, the contours and detours, and by so many cries and howls by which she torments our spirit? Hotto and Capugnano, <note>Refers to Jean-Jacques Boissard (latinized as Ianus Iacobus Boissardus) and other antiquarians who documented Roman ruins.</note> antiquarians of Rome, show us a very remarkable one near Saint Sebastian, where one sees the tomb of the Metelli, which consists of a round tower (as most of their Mausoleums were) twenty-four feet thick, and named **Ox Head** <note>original: "Capo di boue"</note>, because of the <term>Zophori</term> <note>friezes or decorative bands on a building, often featuring animal figures.</note>, the festoons, and the representations found there. Lower down is the Circus of Antoninus, which was anciently intended for the exercise of soldiers. In this old tower, slightly secluded, one hears an Echo that repeats a sequence of words eight times, and even an entire verse distinctly, and several times confusedly. One still sees the place in which they sacrificed <term>Hecatombs</term> <note>Ancient Greek or Roman sacrifices involving one hundred oxen.</note>, the resonance of which made the sacrifice seem greater than it was; whether this place was found this way by chance, or if it was chosen for a greater veneration and celebration of sacrifices, or if it was intended for the burial of those of the house of Crassus to immortalize them in some way so that their name might be multiplied for posterity—I leave that judgment aside. It is true that in a private home, the Echo is hardly agreeable, for it allows everything said and done to be heard from far away; it is only in the stairways and...

# On the Nature and Properties of Sound.

...in large halls and places of pleasure, where one might desire them. 
As for Churches, if an echo serves to help a Preacher be heard, it also interrupts and troubles him greatly by cutting off his speech with its reverberation. Dandinus <note>Hercules Dandinus, an Italian legal scholar and commentator who wrote on acoustics.</note> says that he heard one in a country house in the Milanese territory which repeats up to twenty times. Majolus <note>Simon Majolus (1510–1597), author of *Dies Caniculares*, a popular encyclopedia of natural wonders.</note> speaks of one in the hall at Pavia which responds as many times as there are windows in the said hall; but it would be desirable if they had provided a description of them to aid the science of the Echo.

Saint Clement of Alexandria, in the sixth book of his *Stromata* <note>The original French uses "Tapisseries," a literal translation of the Greek title *Stromata*, meaning "Miscellanies" or "Tapestries."</note>, speaking of the miracle God performed with the sounds of Trumpets and with fire when He gave the Law to Moses, and disputing against the incredulous, alleges several prodigies drawn from natural history to show that the Author of Nature is no less powerful than nature itself. He reports that in England there was a mountain open at the top, and below it a great cavern, in which, when the wind blew in, one heard a sound of harmonious bells <note>original: "timbres"</note> by virtue of the vents, folds, and windings of the said cavern. And following this, he relates what is found in the history of the Persians: namely, that there are three mountains in a flat field, situated in such a way that upon approaching the first, one hears only confused voices shouting and brawling; at the second, the noise and din is even stronger and more violent; and at the third, one hears only songs of joy and rejoicing as if they had been victorious. It is thus that the air, according to the diversity of the objects, forms a diversity of wonders, which the human mind admires while seeking their causes so as to admire them no longer. You see, then, that our Echoes delight in mountains, although cellars have their share of them, despite what some say—that they serve only as a vehicle to carry them more easily.

As for the time in which the closest Echoes are formed, it is difficult to gain any knowledge of it, for Music has no hooked notes <note>The author refers to fast musical notes like quavers or semiquavers.</note> fast enough, nor pauses and rests that can measure them. At 120 geometrical paces <term>geometrical paces|A unit of measure usually equaling five feet.</term> I found one that repeated the word in the time of one minute, regulated by a watch <note>original: "monstre"; an archaic term for a pocket watch.</note>. Another time I found the same ratio of pronunciation to the interval of the entire repetition to be from sixteen to twenty: for when sixteen instants are needed to pronounce the word, twenty others are needed for the interval of the entire repetition, until the evening when the air begins to thicken; but when there are fewer trees, houses, and gardens to cross, it returns more quickly, as I have experimented with an Echo of sixty and seventy geometrical paces.

The part of the day most suitable for examining the Echo is the evening at Sunset, between five and six o'clock. In October I find it much better than at any other time, for at noon and at one, two, three, and four o'clock, the heated air is too thin and weak, and cannot receive any impression from the Echo; and if it resounds, it is not as well as if it had its necessary temperament and some degree of density. Nevertheless, at night and during fogs, there is no way to hear it.

After having led our Echo through eight predicaments <term>predicaments|Aristotelian categories used to classify and describe the properties of a subject.</term>, I encounter its local difference and its situation from right to left, in which it does not always respond as clearly as it does along its perpendicular vocal line:

## PROPOSITION XXVII.

->*Determine what the distances and lengths of the vocal line of the Echo are; whether one / can know the place from which it responds, and of what length the said / line must be to produce an Echo of as many syllables as one desires.*<-

If the Sound loses no part of its strength through its reflection, one must divide its 
vocal or sonorous line <term>vocal line: the path traveled by the sound from its source to the listener's ear</term> into two equal parts, one of which begins at the 
place where the Sound is made and ends at the body that reflects it, and the other 
begins at the reflecting body and ends at the ear that receives the Echo. Thus, 
if the Sound is strong enough to be heard from a thousand paces in a straight line, the body 
that makes the Echo can be five hundred paces away. For example, if the entire 
vocal line is from A to H, when the Sound meets the reflecting surface D E 
at point C, it will reflect back to point B; for the angle of incidence 
A C D is equal to the angle of reflection B C E; and the Sound coming from point A 
cannot arrive at point B by a shorter path than by the lines A C and C B.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the physics of sound reflection. A horizontal line is labeled 'D' at the left end and 'E' at the right end, representing a reflecting surface. Two diagonal lines intersect at point 'C' on this surface. One line segment extends from point 'A' (bottom left) to point 'H' (top right), passing through 'C'. A second line segment extends from 'T' (top left) to 'B' (bottom right), also passing through 'C'. Points 'A' and 'B' are located below the horizontal line, while 'T' and 'H' are above it. The diagram visually demonstrates the angles of incidence and reflection described in the text.</image-desc>

Now, several difficulties are encountered here, the solution to which depends on ex-
perience: for example, namely whether the Sound that begins at 
point A travels faster along the line of incidence A C than it re-
turns along the line of reflection C B, and by how much it goes faster 
or slower than the other. 2. How far one must be from the body 
that reflects in order to hear the Echo. Blancan <note>Giuseppe Biancani (1566–1624), a Jesuit mathematician and astronomer who wrote extensively on the mathematics of echoes.</note> has noted that 
one must be twenty-four geometric paces <term>geometric pace: a unit of measure equivalent to five feet</term> away or there-
abouts—that is to say, forty-eight common paces—to hear the smallest 
Echoes, which are called *monosyllables*, because they respond with only a single 
syllable, for the reason that other syllables return too quickly to the ear and are 
confused when they encounter the others. I have nevertheless expe-

# On the nature and properties of Sound.

It has been experienced that an Echo responds with one syllable at twenty-two geometric paces <note>A geometric pace was a traditional unit of measure, typically equal to five feet.</note>, but one can still perform several experiments to shorten this path.

As for Echoes that respond with 2, 3, 4, etc. syllables, they must be 2, 3, or 4 times further away; and consequently, the one that responds with an entire verse,

> I sing of arms and the man, who first from the coasts of Troy, <note>original: "Arma virumque cano Troiæ qui primus ab oris," the famous opening line of Virgil’s *Aeneid*.</note>

or some other similar Latin or French line, which has fifteen syllables, must be three hundred and thirty geometric paces away, if one allows twenty-two paces for each syllable. If one makes portable Echoes using boards, one will be able to mark all these distances more exactly, and at the same time, how many times the voice can make them heard. Blancan <note>Giuseppe Biancani (1566–1624), a Jesuit mathematician and astronomer who wrote extensively on acoustics.</note> does not believe that they can respond twenty times to a word of two syllables, as is said of the Echo of Milan, which is called the *Simonetta* <note>The Villa Simonetta in Milan was a famous architectural curiosity of the Renaissance, renowned for its "multiple echo" that could repeat a sound up to 30 or 40 times.</note>; from which it would follow that it would be composed of twenty different Echoes, and that the first or nearest being twenty-two geometric paces away (that is to say, forty-four common paces), the last would be 880 geometric paces away, or 1760 common paces, which are worth 4400 King's feet <note>The "King’s foot" (*pied de Roy*) was the French standard foot before the metric system, slightly longer than the English foot.</note>, or about a third of a French league: for the league contains 15,000 King’s feet, as I have noted elsewhere.

Nevertheless, it is not necessary for the distances of different Echoes to be so great, as I have remarked at the Echo of Charenton <note>A town near Paris where a famous echo existed among the buildings and bridges.</note>, which responded to me ten or eleven times, even though the columns that seemed to create the Echo were very close to one another. Others say they have made it respond 18, 20, and 26 times. But because there is doubt whether the Echoes were made by the columns alone (even though they responded from both sides, and when one was in the middle of said columns) or by underground places and neighboring houses, it is necessary to make a portable Echo, by means of which one can know what the distance of the reflecting bodies must be to make them repeat whatever number of syllables one desires, or as many times as will be necessary for the satisfaction of the Listeners.

But it is difficult to find the place where the Echo makes the reflected voice appear, and whether the ear hears it in the same place that the eye sees the image of its object: for example, if the Sound produced at A, and which goes to strike C, is heard by the ear at B, as if it were at point I, where the image appears to the eye, as is demonstrated in <term>Catoptrics</term> <note>The branch of physics and optics dealing with the reflection of light, particularly by mirrors.</note>. I see no reason that should prevent us from discussing the location of the image of Sounds, just as we do for that of colors: this is why I conclude that the voice, which we call the Echo, seems to come from twice as far away as the place where the reflection occurs: for example, if the voice is fifty feet away from the reflecting body that it strikes perpendicularly, it will appear to be one hundred feet away, beyond the body reflecting the voice.

And if the voice strikes the reflecting body obliquely, the Echo will appear opposite the line of incidence, as seen in the preceding figure: from this it happens that those who hear the Echo imagine that the Sound is on the side where it is not. One could speak here of all the deceptions that occur by means of the Echo, but it is very easy to notice them when one understands the science of mirrors, which serve to create the Echoes called *mute*, for the reason that there is only a single point from which one can hear them.

...or that they make the reflected voice heard, even if the direct one is so faint that it cannot be heard.

This occurs when one places the ear at the focus of a mirror where the light of the Sun, or of a candle, gathers most; for the Sound made in the place where the candle is set, and which goes to strike the surface of a concave spherical mirror, reflects between the fourth and fifth part of the diameter of the sphere of which the mirror is a segment. If the mirror is Parabolic, it reflects at the fourth part of the <term>Parameter<note>In the geometry of the era, the "parameter" (or *latus rectum*) was a constant used to define the proportions and focus of a conic section like a parabola.</note></term>, or right side, of which I shall speak in the following Proposition, and in the Book of the Voice. There, one will see the manner of making all kinds of reflecting bodies and the technical terms that are necessary for understanding conic sections. That is why it is not necessary to dwell here any longer on the Echo, which can remind us that all parts of our body must be resonant Echoes to sing and eternally repeat the praises of God, whose Temple we are, as the Apostle teaches in the first Epistle to the Corinthians, chapter three.

### COROLLARY I.

One can conclude the speed of Sound through the experiments made with Echoes; for one easily pronounces two syllables one after the other, and their Echo is heard while the pulse beats once—that is, in the time of one <note>original: "seconde minute." In early modern science, the "first minute" was 1/60th of an hour, and the "second minute" was 1/60th of a minute—what we now simply call a second.</note> second. Now, the voice travels ninety-six geometric paces in this span of time, as it goes and returns twice for the vocal line of one syllable, which is about twenty-four geometric paces. Consequently, one can say that Sound travels one hundred geometric paces in one second, and two <term>leagues<note>A league was a common unit of distance, though its precise length varied by region; here it is used to measure the vast speed of sound over time.</note></term> in one minute of an hour, etc., and that it would circle the earth in sixty hours, which is worth two and a half days. But I shall speak more fully and more exactly of this speed in another place.

### COROLLARY II.

One can further compare Sound to light—whether from the Sun, the Stars, or other luminous bodies—which would reflect an infinite number of times if it encountered a <term>vacuum<note>original: "vuide." The author is speculating on the behavior of light and sound at the theoretical edge of the universe.</note></term> beyond the Firmament; that is to say, if there were no more space beyond the Stars through which it could pass; otherwise, it would fade away near the said vacuum. For the Sound produced near the same vacuum would vanish or reflect, and because nothing can be annihilated—since annihilation is as difficult as creation—it follows that Sound and light would reflect back within the same side of the space in which they were produced. However, there is this difference: light would reflect an infinite number of times, whereas the reflections of Sound would soon cease, for the reason that the disturbed air restores itself and resumes its rest as quickly as it can.

Now, Contemplative Theologians may consider whether the soul, once separated from the body, if it did not find God and met only an intellectual vacuum—that is, if it met no other being but itself—would make an infinity of reflections upon itself, like light encountering a vacuum, or if it would cease to know. I leave several other specu-

### COROLLARY III.

Those who undertake to provide the science of the Echo must determine the manner in which all types of reflecting surfaces reflect Sound, particularly regular ones, and consequently demonstrate whether the location of the Voice is perceived at the meeting point where the two main reflected sound rays, which strike the two ears, encounter one another. And for this subject, one must consider whether Sounds maintain equality of angles—both of incidence and reflection with the reflecting plane, and those of inclination with the perpendicular of the point of incidence—and whether there is a "perpendicular of Sound." One must also ask if the plane formed by the sound ray of incidence and that of reflection is the surface of reflection, and if it is perpendicular to the reflecting surface; if the part of the perpendicular of Sound comprised between the straight reflecting surface and the point where it is met by the extended reflected sound ray is equal to the part comprised between the reflecting plane and the place where the Sound is made; or if it is less when the plane is spherical convex, or greater when it is concave spherical, as occurs with the rays of the Sun. Finally, it is necessary to consider in the reflection of Sounds everything that is customarily established for the reflection of light. But the life of a very learned man is not too long to accomplish this science; that is why it is enough to have touched upon it here. To this I add what follows concerning concave and convex reflecting surfaces, so that those who have the convenience to perform the necessary experiments to resolve this difficulty may increase Physics through new knowledge.

### PROPOSITION XXVIII.

### *To explain all the figures suitable for making artificial Echoes, which pertain to Conic sections, and their principal properties.*

ALTHOUGH spherical concaves and Parabolics may serve to make Echoes, as I show in the *Book of the Voice*, in which I explain the manner of describing these two sections or lines, as well as the Hyperbola, their generations, and their uses from the 23rd Proposition to the 32nd; nonetheless, the Elliptical concave is the most suitable of all for this subject. For if one builds a wall at the end of a garden, such as that of the Tuilleries garden <note>The Tuilleries refers to the royal Tuileries Garden in Paris, a famous public space created by Catherine de' Medici in 1564.</note>, which follows the shape of the semi-ellipse G D C B A F H,

But the small parabola of the previous figure, which turns its convex side toward those that pass through, is the most suitable of all, both because it can be more easily attached to the large one, and because fewer sound rays <note>original: "rayons de la voix" (rays of the voice)</note> are lost.

First, it must be noted that these parallel rays can be sent wherever one wishes by means of a straight or flat mirror. Second, curved lines are sufficient to understand everything we have said, even if they are circular rather than parabolic, for it is enough to know how to describe them. Nevertheless, I would advise using this invention for <term>burning mirrors</term> <note>Mirrors designed to concentrate sunlight into a single point to generate intense heat or fire.</note>, or at least for lamps that illuminate to infinity, because it is too difficult to adapt these parabolas for the <term>Echo</term>, for which the <term>Ellipse</term> is much better. One might also find a material that would resist fire by using water placed in the concave part of the small parabola to prevent it from overheating, just as water is placed on the heads of <term>alembics</term> <note>A device used in distilling liquids, where the top is often cooled with water to condense vapors.</note>. This is difficult unless the mirror is very large, which is why the small concave glass of the other figure is more suitable for making telescopes.

I leave aside a thousand inventions that can show objects and give a thousand kinds of different shapes to light, whether one wishes to spread its rays or gather and preserve them with the same strength—for example, the way of making glasses that distance objects and make them appear as small as one likes by means of the <term>hyperbola</term>, etc. It is enough to have touched upon this subject to provide an opening for Architects and Engineers who wish to demonstrate their industry and the subtlety of their art through the different Echoes that can be created in halls, courtyards, gardens, flowerbeds, Churches, and other places.

As for the triangle that the plane <note>In geometry, a "plane" is a flat, two-dimensional surface.</note> produces when it cuts through the vertex <note>The highest point or "tip" of a cone.</note>, it does not appear in this figure.

The second section AD, whose two focal points <note>original: "focus"</note> are at points E and C, represents the ellipse; the third EG is the Parabola, of which we have explained some properties. The fourth, marked by HI, is the Hyperbola, to which the other Hyperbola PQ is opposed, whose two centers are at E and T. Now, among other properties of these sections, those concerning reflection are excellent, and particularly the reflection that occurs from rays falling upon their convex surfaces in such a way that they would pass through the center or the focal point E. For those that fall in this manner upon the circle are reflected just as if they came from its center; those that fall toward one of the centers of the ellipse—for example, toward E—reflect as if they came from center E; those that fall toward the focal point of the parabola E all reflect parallel to one another, from which we derive what I said about parabolic lenses <note>original: "lunettes paraboliques"</note>; and those that fall toward one of the centers of the Hyperbola—for example, rays coming from point G, M, or X, etc., toward E—all reflect toward the second center of the Hyperbola, T.

I leave aside several other things that I explained in the 16th Chapter of the 4th book of *The Truth of the Sciences* <note>original: "la Verité des sciences"</note>, in the 16th chapter of the first part of the first volume, and in the 6th chapter of the second volume of the work *Against the Deists* <note>Deists were thinkers who believed in a creator through reason but rejected organized religion and revelation.</note>, and in the first volume of the *Commentaries on Holy Scripture*; furthermore, one may consult the *Harmonic Dictionary*, where I explain the reason for the names of each Conic section.

## COROLLARY.

When it is said that the mirrors of which I have spoken would burn to infinity, it must be understood to mean up to such a great distance that it would seem infinite to us. For they would cease to burn when they began to lose their perceptible parallelism, due to the fact that rays are not exactly parallel when they fall from the center of the sun onto the surfaces of the mirrors. And one can determine the place where they would cease to burn, or to heat, or to make objects appear the same size; the same must also be said of the refraction glasses <note>Lenses.</note> of which we are about to speak.

## PROPOSITION XXIX.

->*To determine if Sounds break—that is to say, if they undergo refraction like light—when they pass through different media.*<-

THIS difficulty is even greater than the preceding one, especially since the experiments necessary to resolve it are more difficult to perform. Nevertheless, one can make use of air and water, which are the vehicles and common subjects of light and Sound, to discover what must be known on this subject. For if Sound "breaks" <note>Refracts or bends.</note> like light when it is produced in water or in air, it will not be heard at the place where it is produced, but further away or closer, and higher or lower, or from a different side than that where it is made. For example, if the Sound is made in the air at point G, and it reaches the surface of the water AB, the vocal line <note>original: "ligne vocale"; the path or "ray" along which sound travels.</note> GN, which would continue to the point...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how sound travels between different media. Having established the geometric properties of reflection on the previous page, the author now transitions to the concept of refraction—the "breaking" of a sound ray as it passes from a less dense medium (like air) into a more dense medium (like water).</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>Book One</header>

H along the straight line G H, if the medium <note>A "medium" is the substance through which sound travels, such as air, water, or wood.</note> were uniform, it breaks at its point of incidence N toward the perpendicular C D, and goes to point I, forming the angle of refraction H N I and the broken angle I N D. Because the image is found along the continued line of incidence, the sound made at point G will appear to be at point H, whereas it would have appeared at point S if the medium were uniform. Similarly, if the sound were made in the water at point I and was refracted at the surface of the air at point N, upon leaving the water, one would hear it away from the place where it was actually made; for the <term>sonic line<gloss>the path or "ray" along which sound travels</gloss></term> moves as far away from the perpendicular C D when leaving the water to enter the air as it moves toward that same perpendicular when passing from the air into the water, provided we assume it follows the laws of refraction.

I have proposed this so that those who have the opportunity to perform the necessary experiments to resolve this difficulty may know how to proceed. For if one knows the angle of incidence that the sound makes upon the surface of a medium—be it denser or thinner than the one in which it originated—and the refraction it undergoes, it will be easy to determine all the refractions of other inclinations of the <term>vocal line<gloss>the sound ray</gloss></term>, provided they follow the analogy that I explain in the following figure. In this figure, line A B represents the surface of the water, or the common boundary of the air and water. G and E signify the sounds made in the air; I and K show the locations and points where the "broken" sounds go. G N I is the line composed of the line of incidence and the line of the sound’s refraction made at G, just as E K is the line composed of the incidence and refraction from point E. G N C, or H N D, is the angle of incidence made by point G upon the surface of the water A B, just as E N C, or F N D, is the angle of incidence from point E.

It must be noted that there are certain terms necessary for understanding refraction; therefore, I shall explain them using this figure. In it, I assume that line A B represents the surface of the water, or rather the junction or contact point of the air and water where the ray breaks. Thus, the sonic ray G N I has two parts: the part in the air, G N, is called the *ray of incidence*, and the part inside the water, N I, is the *ray of refraction*. Consequently, G N I is the broken ray. The surface A B may be called the *refractor* <note>original: "rompante," literally "the breaking surface."</note>. Point N deserves the name of "incidence and refraction" since it joins both rays together. N M is the refracted ray I N extended to M, just as N S is the incidence ray G N extended to S. The line C R, drawn through the point of incidence and refraction N, is called the *perpendicular* <note>In geometry, a line that meets a surface at a right angle (90 degrees).</note>, just as the line that would fall perpendicularly from point G onto the surface of refraction N B can be called the "perpendicular of the object" (if we assume the sound is made at point G) or the "perpendicular of the hearing" (if the ear is at that point). We also call the plane that passes through the ray of incidence and the perpendicular the *surface of refraction*, because it also passes through the ray of refraction, and everything concerning refraction occurs within it. The angle that the perpendicular C N makes with the ray of incidence G N is called the *angle of inclination*. That which the ray of incidence and refraction make together is called the *angle of refraction*, and that which the perpendicular makes with the ray of refraction is called the *broken angle*.

The rays N H and N I are called *divergent* because they always move away from each other; and because they move closer together as they go toward N, they are

# Of the nature and properties of Sound.

...called convergent, just as they are parallel when they are always equidistant. This being established, it is first certain that a ray of light that falls perpendicularly along C N is not refracted [broken] at all, a conclusion that must also be applied to the <term>sound ray<gloss>referred to in the text as the "vocal ray"</gloss></term>. Secondly, an oblique <term>light ray<gloss>original: *rayon lucide*</gloss></term> falling into a denser medium—for example, from air into water—moves closer to the perpendicular the more oblique it is, just as it moves further away when falling into a rarer [less dense] medium—for example, when it moves from water into air. 

However, it is difficult to know the proportion of the oblique paths of the rays of incidence compared to the proportion of the refractions. For although Maurolycus <note>Francesco Maurolico (1494–1575), an Italian mathematician and astronomer who studied optics.</note> maintains that every refraction has the same ratio to every inclination as the first refraction has to the first given inclination, and although he has proven that the refraction occurring in crystal relates to the inclination (the angle of the ray of incidence with the perpendicular) as three is to eight—which makes the ratio of the <term>eleventh<gloss>original: *Diapason Diatessaron*, a musical interval spanning an octave and a fourth</gloss></term>—from which it would follow that the greatest inclination, being 90 degrees, would produce a refraction of 33 and 3/4 degrees; nevertheless, Kepler <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630), the famed astronomer who also conducted groundbreaking research in optics.</note> has conducted other experiments showing that refractions are not entirely proportional to inclinations, though they are close enough from the first degree of inclination up to 30, and that they increase from 30 up to 90 degrees, which results in a refraction of 48 degrees.

But since one of the most excellent minds of this century <note>This likely refers to René Descartes, whose *Dioptrique* (1637) first published the Law of Sines for refraction.</note> has found the true proportion of refractions to inclinations, I wish here to note the analogy, so that once it is found that experiments correspond to it, all learned men may ask him to provide the reasoning and the science behind it. He has thus found that there is the same ratio from the <term>Sine<gloss>A mathematical value used to calculate angles in right-angled triangles</gloss></term> G O of the angle of incidence G N O, to the Sine P E of the angle of incidence P N E, as there is from the Sine I R of the refracted angle N I R to the Sine K Q of the refracted angle N K Q; he will demonstrate this in his *Dioptrics* whenever he pleases. I only provide here the table of refractions occurring in water, which he calculated assuming that when the incident ray makes an angle of thirty degrees, its angle of refraction is 11 or 12 degrees, after having considered this table.

Horizontal lines (GO, PE, IR, KQ) represent the sines of the angles of incidence and refraction. Other points including H, F, S, L, M, and R are used for geometric construction.</image-desc>

Now, if anyone wishes to establish the <term>Dioptrics of Sounds<gloss>The study of how sound waves bend when passing through different materials, such as air into water, similar to how lenses bend light</gloss></term>, it is necessary to experiment whether the two ears hear the Sound in the ray of refraction at the place where the two rays of the ear meet the perpendicular of the direct sound: whether it is heard as being further away when it occurs in the air and the ear is in the water, or as being closer when it occurs in the water and the ear is in the air; and for this purpose, one must make use of <term>divers<gloss>original: *plongeons*, referring here to people who dive and remain submerged for experimentation</gloss></term> who can remain underwater long enough to consider whether the same Sound they heard outside the water seems more or less strong, clear, and distant when they submerge themselves under the water: for as for the high-pitched sound produced in the air, it changes in the water as I show in the following Proposition. I leave aside a thousand other considerations that are necessary to find the refraction of Sounds, which deserves the work of the most excellent minds.

### COROLLARY.

It is certain that one can make lenses and crystals that will change the rays of the Sun and other luminous bodies, such as a candle, into such lines and at such points as one desires, as we have said of mirrors—that is, which will make them from parallel to convergent, or divergent; and if they wish to join or separate, they will change them into parallels, or join them, or separate them further according to the given ratio: and consequently, one can make lenses that will burn, and which will represent the ob-

But I do not believe that the rays of Sounds are capable of being shaped into these figures <note>The author is skeptical that humans can manipulate "sound rays" into specific shapes or focus them in the same way we use lenses for light.</note> through human industry: for as for the Angels, if they dispose of the vibrations of the air as they please, I do not doubt that they could do the same thing with Sounds as with light.

### PROPOSITION XXX.

->*To determine by how much Sound is lower in water than in air; and whether one can infer from this how much rarer air is than water.*<-

It is certain that an instrument that sounds in the air and in the water produces different Sounds, and that the sound it has in water is lower by a **Major Tenth** <note>In music, a major tenth is an interval encompassing an octave plus a major third (e.g., from C to the E in the next octave).</note> than that which occurs in the air, as all experiments clearly show. When these are performed with a bell, its Sound, having two degrees of gravity <note>original: "grauité"; here meaning depth of pitch.</note> in the air, has five in the water: this happens because of the density, or coarseness, of the water, which resists the movement of the body making the Sound more strongly, or prevents the parts of the bell from vibrating as fast as they do in the air.

And because the high and low pitch of Sound depends on the fast or slow movement of the liquid bodies serving as the vehicle for Sound, one can conclude that the speed of the movement of air is to the speed of the movement of water struck by the same instrument as five is to two. Furthermore, the rarity of air is to that of water as 125 <note>The OCR says 12, but the later calculation of 125 to 8 confirms this is a typo in the original or OCR; it should be 125.</note> to 8, insofar as one finds through experiment with all other bodies that produce Sound that their solidities are in a **tripled ratio** <note>A "tripled ratio" refers to cubing the numbers (x³), a common method in the 17th century to relate linear measurements to volume or "solidity."</note> of their Sounds, as I shall say elsewhere. From this comes the fact that bells that produce an Octave are in an eightfold ratio to one another, because their Sounds follow the ratio of their diameters, which, being tripled <note>cubed</note>, gives the eightfold ratio of their solidities <note>If a bell has twice the diameter, it has eight times the volume (2³=8).</note>. Consequently, the ratio of the Tenth, which is five to two, being tripled, will give that of 125 to 8, which is a little larger than the fifteen-fold ratio of fifteen to one. This is enough to make those of good mind consider whether one can say that water is only fifteen times denser than air, and whether the proportions reported for these two Elements are false—such as that of Aristotle, who makes it ten-fold, or that of others who make it a hundred-fold, and that of Kepler, who makes it 1,533,304,682: such that the air of a room twelve feet in all directions would have no more matter than an eighth part of a cubic inch.

I know that several things can be brought against the Proposition I have explained: for example, that an inch of water, being exhaled, can fill a room of several feet, and that vapors fill the space with a great quantity of air, etc. But one must respond that water, being rarefied, is lighter than the air that constrains it to rise, although it is not as **diaphanous** <term>diaphanous: translucent or allowing light to pass through</term>: for the qualities of transparency do not follow the density of bodies. I leave aside several other objections to bring forward one stronger and more appropriate to this subject than the others—namely, that the Sound of a bell which has five degrees of gravity ought to be less deep in liquids less heavy than water. For example, when the **spirit of wine** <note>original: "esprit du vin"; distilled alcohol or brandy.</note>, which is called **aqua vitae** <note>original: "eau de vie"; literally "water of life."</note>, is lighter and consequently rarer than water, the Sound that the bell makes...

<header>Book One</header>

The same must be said of the Sound made in turpentine oil, and in other liquids lighter or heavier than water: which does not happen, for the Sound remains almost always in **unison**, and rises at most only by a **semitone**: which does not contradict what I have said, because the heaviness of water is perhaps only in a triplicate ratio of sixteen to fifteen <note>original: "raison triplée"; a mathematical term referring to the ratio of the cubes of the numbers</note> to the heaviness of the brandy. Now, it is easy to perform several experiments of Sounds in all sorts of liquids and mediums, which is why I shall speak of it no further. I add only that a bell cannot ring in oil, nor in milk, and that it makes the same Sound in wine as in water, or at least the difference is not perceptible.

## COROLLARY I.

One can perform several other experiments to know the ratio of the density of water to that of air, in order to compare them with the preceding ones: now, it seems they can all be reduced to three methods, namely: that which one uses to weigh the air, in order to judge its density by its weight; the space that it fills; and the resistance it offers both to the rays of luminous and sonorous bodies, and to the movements impressed upon it. As for the manner of weighing it, some believe that its heaviness is to that of water as the weight of bodies weighed in air is to the weight of the same bodies weighed in water: for example, that air is rarer and lighter than water in the same ratio that gold is lighter in water than in air; and because the water which is of an equal volume to the gold is nearly twenty times lighter, and consequently that gold weighs less by a twentieth part in water than in air, it would follow that water would be almost as rare as air.

Now, this method is not good, for besides there being no appearance that water is so rare, it would follow that it would have all sorts of proportions with the rarity of air according to the different bodies one weighs in air and in water, and one would have to conclude that there would be no proportion between the rarity of air and water when a body that weighs in air does not weigh at all in water: but I have explained a better way to weigh the air in the seventeenth Proposition.

The second method considers the proportions of the spaces that air and water fill; for if a cubic inch of water can fill a bladder <note>original: "vessie"; animal bladders were commonly used as flexible containers for gases in early physics</note> of one hundred cubic inches when it is converted into vapors, or into air, it must be said that water is a hundred times denser and heavier than air, following the experiment that Bacon <note>Francis Bacon, the English philosopher</note> says he performed in his *New Organon* <note>original: "nouuel Organe" / *Novum Organum*</note>, page 286, where he remarks that a part of brandy being reduced to vapor fills a bladder a hundred times larger than the said part.

The third method consists in the proportion of the resistances of air and water: now, this resistance is noticed first in the rays of light, which seem to have more difficulty entering water than air; from this, it follows that they refract <note>original: "se rompent"; literally "break," referring to the bending of light</note> more in water. I leave aside for now the Sounds of which I have already spoken, in order to remark upon the other resistance made by air and

But it would be necessary to test by how much faster the shot goes through the air than through the water; and supposing that the ball is carried four hundred paces point-blank <note>The distance a weapon can fire a projectile in a straight line</note> in the air, how many paces it would go in the water. For if it goes a hundred times further in the air, one might say that the air is a hundred times more rare: unless one believes that it is necessary to triple the ratio of these speeds to obtain the difference in densities: for it would then be enough for the ball to go ten times further in the air to say that it is a hundred times more rare than water; although one cannot conclude this density clearly enough by the said resistance, since we see by experience that fish cleave through the water as fast as birds cleave through the air, although there is no appearance that they have ten times as much trouble or strength as the birds.

## COROLLARY II.

If one can judge the proportion of the density of these two elements by the movement of heavy bodies descending through them, I add a very exact observation that can serve to find it: namely, that a musket ball which descends thirteen feet in the air in two units of time, descends in the water in five units of time; for having made a channel two or three inches wide and 13 feet high, the lead ball falls through the air in one second, and through the water in two and a half seconds; so that it could descend 80 feet in the air while it descends twelve feet in the water. But it is difficult to know if one must follow the simple ratios of the times of these falls, or their doubled or tripled ratios, to determine the proportion of the said densities.
When the lead ball is hollowed out in such a way that it weighs three times less than the solid one, they both descend as quickly in the air as the other, but the hollow one descends in the water in five seconds. On this point, it should be noted that experiments can only succeed with spherical bodies: for other shapes hinder them marvelously in the water; for example, a quadruple <note>A gold coin, used here as a flat weight</note> descends only in 12", and a lead plate of the same width in 8". A square parallelogram of China wood <note>original: "bois de la Chine"; likely refers to a dense exotic wood like Lignum Vitae or Rosewood</note> half a foot long and one inch wide descends in 5" and 1/2; and as many shapes as one can imagine speed up or slow down the movement in water quite sensibly.
One can further consider the speed of movements made by bodies descending in water, whether by their weight or in other ways; and similarly those of bodies pushed down to the bottom of the water when they return to its surface, in order to observe if those heavier than water descend, and those lighter rise, by increasing their speed in the same proportion as the movements they have in the air—for example, to know if the elderberry pith <note>The light, spongy tissue from the center of an elderberry stalk, often used in early physics experiments</note> which rises from the bottom of a twelve-foot high channel to the top, having risen the first foot in a given time, rises four feet in two units of time; that is to say, if bodies lighter than water increase their speed in a doubled ratio <note>Referring to the square of the time ($t^2$)</note>, and following the square roots of the times, as happens with the speed of heavy bodies descending in the air.

# Book One

...of which we will speak extensively in the second book on Movements. Whatever the case may be, it is difficult to conclude anything regarding the density of water and air by the descent [of objects] through them, because one encounters as many different proportions as there are weights of different shapes. Shape makes almost no difference in air—for a quadruple weight <note>An object four times the weight of a standard reference</note> falls almost as fast as a gold ball in air—whereas it takes three times longer to fall in water than the aforementioned ball. And stones, which are much lighter than lead, descend as fast in water when they are in the shape of a parallelogram <note>A flat, rectangular shape</note> as a lead ball does. From this, it is easy to conclude that bodies must have the same shape to draw any conjecture from their movements.

### COROLLARY III.

One can imagine several other means to find the proportion of these densities, particularly by the compression of water and air. For if one takes two hollow spheres, or two syringes that are so tightly closed that nothing can escape, and one is full of water and the other of air; if one squeezes the two spheres and other vessels until they burst, one will see how much more <term>condensation</term> <note>The reduction of a substance's volume through pressure</note> the air has endured than the water. For example, if the vessel containing it has been so pressed before breaking that its hollow held a hundred times less space than before it was pressed, and the hollow of the water vessel decreased by only a hundredth part, one would conclude that air is 99 times rarer than water.

### COROLLARY IV.

The experiment conducted in water to determine if Sounds refract <note>original: "se rompent", literally "break"; in the physics of this period, this refers to the bending of waves as they pass from one medium to another</note> like light, or the opposite of light, cannot give us enough certainty to conclude what the case is, especially since a Sound made "between two waters" <note>Meaning completely submerged or surrounded by water</note> appears so weak that it seems one can make no other judgment of it than that of its weakness and its <term>gravity</term> <note>In acoustics, "gravity" refers to a low pitch, the opposite of "aigu" or high pitch</note>.

Now, when I say *between two waters*, I mean that the bodies producing the Sound are so surrounded by water that it touches them on all sides and at every point of their surfaces. For if they sound in air that is beneath the water, they do not change the <term>pitch</term> <note>original: "l'aigu", referring to the highness or sharpness of a sound's tone</note> of their Sound at all, because the ear—whether plunged into the water or free in the air—always hears the same pitch of the Sound produced in the air, whether the air remains connected to the entire mass of other air or is separated from it, as happens when one plunges a vessel full of air into water, wine, oil, milk, or some other liquid, or when one encloses it between four walls. From this, it must be concluded that the pitch does not change at all from its first production, even though the other mediums through which it passes are different; but the ear easily perceives that it is weaker than if it heard it in the same air where it was first produced.

When the ear is submerged in water, and the Sound is similarly produced under water, the ear hears it as weakly as if it were produced in the air, because the medium, whose movement makes the Sound perceived, communicates

#### COROLLARY V.

If one prefers to judge the ratio of the <term>density</term> <note>How thick or compact a substance is</note> of water and air by the strength of the Sounds rather than by their lowness or highness, one must measure this strength to know how much weaker it is under water than in air; for one could say that water is as much denser than air as it diminishes the strength of the Sound. Now, because it is easier to measure highness <note>pitch</note> than strength <note>volume</note>, I have used the former instead; but we will say more on this subject in the books on *Movements*, which will supplement what is lacking in this one.

## PROPOSITION XXXI.

### *Whether a high sound is more pleasing and more excellent than a low one.*

This question can be decided by experience and by reason, but one must take the low and the high from the same genre; that is to say, on the same instrument, or within human voices. For it would be a difficulty if one wished to compare the high voice of a man with the low sound of a <term>Viol</term> <note>A stringed instrument played with a bow, common in the 17th century</note> or a <term>Lute</term> <note>A plucked string instrument with a rounded back</note>.

One can therefore understand this difficulty by comparing the low and high sounds of the same instrument—for example, the Lute, the Viol, the <term>Spinet</term> <note>A smaller, rectangular version of a harpsichord</note>, or one of the Organ stops, or the human voice. The comparison of voices can be made in two ways: namely, the low voice of one who sings the <term>Bass</term> and the high voice of a child, or the low and high voice of the same person. But one must not compare a good voice with a bad one, for the quality of the low voice must be equal to that of the high voice so that the comparison is perfect. We must first compare the voice of the same man to know if he sings more pleasingly low or high, when he has an equal facility for singing both.

For example, I suppose that his voice has the range of an <term>Octave</term> without being forced, and consequently, his middle voice being at *G* <note>original: "G re sol vt"—a name for the note G in the old system of music theory</note>, he can easily rise to high *C* <note>original: "C sol vt fa"</note> or to *D* <note>original: "D la re sol"</note>, and descend to low *C* <note>original: "C fa vt"</note>. The question is whether the low *C* will be more or less pleasing than the high *C*. One can also ask the same thing of the middle *G* compared to the same high *C*, for the former is low in comparison to the latter. Then we will compare the low or middle voices of the <term>Tenor</term> <note>original: "Taille"—the standard mid-range male voice</note> with those of children and <term>Sopranos</term> <note>original: "Dessus"—the highest vocal part</note>.

As for the voices of the same man, it seems that the middle one is the most natural and the most pleasing; after that, those in the high range are more pleasing than those in the low range, which approach silence, especially since they have less hoarseness and are all the more "lively" and "awake" because they have a greater speed in their movements. And this reason proves not only that high voices are more pleasing than low ones, but also more pleasing than middle ones, although...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition XXXI regarding whether high or low sounds are superior. It moves from physical properties to aesthetic and philosophical arguments, citing Aristotle and contemporary singers of the French court.</meta>

->Book One<-

<warning>There is significant ink staining and bleed-through on the left margin, though it does not obscure the text.</warning>

...these middle [notes] can compensate for the speed through their natural sweetness.

Nevertheless, Aristotle holds the contrary view in the 7th Chapter of the 5th book of *The Generation of Animals* in these terms: "Lowness of voice seems to be of a more noble nature, and in melodies the low is better than the high-pitched" <note>original Greek: "ἡ δοκεῖ γενναιοτέρας εἶναι φύσεως ἡ βαρυφωνία, καὶ ἐν τοῖς μέλεσι τὸ βαρὺ τῶν συντόνων βέλτιον"</note>. This is to say that the low voice seems to be the most noble, that the low sound is better than the high sounds of ensembles; and that the low voices of songs are more excellent than the high ones, insofar as the thing that surpasses others is more perfect, and that the lowness <note>original: "gravité"</note> of the voice consists in an excess of magnitude, "for perfection consists in superiority; and lowness is a kind of superiority" <note>original Greek: "τὸ γὰρ τέλειον ἐν ὑπεροχῇ. ἡ δὲ βαρύτης ὑπεροχή τις"</note>; for that which is great is preferable to that which is small, just as a great good is preferable to a lesser one.

This can be confirmed by considering the greater strength of he who has the thicker voice, and consequently larger and ampler body parts, which are in some way represented by the voice, which depends upon them, and which is like the mirror of the soul and the body. From this it follows that low voices have more majesty, weight, and strength to impress and produce powerful effects upon listeners, being similar to the sound of thunder and the cannon, which shakes and astonishes walls and men more strongly than do lesser noises.

And if one compares an excellent <term>Bass</term> <note>The lowest range of the human voice</note>, like that of Sieur Moulinié <note>Étienne Moulinié, a prominent French composer and singer active in the court of Gaston d'Orléans</note>, with an excellent <term>Treble</term> <note>original: "Dessus"; the highest range of the human voice</note>, like that of Sieur Bertaut <note>A singer in the King's service, likely Pierre Bertaut</note>, both singers of the King's Music, one will find those who take more pleasure in hearing the Bass than the Treble: though one must not follow the judgment or sentiment of men in this matter, since it is as inconstant as their humor, and the Bass pleases more at one time, and the Treble at another to the same listener, according to how they are differently disposed. This is why, leaving aside the differing judgment of men, which arises from different dispositions of the body or mind, one must consider the lowness <note>original: "gravité"</note> or highness <note>original: "aigu"</note> of the Sound in itself, in order to find which is the most pleasing, or the most excellent: for as to the strength of the Sound, the low is the strongest, when it is pushed with a proportionate force, and consequently it makes a stronger impression on the bodies encountered within the extent of its action.

But because that which has more strength is not always the most pleasing; although it may be the most excellent in its kind—since the sound of thunder, though great, strong, and powerful, and consequently excellent, is not pleasing, and it wounds the hearing and causes deafness—one must here distinguish the quality of *excellent* from that of *pleasing* in Sound, and see what makes it pleasing, for the more it has of the quality that makes it pleasant, the more pleasing it will be.

Now, that which makes it pleasing must be taken not only from what it has in itself, but from what it has relative to the ear, or to the imagination, which receives the pleasure of Sounds; and because we experience that the low Sound does not please some as much as others, we must believe that men have dispositions within them that contribute more to some pleasures than others, which are similar to the dispositions of smell and taste, which cause that which is pleasing to one to be displeasing to another: for some love the flavor of the orange and the lemon, and others hate them, or do not love them as much; and such a person takes pleasure in smelling the carnation, who hates the scent of the lily and the rose.

This is why one must consider the disposition and the imagination of the listener, although we have neglected it in judging the excellence of Sound,

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the aesthetic preferences of sound, specifically addressing the debate between high-pitched (acute) and low-pitched (grave) tones. It follows the previous discussion of Aristotle's preference for low voices by presenting the counter-argument: the widespread human preference for the "Dessus" or treble part.</meta>

### On the Nature and Properties of Sound.

It being not a question here of the best Sound considered simply and absolutely, but of Sound compared to the different types of listeners; although in this matter one may follow the sentiment and opinion of the great multitude, particularly those who have a good ear. Many hold that the Sound which is in the middle between the low <term>grave: the low-pitched register</term> and the high <term>aigu: the high-pitched register</term> is the most agreeable of all, both because it is less forced and is more natural and vigorous, and because it signifies a good temperament, and gives a beautiful air and a fine tone to speech.

Nevertheless, one encounters a greater number of men who take more pleasure in high Sounds than in middle ones; and we experience that the Treble parts <term>Dessus: the highest part in a musical composition</term> of concerts are much more agreeable than the other parts, and that the Treble alone ravishes the listener when it is well sung; so much so that it seems Composition was invented to make the Treble seem excellent, and to make one taste its goodness through comparison with the other parts, which give it brilliance, just as black and other dark colors do when they are placed opposite white. It must therefore be concluded that the high Sound is the most agreeable, provided it does not surpass the capacity of the ear, as we experience in the performances <note>original: "recits"</note> of young children, whom we love to hear more than any other concert, because the high voice represents to us the innocence, the delicacy, and the youth of children, who are more full of life, or closer to the source of life, and who sing more delicately and more sweetly than those who sing the other parts, or because the high Sound flatters the ear and better focuses the spirit.

For the high voice, being created by vibrations of air <note>original: "battemens d'air"</note> that are more continuous and less interrupted than those of other voices, approaches more closely the works of nature—which are continuous—and distances itself further from silence and nothingness than do low voices. Now, all creatures flee from the void and from nothingness, and each loves "being," in which high Sound participates more than the low; for it encompasses the low, which it surpasses as much in degrees of being as it does in its quality of highness, which is like form and light in regard to the low, which is similar to matter and darkness. From this it follows that when the Treble joins the other parts, it brings to them a great light whose rays penetrate even into the hearts of the listeners; indeed, when it sings all alone, it appears like a flash of light that obscures the preceding voices and penetrates to the deepest part of one's thought; so that if one hears other voices after the Treble, and if it falls silent for a short time, it seems as though one has left the light of the Sun to return into darkness.

->COROLLARY.<-

Since one is forced to admit that there are almost no demonstrations in Physics, or the science of natural things, I do not doubt that one could hold that low Sounds are the most excellent, whether by reason of the larger bodies that produce them, or the rest and unity which they more closely approach, or for other reasons that one might imagine; this is why everyone is free to believe what they wish. On this matter, one may see other similar difficulties that I propose in the penultimate Proposition of the *Book of Songs*, and at the beginning of the *Book of Com-*

<meta>This page transitions from discussing the aesthetic qualities of high and low sounds to a physical investigation of sound's underlying cause: movement. It continues the "First Book" (Liure Premier) of the treatise, likely Mersenne’s work on acoustics.</meta>

Book One

position. Since it appears highly likely that Sound is nothing other than the movement of air, or of other bodies, we must now speak of this movement in order to understand the nature of Sound more perfectly.

## PROPOSITION XXXII.

*To determine whether there is movement in nature, and what is necessary to establish it.*

I am not speaking here of movement in general, as one does in Physics, but only of *local motion* <term>mouuement local: the physical displacement of an object from one place to another</term>, which alone produces Sounds. Now, although it is very evident that there are many different movements in nature, many difficulties are nevertheless proposed against its existence. These so embarrass the mind that one is almost forced to offer only experience as their solution. For example: if there is movement and it is continuous, as one imagines, it follows that a tortoise travels as fast as an Eagle, since at each moment of time the Eagle travels no further than what corresponds to that indivisible <note>An "indivisible" refers to the smallest possible unit of time or space.</note>; and consequently, the Eagle will never be able to reach the tortoise, which will be one step ahead. For while the eagle travels half the step, the tortoise will advance a little, and a little more still while the eagle travels half of that half—that is, a quarter of the step—and so on through the other parts to infinity. 

That is to say, the human mind is not capable of understanding how it is possible for one continuous movement to be slower than another. This has constrained the Spanish Philosopher Arriaga <note>Rodrigo de Arriaga (1592–1667) was a Jesuit philosopher known for his "Cursus Philosophicus."</note> in his sixteenth physical dispute, and several others, to say that the slowness of movement is nothing other than an interruption by several rests, even though the senses cannot perceive them. They argue these rests are longer, or greater in multitude, as the movement is slower. For example, if the movement of the tortoise is a hundred thousand times slower than that of the eagle, the number of rests between the parts of the eagle's movement will be a hundred thousand times fewer than those in the movement of the tortoise. 

He also supposes this in the natural movement of stones and other heavy bodies falling toward the center of the earth. And although this imagination is not free from great difficulties—such as that of the radius of two concentric circles, which moves along the larger circumference in such a way that it seems necessary for the said rests to be as large upon it as upon the smaller one <note>This is a reference to the "Wheel of Aristotle" paradox, where two circles of different sizes seem to cover the same distance.</note>—nevertheless, some persist in this thought. They prefer to posit physical indivisibles <term>indiuisibles Phyfiques: atomic units of space or matter that cannot be further divided</term>, some much larger than others, which can change place entirely or only in part in a single moment, rather than embracing the continuity of movement, or the infinity of parts or points that make up the length of a space. 

However, I much prefer to follow the idea of an infinity of imaginary points, or parts, which make up the continuum both in lines and in movement. This allows me to respond that the eagle travels much more distance in the same amount of time than the tortoise, just as occurs with the part of a radius further away from its center.

Be that as it may, it is not necessary to know the ultimate truth of this matter to determine what pertains to the speed or slowness of movement, since it suffices to know that speed causes the moving object to pass more...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the nature of movement. Having previously touched upon the paradoxes of motion (like Zeno's paradox), the author now clarifies how speed relates to physical density and distinguishes between physical local motion and the instantaneous movement attributed to spiritual beings.</meta>

<header>On the nature and properties of Sound. 75</header>

faster in the same space, or that it travels more distance in the same time than that whose movement is slower: just as it happens that a body is more rarefied when it fills a larger space, and denser when it fills a smaller one. This must be noted carefully, because speed resembles density in some manner, just as slowness resembles rarefaction, or vice-versa.

As for the things necessary to establish movement, it is very difficult to regulate them. For if one takes different locations in regards to some fixed point of the world—for example, in regard to the **North Pole**—it is not necessary for a body to move to change its location, provided that the Pole itself moves. Thus, if the location of the **Sun** were determined by its distance from certain points on the earth, it would change its location even if it were stationary and the earth turned around it, as the **disciples of Copernicus** imagine; consequently, one could say the Sun would have a movement. But if one establishes the movement of each body by the space it leaves and which it previously filled, and if one imagines that this space is entirely immobile, it will be easy to understand the change of this location—provided one adds that it does not happen in a single moment, but over a span of time. Several **Theologians** teach that spirits separated from matter—for example, **Angels** and rational souls—can change location and leave France to find themselves in China in an instant. This means they do not employ any time to pass through the Provinces between France and China, because their changes of location can be made by interrupted instants. This is easy to understand, insofar as the understanding does the same thing when it has a thought of the earth and immediately afterward a thought of the stars, without thinking of what lies between the two. However, I am not speaking now of this type of movement, which does not properly belong to **Physics**, and which can neither produce **Sounds** nor be perceived by the senses.

This being settled, I take local motion <term>mouuement local: the physical displacement of an object through space</term> here to be the action by which a body leaves the space it occupied and passes successively to another space distant from the previous one. This remains true whether the extent and size of the world be finite or infinite, and whether there be a "high" or "low," or "right" or "left," or not. It must nevertheless be added that for local motion, it is sufficient that the parts of the moving body do not always touch the same parts of the surrounding space, even if the body considered as a whole does not change the space containing it. This ensures that spheres turning on a fixed axis between two pivots are not exempt from the movement of which we speak. Now, we have no need of external bodies to experience and understand local motion; for even if there were only one man in the world and all the rest were annihilated, he would feel quite well the movement his hand made from his feet to his head, and that which he made with other parts of his body. This would similarly happen to an indivisible spirit—which one might imagine reduced to a point—which would perceive its own movement even if there were no other created thing in nature. Here, one must suppose that movement can occur in those spaces that some call **imaginary spaces** <note>In 17th-century philosophy, "imaginary space" refers to the infinite, void-like extension that was thought to exist beyond the boundaries of the created universe, or simply the concept of space itself independent of matter.</note>, and which they believe to exist from all eternity, although they are perhaps nothing other than

He argues that motion is an inherent physical reality that can be perceived even in a vacuum or through rotation without change of overall position.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from metaphysical considerations of movement—such as those of angels—to the physical laws governing the cosmos. The author, likely Marin Mersenne, is pivoting from abstract theological movement to the observable mechanics of the universe.</meta>

...the Divine power, the idea of which is much more imperfectly in our minds than the image of the Sun in the light received upon the most uneven surfaces imaginable. But I leave these considerations to consider the movement of all bodies in general, before treating them in particular.

### PROPOSITION XXXIII.

->*Considering the movements of all bodies in general, and the space in which they occur.*<-

We cannot know if the spaces beyond the stars are finite or infinite, nor if they are empty or filled with some dark or luminous bodies; for it may be that the space containing the visible part of the world from the earth to the stars is but a point compared to the rest of the world beyond, and that this vast part contains other stars, each of which is a hundred thousand times larger than the firmament <note>In the cosmology of the time, the "firmament" usually referred to the sphere of the fixed stars.</note>. For the power of God is infinitely greater than our imagination, and no creature <note>original: "creature"; here referring to any created thing, including the universe itself.</note> can be more reasonably compared to Him than one that would be infinite. But since it is not possible for us to know if He made such a creature, nor even if it is feasible, and since we have no more knowledge of the space and bodies imaginable beyond the firmament than a man raised in a forest—who never left it and never heard of it—would have of the ebb and flow of the sea, it is enough to consider what concerns us and the movements we perceive.

Now, there are particularly two kinds of movement: some seem straight to us, and others circular. For example, it seems that bodies called heavy descend straight toward the center of the earth, and they similarly go straight when thrown upward, downward, or to another side. As for the others, they seem circular, as observed in the movement of the Sun and the Moon. But because **Astronomy** and **Physics** have not yet provided a demonstration to show whether it is the earth that turns or the Sun, and since everything purely sensible to us can be explained by one or the other of these movements, we will touch upon this difficulty only as much as necessary to examine several rare experiments mentioned in the book that follows. It must only be noted that there is neither "up" nor "down" in this world, strictly and absolutely speaking, since what is "up" in regard to one is "down" in regard to another. For example, we imagine that our **Antipodes** <note>The term for people living on the opposite side of the globe, whose feet are "opposite" to ours.</note> are down beneath our feet, and they think the same of us; and one might say that the center of a circle or a sphere is its highest place, and the circumference is the lowest. Whatever the case, it is enough that we understand each other when we speak, so that words do not convey anything other than what is in the idea and the mind, and that we avoid all difficulties arising only from different understandings of terms. But before beginning the second book, I wish to finish this one with a Proposition that will serve as a transition to the third book for those who do not take pleasure in the difficulties of **Physics** and who want only what serves precisely for **Music**, so that they may skip the second book without any prejudice, or

# On the nature and properties of Sound.
G iii

...inconvenience; so that one can join this last Proposition to the first of the third book.

### PROPOSITION XXXIV.

*To demonstrate whether a string stretched by a peg, or by a weight, is equally stretched in all its parts, and whether the force that tightens it communicates its impression sooner and more strongly to the parts near it than to those further away.*

**This** Proposition is more difficult to determine than many imagine, for the parts of the stretched string <term>string; original: "chorde", which refers both to musical strings and functional ropes</term> that are closer to the weight or the tuning peg seem more stretched than those further away: since the force that tightens the string passes through the parts it is closest to before reaching those further away, and has all the more vigor as it is closer to its origin.

And we experience that strings usually break at the parts near the weight that tightens them; which happens, it seems, because they are more stretched in those places than toward the middle, where they never break. To which one adds that strings yield and sag more easily in the middle than in any other place, as seen on instruments, and on the ropes used to pull boats along rivers: these reasons and all the others that can be cited here make the Proposition difficult. We also experience that strings are easier to break when they are long than when they are short; and consequently that they endure a greater tension. Indeed, the length of bodies causes them to act more powerfully, or to yield more easily, for if one pushes a pike <note>A long spear-like weapon.</note> against another body, one will overturn it more easily than if one pushed a shorter staff of equal thickness, even if one pushed it with equal force; from which some conclude that force increases in proportion to its distance from its source, as we notice in the strength of seeds which are weak at their beginning, and increase their vigor as they move away from their matrices <note>original: "matrices"; referring here to the seed-pod or womb-like source of growth.</note>, in which they were enclosed and as if mortified <note>original: "mortifiées"; used here to mean dormant or lacking active life before sprouting.</note>.

And we see in mechanics that force is all the greater as it moves further from its center: for if one encounters the arms of a wheel, or of a windmill, or the movement of the arm toward the hand, one experiences that the force is much greater than at the axle, or at the center of the said wheels, or toward the shoulder, where the movement of the arm begins. To which one can relate the movement of stones and other missiles, or bodies that are thrown, which have more force and effect when they are far away and have already traveled a long way, than when they are near the arm, the bow, or the arquebus <note>An early type of portable firearm.</note> by which they are pushed: and consequently one can say that the string is more tightened at the parts distant from the force than at those closer to it, since forces increase in proportion as they move away from their beginnings, as we observe in rivers, which are but streams at their sources, and even in rumors that are spread; which gave rise to the Proverb <note>Likely referring to the Latin proverb "Vires acquirit eundo" (It gains strength as it goes), famously applied to Rumor by Virgil.</note>
