<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition XXXIV regarding whether tension is uniform across the length of a string, specifically addressing objections related to the distance from the source of the tension (the peg or weight).</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>Book One</header>

...of fame and of discourse, *it gathers strength as it goes* <note>original: "Vires acquirit eundo"; a quotation from Virgil’s Aeneid describing how rumors grow as they spread.</note>: and the sound of a cannon is greater a thousand paces from the cannon than at the place where it begins. From this comes the idea that the strings of instruments break more often near the bridge <term>bridge; original: "cheualet", the piece of wood that supports strings on a musical instrument</term> than near the nut <term>nut; original: "fillet", the ridge at the end of the fingerboard</term>, because the tuning peg is further from the bridge, near which the force is found to be greater than in any other part of the string—if this force increases like the others in proportion to its distance from its origin.

One can also say that if the string were equally stretched in all its parts, the same force would equally stretch a string a thousand leagues long and a string one foot long, which seems incredible. Furthermore, if the longer one were equally stretched with an equal weight, then when moved from its straight line, it would make as many back-and-forth vibrations as the shorter one which is equally displaced. But this does not happen, for the string **A B** being pulled to **G** takes twice as long to return to **F** as the string **A F**, which, being pulled as far as **E**, or as far as the string **A B**, returns twice as fast, and consequently twice as often to **H**, as the string **A B** returns to **F**.

Nevertheless, one can say that the strings of instruments are equally stretched in all their parts, because they produce a unison <note>When two notes sound at the exact same pitch.</note> when one places the bridge in the middle; and because the peg or the weight can send all its force through all parts of the string at the same time, just as the weight at the top of a pike <note>A long wooden shaft or spear.</note> presses as much at the same time on the hand holding the pike by the bottom end as it does on the top end; and as does the movement one imparts to a staff by pushing or pulling it, which is imparted equally to all parts of the staff at the same time. This is why the same weight stretches a string of a thousand leagues as easily as one of a single foot, which I will explain when responding to the fifth objection.

And if one objects that the unison demonstrates only that each half of the string is equally stretched—on the grounds that the two ends are equally tensioned because the top point where the string is attached makes the same impression on the string as the weight that stretches it, such that the impression of both weakens as it approaches the middle—one can respond that if one takes a length near the middle that is equal to another length taken near one of the ends, these two lengths will be in unison. This is true even though the two bridges determining the length in the middle provide no new tension to the string, and only support it in the same position they find it. Consequently, the string is equally stretched in all its parts. 

<image-desc>A physics diagram illustrating string tension. A horizontal line is labeled with points A, L, H, F, and B. From point F, a vertical dashed line drops down to point G where a weight is attached. Similar vertical dashed lines drop from points L and H to points K and I respectively, also showing weights. Diagonal lines connect point A to the displaced points K, I, and G, demonstrating how the same weight displaces the chord to different degrees depending on the length of the segment.</image-desc>

I demonstrate this by this figure, which represents the string **A B** equally stretched in all its parts: for if one suspends the weight **E** in the middle of the string **A B** at point **F**, it will bring it down to point **G**, as I assume. And if one divides the string **A B** into **A F** and **F B**, the same weight **E** will bring the string **A F** to point **I**, and the string **A H** to **K**, and so on to infinity; now the same weight attached to the string **A B** at point **F** does the same thing, that is to say, it lowers...

<meta>This page continues the technical rebuttal to objections regarding the uniformity of tension in a musical string. It uses geometric points and physical analogies to argue that a string, when pulled by a weight or turned by a peg, experiences equal tension throughout its entire length, rather than a force that increases with distance from the source.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>On the nature and properties of Sound.</header>

the points **L** & **H** as far as **K I**, just as if it were attached to the points **L** & **H**, as seen in string **A G**—that is to say, **A I F**, which passes through **K I**. Therefore, the weight attached to a single part of the string stretches it as much in every part as if it were attached successively and separately to each part. The reason for this is that **A B** resists just as much and stretches twice as much when it is pulled from point **F** to **G** as string **A F** does when it is pulled from point **H** to **I**, and as string **A H**, which is pulled from point **L** to **K**. The latter stretches half as much as string **A F**, and four times less than string **A B**, even though it resists equally.

For the elongations <term>elongations; original: "alongemens", the physical stretching or lengthening of the string under tension</term> of strings have the same ratio as their lengths; and it is just as difficult to stretch a four-foot string (a quadruple length) as it is to stretch a one-foot string (a sub-quadruple length). But we shall discuss in another place how much these elongations diminish the thickness of the strings; for it is enough to have shown here that the strings of musical instruments are equally stretched in all their parts.

As for the objections brought against the equality of this tension, one can answer the first by saying that the force which tightens the string is communicated to every part at the same time. Otherwise, when the force exceeds the resistance of the string, it would break at the extremity to which it is applied before it had communicated its force to the middle or to the other end. This is contrary to experience, which shows that the string is stretched in all its parts before it breaks, regardless of how great the force applied to it may be; for the string is just as taut at one end as at the other, and produces an equal Sound in all its parts regarding the low and the high <note>In other words, the pitch (the "gravity and acuteness") of the string's vibration is consistent along its length, which would not be the case if tension varied.</note>.

We can therefore compare the force of the weight, or of the tuning peg <term>tuning peg; original: "cheuille"</term> that tightens the string, to the movement imparted to the stick we mentioned earlier, the middle of which is moved as soon as the end to which the force is applied; and to a ray of the Sun, which illuminates the diameter of its sphere all at once.

The second objection is taken from the breaking of strings, which occurs at the place where the weight or the force is attached. But this break can happen in that location because of the stretching of all parts of the string, which meets near the weight, or due to the effort given to the string when knotting it, or when uncoiling it, or due to other circumstances observed in different experiments. This does not occur with the pegs that tighten the strings without the need to untwist or knot them. From this it follows that they break more often near the bridge <term>bridge; original: "cheualet"</term> than near the pegs, where they are better preserved. Now, one can consider here several types of tension: first, a string can be stretched with a peg, a winch, a screw, or another instrument, as happens on the Lute and other necked instruments. Secondly, it can be stretched and pulled by a weight attached to one of the ends, as would happen if one stretched the strings of a Lute or a Harp with weights to tune them; this can be done by a "sourd" <note>The term "sourd" literally means "deaf person," but here likely refers to an experiment performed "silently" or using a specific acoustic device like a mute or a monochord setup described in Mersenne's other works.</note>, as I demonstrate in the third book on stringed instruments.

In the third place, the string can be tightened at the same time by two pegs placed at the two ends of the string, by turning them both equally, or by two weights attached to the two ends, which pull it equally.

<summary>This page argues that tension is uniform throughout a musical string, regardless of its length, using the principle that elongation is proportional to length. It refutes the idea that strings are more tense near the tuning source, attributing breaks near the bridge or pegs to mechanical wear and knots rather than unequal force.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical properties of musical strings, specifically how tension is distributed across their length whether applied by a tuning peg or a hanging weight.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>Book One</header>
<sig>e</sig>

on one side and the other. In the fourth place, being stretched by two pegs, or attached on one side to the bridge <term>bridge; original: "cheualet", the piece that supports the strings and transmits their vibration to the body of the instrument</term>, and on the other to the peg, it can receive a new tension by a weight attached in the middle, or in some other place of the string stretched horizontally. This can similarly happen when it is braced by two weights attached to the two sides.

Finally, one can brace it at whatever point or part one wishes by means of a movable bridge, to which one can give all sorts of heights, as is experienced on the Monochord <note>The monochord was an ancient scientific and musical instrument consisting of a single string stretched over a soundboard with a movable bridge, used to measure mathematical intervals.</note>; now the bridge has the same effect in raising the string as the weight has in lowering it.

This being established, one must see the effect of these different tensions in order to respond to the second objection. First, consider the effect of the first two ways of tension, which are greatly different; for when the string is braced with a peg, it seems that it has no more difficulty and suffers no more <note>The author uses "suffers" to describe the physical stress or strain placed on the material of the string.</note> on the second day than the first, because the peg gives it no new force and only holds it in the same state. But when it is braced by a weight, it suffers constantly, insofar as the weight acts as strongly on the second and the hundredth day as on the first: this is why the string often breaks near the weight, whereas it breaks near the bridge when it is stretched by a peg.

Moreover, when the string is stretched with a weight, if one puts another weight in the middle, or at some other part of the string to pull it down—like weight **E** which is attached to three different points of the preceding string **A B**—weight **C** rises and gives freedom to weight **E** to lower the string more and more, until it breaks, if weight **E** is strong enough to break it. And if it is not strong enough, and yet is greater than weight **C**, it overcomes it and takes the string off the plane, or the support on which it was stretched. But when it is braced with a peg, it only obeys in the middle until it can no longer endure being elongated, insofar as the peg always holds firm without yielding to the weight placed in the middle, or at some other point of the string, or at the bridge which has the same effect in raising the said string as the weight has in lowering it.

Now all the parts of the string, which is raised to a certain tension and remains in this state, contribute equally in enduring the tension; so that they are reduced to an equilibrium of resistance and endurance, in which they remain until one of the parts disunites and separates from the others, causing the rupture of the string.

Nevertheless, it seems that the weight gives a tension just as equal to the string as the peg does, since its power extends to both ends of the string at the same time. There is no other distinction, except that it breaks near the weight, or far from the peg as is believed—though there is nothing fixed in this matter, for it often breaks near the peg and far from the weight. This happens whenever it is weaker toward the peg, or far from the weight, than in any other place: so that I estimate there is no other reason for the breaking of strings in certain places rather than others, except that they are weaker. It seems impossible to find a string that is without inequality in all its parts, of which some are weaker than others, whether it be made of brass <term>brass; original: "airain", a term often used historically for copper alloys including bronze</term>, iron, or other metal, or of silk, hemp, gut <term>gut; original: "boyau", animal intestines processed into strong, flexible strings</term>, etc., as I have shown...

<summary>The author compares how strings respond to tension from tuning pegs versus hanging weights, concluding that strings break not because tension is uneven, but because of natural physical inconsistencies in the material itself.</summary>
<keywords>tension, string rupture, musical acoustics, monochord, material science, bridge, tuning peg, gut strings</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 80 regarding the physical properties and stresses placed on musical strings, transitioning from the causes of string breakage to the mechanics of tension and leverage.</meta>

# ->On the nature & properties of Sound.<-

...to the discussion of the material of harmonic strings <term>harmonic strings; original: "chordes harmoniques," referring to strings designed to produce musical intervals and tones</term>.

When a string is equally stretched from both sides, many believe it ought to break in the middle, because the force of the two pegs or the two weights reaches the middle of the string—where they meet—sooner than anywhere else. Others say that if it is equally stretched from both ends, it must break into an infinite number of parts, as there is no reason why it should break in one place rather than another. But experience shows that it almost always breaks at one of its ends, as I stated in the eighth question of the *Preludes to Harmony* <note>original: "Preludes de l'Harmonie," likely referring to Mersenne's *Questions harmoniques* or an earlier section of his work</note>, unless it happens to be weaker in the middle than in other places.

Nevertheless, the locations where strings break under varying tensions are so unpredictable that it is almost impossible to draw certain and necessary conclusions from them. For instance, we experience that they often break in the middle when used to tow boats on the water, even though they are very slack in that spot—where it seems they are less tense, if one measures the degree of tension by the stiffness of the rope. One might say this break occurs in the middle because the weight of the entire rope gathers there, or because the middle is soaked more often and for longer in the water, which causes it to rot and consequently weakens it more than the other parts. However, I shall speak further of this breakage when responding to the third objection.

As for the tension a string receives by suspending a weight in the middle, or at some other part, or by using a bridge <term>bridge; original: "cheualet", the support that lifts the string</term>, I have already spoken of this in the explanation of the preceding figure. That explanation suffices to understand how much more a string is tightened by a weight or a bridge placed in the middle than by any other means. But several things can be considered in this manner of tension: for example, knowing what weight must be suspended in the middle, or what the height of the bridge must be, to stretch it as much as a given weight suspended at one of the ends of the string (whether vertical or horizontal). This can be determined by the equal stretching of the string, and more easily by the Sound produced on either side of the string when it is raised in the middle by a bridge, or lowered by a weight, and by comparing it to the Sound of the same string (or another equal one) stretched by one or both ends, whether with weights or pegs. However, this tension requires a specific discourse of its own.

Regarding the third objection, I reply that the sagging <note>original: "l’abaiſſement," referring to the physical dipping or displacement of the string when pressed or weighted</note> which occurs more easily in the middle of the string is because the string is further from the bridges at that point than anywhere else. The bridges represent the supports of two levers, and all parts of the string are more or less stiff in proportion to their distance from the said bridges, as seen in the preceding string to which weight E is attached <note>This refers to a diagram on a previous page where points A, B, F, G, etc., illustrate the geometry of the string</note>. For the same ratio exists from the string—or the lever A F or B F—to the sagging F G, as from the lever A H and A L to the sagging H I and L K.

This is why it is easier to move the string in the middle than in any other place. Just as one moves a lever that is double in length twice as easily as one that is half the length <term>half the length; original: "souzdouble", a mathematical term for the sub-double or 1:2 ratio</term>, so too one lowers a string of double length (with equal tension) twice as easily as one of half the length.

<summary>This page explores the physics of string tension and breakage, arguing that strings typically break at their ends rather than the middle, and explains how a string acts as a system of levers when displaced.</summary>
<keywords>string tension, physics of sound, bridges, levers, Mersenne, acoustics, mechanics, breaking point</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the mechanical properties of strings, specifically addressing how the length of a string affects its tension and its tendency to break, while using analogies from levers and ballistics.</meta>

# Book One

Now, these greater sags <note>original: "abaiffemens" (abaissements), referring to the downward deflection or "dip" of a string due to its own weight</note> coexist perfectly well with the equal tension of the parts of the string, just as the easier movement of a longer lever <term>lever; original: "leuier"</term> coexists with the force being equal throughout all its parts—even though they obey with more difficulty and offer more resistance when they are closer to the weight or the fulcrum <term>fulcrum; original: "appuy"</term>. I shall provide the reason for this in the discourse on the strength of the tuning pegs of the Lute <term>Lute; original: "Luth," a popular stringed instrument with a neck and a rounded body</term> and other necked instruments, for that force is related to the lever.

One may also consider the weight of the entire string, which is more apparent in the middle than elsewhere, because it forms a smaller or larger arc in the middle depending on how heavy or tense it is. The center of this arc meets the line that cuts the string perpendicularly through the said middle. But I will explain this arc and everything pertaining to it in another discourse; for now, I wish to respond to the fourth objection, which asks whether a longer string breaks more easily than a shorter one.

The fourth objection contains a proof contrary to the others, for its purpose is to show that a longer string is more tense under equal force, whereas the other objections were made to prove that a string is more tense when it is short. The reasoning rests on whether the string breaks more easily when it is longer—as occurs in the examples cited there—which seem to show that the larger a body is, the more effect it has. It suggests that the power <term>power; original: "vertu"</term> and the force that pulls or pushes is greater the further it moves from its starting point, up to a certain limit that bounds the sphere of activity <note>original: "ſphere d'actiuité," a philosophical term for the range within which a force can act</note>, or the proportion between the moving force and the body being moved.

This can be confirmed by longer cannons <term>cannons; original: "Canons"</term>, which have a greater range and accuracy; and by the blowguns <term>blowguns; original: "Sarbacanes"</term> used by children to shoot pins and small arrows much further than they do with shorter ones, even though they seemingly blow their wind or breath with equal force into both the long and short tubes.

Indeed, the larger and more massive the bodies that act or are acted upon, the more force they have to act and resist, as is experienced with large vessels both at sea and on rivers. They strike whatever they encounter much harder than small boats do, even though both move at the same speed. This is because the force of the wind is better imprinted <note>original: "s'imprime," referring to the transmission of "impetus" or force into an object</note> upon large mobile bodies than upon small ones, since all impressions and qualities communicated to bodies are received according to the capacity of said bodies—each receiving only as much as it needs and is capable of. This is why one cannot throw a straw as far as a stone, even if one exerts the same effort for both. This is because straw and other such things are not capable of receiving as great an impression as heavy objects; or perhaps because there is a greater proportion of surface area to weight in straw than in a stone or other heavier body. From this, it follows that the air resists the surface of the straw much more than it is overcome by its weight, whereas the air is overcome much more by the weight of other bodies than it resists their surfaces. This similarly concludes the argument regarding the natural descent of heavy bodies toward their center <note>The "center" refers to the center of the Earth, the destination of all heavy things in the physics of this period</note.

<summary>This page explores the physics of tension and length, arguing that longer strings or objects (like cannons or levers) can exert or receive more force due to their mass and "capacity" for movement. Mersenne uses analogies of ships, blowguns, and falling stones to explain how surface area and weight influence resistance and momentum.</summary>
<keywords>tension, lever, lute, ballistics, air resistance, mass and weight, mechanics of strings, Marin Mersenne</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into why physical objects—specifically musical strings and long weapons like pikes—behave differently based on their length and mass. Mersenne is grappling with the mechanics of resistance and why longer objects sometimes seem more fragile or more powerful than shorter ones.</meta>

## On the nature & properties of Sound:
### 83

To which one may add that one pushes much more air when throwing a light body than when it is heavy, especially since a light body contains more air within its pores <note>In 17th-century physics, it was believed that light objects were filled with tiny holes or "pores" containing air, which accounted for their low density.</note>; now, air does not "desire" to be moved within air, nor to be thrown from one place in the air to another. We experience something similar in water, for those who swim underwater <note>original: "entre deux eaux," meaning submerged between the surface and the bottom</note> can only move a portion of water from one place to another with great difficulty. Now, we are always "swimming" and living within the air, and air is perhaps nothing other than a rarefied water. But this reason for resistance seems to contradict the objection, since a longer string <term>string; original: "chorde"</term> ought to offer more resistance than a shorter one, and consequently, it should be harder to break than the shorter one—if the resistance of the length increases in the same proportion as the other aforementioned effects which move away from the force or the source of motion. However, this is contrary to experience.

Certainly, it is very difficult to resolve this difficulty, to which I respond with nothing else but this: I do not believe a string breaks more easily simply because it is longer, unless it is because of the greater vibration <term>vibration; original: "branle," referring to the swinging or oscillating motion</term> and the greater shock that it suffers; or because one encounters more weak and unequal parts in a long string than in a short one, through which it happens to break. And the majority of examples, such as that of the longer pike <note>A "pike" was a very long thrusting spear used by infantry, often 10 to 20 feet long.</note>, etc., can be explained by the greater vibration made by larger bodies. One could also say that the longer string receives a greater impression because of its greater quantity—were it not for the fact that it would seem to follow that it would break two, three, or four times more easily when it is two, three, or four times longer, which does not happen.

Now, it is difficult to relate this greater vibration to any principle of Mechanics, unless it be to the screw <term>screw; original: "vis"</term>, for the vibration, not lengthening the pike at all, cannot be related to the lever <term>lever; original: "leuier"</term>. But if we add a new principle—namely, a greater motion—we find through experience that the vibration adds a new motion to that which the staff or the pike received from the arm; now, two or more motions existing together produce a greater effect than when they act alone. But if the pike, or another body, has a greater effect even when receiving no vibration than a half-pike or some other smaller body, one must admit that quantity increases force, even though the force moving said bodies is equal. This may happen because there is less surface area in large bodies than in small ones in proportion to their quantity and their weight; or rather, one must respond that a pike, a boat <term>boat; original: "basteau"</term>, or other bodies being pushed never have a greater effect unless they are pushed harder, and that we are mistaken when we believe they are pushed with an equal force which increases in proportion to the size of the bodies. Just as a ship <term>ship; original: "nauire"</term> cannot be moved as fast as a small bark <term>bark; original: "barque," a small boat</term> by an equal wind, the entire pike and larger bodies cannot be moved at an equal speed by an equal force, even though one cannot notice this inequality in the strength of the hand of the one who pushes or pulls all sorts of bodies.

The objection contains several other things which belong to the principle and the extent of forces and "seeds" <note>original: "semences," here referring to the latent origins or internal principles of physical properties</note>; to the force of rays; to the semi-diameters of wheels <term>wheels; original: "roües"</term> and other mechanical engines; to the manner of...

<summary>Mersenne explores why longer strings and pikes behave differently than shorter ones, debating whether "vibration" (branle) adds a secondary force to an object's motion and questioning the role of surface area and mass in resistance.</summary>
<keywords>vibration, mechanical force, physics of strings, resistance, pike, lever, ship analogy, air density</keywords>

# First Book
## 84

...moving bodies through the air, and the different speeds at which they move, and finally to the noises and winds, which are greater when they are further from their source—of which we will not speak here, because they require specialized treatises, and we have only fallen into this discourse by chance.

As for cannonballs and other bodies that travel further when the tubes and blowguns <term>blowguns; original: "sarbatanes," hollow tubes used to launch projectiles with breath</term>, or other similar objects are longer, we will perhaps speak of them in the book on the *Music of Cannons, Drums, and other noises used in war* <note>Mersenne often applied the mathematical principles of music and vibration to the study of acoustics and ballistics in military contexts.</note>.

The fifth objection contains two points, the first of which is true, and the second is false: for one should not doubt at all that the same weight, or the same force, stretches a string equally regardless of its length—even if one end were attached to the heavens <note>original: "firmament"</note> and the other to a nail in a floor, or on a <term>monochord</term> <note>A monochord is a scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used since antiquity to study the mathematical relationships of musical intervals.</note>. Consequently, this holds true even if the first string were 16,030,000 leagues long and the second were only one foot in length, since weight breaks a long string as easily as a short one, as we have remarked above and elsewhere. The second part of the objection draws a conclusion from equal tension to the equality of the "returns" <note>Referring to the frequency of vibration or oscillation.</note> of the string, the falsehood of which will be demonstrated in another place.

Now, one can conclude from this whole discourse that the strings of musical instruments are equally stretched in all their parts, and that any breakage that occurs near the bridge <note>The "chevalet" or bridge is the piece of wood that supports the strings and transmits their vibration to the instrument's body.</note>, or elsewhere, comes from the fact that the strings are weaker or more worn in the places where they break, or because of different accidents encountered in different instruments, the different material of the strings, and the different ways of tensioning them. I reserve the discussion of these matters for the treatise on instruments, in which it will be easier to examine all the different occurrences and properties of strings.

->ADVISORY<-

I will speak more particularly of everything concerning the force and motion of strings in the third book, after having considered in the second the motions of the greatest bodies in the universe—such as the earth and the stars—and several things touching upon the motion of heavy bodies toward their center, and the violent motions made by means of wheels and otherwise. To this, it will always be easy to add many other things, both through experience and through reason, given the great multitude of different motions observed in nature. Now, the book that follows contains an examination of what the most excellent philosopher Mr. <term>Galileo</term> <note>Galileo Galilei (1564–1642), the Italian astronomer and physicist whose "Dialogue Concerning the Two Chief World Systems" (1632) is referenced here.</note> proposed in his *Dialogues on the Systems of <term>Ptolemy</term> and <term>Copernicus</term>* <note>Ptolemy represented the geocentric model (earth-centered), while Copernicus represented the heliocentric model (sun-centered).</note>, and several other things that deserve to be considered by the best minds in the world, so that they might establish some principle on this subject which provides a multitude of advantageous consequences for <term>physics</term> <note>At this time, "Physics" or *Physique* referred broadly to natural philosophy, the study of the physical laws of the natural world.</note>. If any conclusion is found in this first book that is not sufficiently well-deduced, or any comparison that does not please, everyone is free to leave them or to adapt them as they like; I wish this to be understood for all the other books as well.
